# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum XV

## Funding proposal package for FP240

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

a) A funding proposal summary titled "Collaborative R&D/IO Programme for Promoting the Innovation of Climate Techentrepreneurship" submitted by Korea Development Bank (KDB);

b) Request letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

f) General recommendation of the funding proposal.

These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated authority(ies)/ focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy for Climate Change-related Financial Instruments, this addendum titled "Collaborative R&D/IO Programme for Promoting the Innovation of Climate Techentrepreneurship" submitted by Korea Development Bank (KDB) is being circulated and shall be distributed here only to shared Members and External Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

### Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accreditations are valid for 5 years from date of issue. Any changes to the accreditation status provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the project proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

### Under GCF Policy 3: Project and Programme Policies
project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G-4.

Please submit the completed proposal to:
fundingproposal@unfccc.org

*FP (Accredited Entity/Short Name)/Country/Region) [YYYY/MM/DD]*

The programme has four Executing Entities (EEs) as below.

(1) The Korea Development Bank (KDB), Accredited Entity, co-Executing Entity of Components 1 and 4

The programme will leverage the proven three-tier venture investment platforms (KDB NextRound, NextOne, and NextRise) and global VC and PE networks to provide a comprehensive suite of services for the four components. KDB Venture Desk and KDB Silicon Valley LLC (VC subsidiary). Above all, KDB has extensive experience in managing the complex multi-country project management amongst the four (4) different components across five (5) NOL countries with capacity of both Accredited Entity and Executing Entity.

(2) Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI): co-Executing Entity of Components 1 and 4

GGGI is a treaty-based intergovernmental organisation that has been carrying out a number of climate actions and green growth promotion activities in the NOL countries. GGGI has played an increasingly prominent in-country presence, GGGI has built trust with the five NOL governments through its extensive experience in the Programme Preparation Facility (PPF) phase of the programme. GGGI had closely worked with the five governments to develop a comprehensive strategy aligned with the five government national policy direction and specific country needs. GGGI will continue to play an important role in the programme's core governance structure for strengthening country ownership - including the NOL Country Coordinating Mechanism (CCM) – in consultation with NGOs and key local stakeholders in the five NOL countries.

Its signature initiative, entitled "Global Greenpreneurs Program", has supported high-potential local green entrepreneurs — ca. 800 youths in total from 25 countries across Asia Pacific region— to access business services for 57 teams from 20 countries. In addition, GGGI has extensive experience in providing technical assistance and capacity building for MSMEs in diverse sectors with various green investment interventions.

(3) NH Investment & Securities (NHIS): Executing Entity of Component 2

NH Investment & Securities (NHIS) is the leading comprehensive financial investment company with overwhelming competitiveness in all business areas. NHIS has been a pioneer in providing innovative products and services, and values and initiative is increasing. NHIS provides eco-friendly and green investment solutions by leveraging its capabilities to become "Green Impact Finance" by fully leveraging its capabilities.

NHIS has been striving to lay a solid foundation for practical implementation of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and has invested ca. USD 15 billion in green impact related investment banking & brokerage activities across green finance and CSR.

In addition, its Carbon Finance Team has accumulated experiences in providing comprehensive carbon offsetting services both at home and abroad, and has provided comprehensive carbon-offset consulting services, with a pursuit to lead the development of various innovative financial products such as green credit securitization and development of diverse financial products.

**Page 8**

**(4)** **NII Absolute Return Partners (NII ARP): Executing Entity of Component 1**

**A.21. Executive summary (max: 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)**

*Climate Technology Acceleration* at the Core of a Paradigm Shift towards Low Carbon and Climate Resilient Development in ASEAN Countries. This proposal is inspired by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC, 2018) that spotlights a need to accelerate climate technology leapfrogging as the solution while climate actions are more urgent than ever.

NII ARP will source and digest climate technology solutions through technology transfer in an accelerated manner. The programme will focus on five countries from Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, and the Philippines to Vietnam (in alphabetical order). Pivoting from these five countries, the programme will support the regional innovation ecosystem conducive to achieving a common vision of shared prosperity in the ASEAN community.

**Programme Canvas**

NOL Countries Shall Accelerate Climate Technology Leapfrogging as the Solution, while Climate Actions Are More Urgent than Ever. Many sources demonstrate that the five ASEAN economies are

*UNFCCC, 2018: Climate Technology Incubators and Accelerators. Bonn; United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.*

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

severely exposed to climate vulnerability and its adverse impacts and associated risks. Alarming consequences include worsening natural disasters and extreme weather events such as hurricanes, tornadoes, floods, heatwaves, droughts, wildfires, landslides, and tsunamis, the submersion of inhabited coastlines, decline in water quality and quantity of water supply, declining agricultural productivity, increased outbreak and spread of water-borne diseases, and reduced effectiveness of natural coastal defenses. The combined effect of these adverse impacts is to increase vulnerability and disproportionately increased climate risks to the most vulnerable, compounded the negative impacts of poverty, conflict, and inequality.

The fastest climate solutions therefore need to be deployed throughout the five NCD countries given the climate urgency. Surrounded by worsening multi-dimensional ravages of global warming, developed economies are increasingly investing in technology transfer and capacity building initiatives for the five countries to kick their carbon intensive pathways through partnering with enterprises armed with an innovative portfolio of technologies and business models. The Global Climate Technology Fund (GCTF) and R&D&BC platform on a quest for tailored business models that best fit the five countries’ mitigation and adaptation technology needs, set up an investment fund scale to encourage the market penetration of climate solutions, and provide technical assistance and capacity building for technology uptake.

## Local - Global Joint Venture (JV) Partnership as the Key to Technology Transfer Mechanism that Promotes Climate Technology Leapfrogging in NCD Countries. The programme conceives technology transfer via a joint venture (JV) as a result of collaborative R&D&BC acceleration* as a key profound concept, given the five countries’ limited capacity and resources to independently develop, test, validate, digitize, and deploy climate technology solutions through technology development and transfer in advancing mitigation and adaptation actions amid rapidly exacerbating climate crisis. JVs will eventually lead up to market penetration of climate solutions, and provide technical assistance and capacity building for technology uptake.

and most importantly, strengthen local human capital capacity that is a crucial factor in internalizing and accelerating the adoption of climate solutions. The programme will be implemented through a network of diverse cross-border innovation ecosystem stakeholders from academic institutions and start-up support intermediaries to large corporations under the common agenda of sourcing and disseminating tech-inspired climate solutions.

### NCD Countries’ Strong Ownership

The programme will build on existing local platforms and initiatives for the sake of the absorption of the programme into local governance. Every programme country has a national steering committee that is responsible for the overall implementation, coordination, and oversight in terms of forms, levels, and maturity. The programme team will team up with such bodies to maximise the use of the current infrastructure and resources. This will ensure that the programme can be implemented without laying track even after the GCF-KDB exit. It shall minimise expenses for a new office set-up with newly purchased office supplies, electronics, and furniture. This satisfies a government demand to minimise the cost of implementing the programme.

In addition, recognising that the public sector engagement on public policy and regulatory framework is key to the success of the programme, the five countries will establish a Project Steering Committee (PSC) for each of the five NCD countries to be part of the Project Steering Committee that shall give guidance for the overall implementation of the programme. The PSCs will also provide oversight over the planning and execution of country-specific activities in the country-specific Sustainable Management Unit (SMU). The contextually fit SMU governance will ensure strong country ownership and participation of individual NCD countries.

### References

## Section II: Partnerships for the Goals – Cross-border Collaboration with Advanced Innovation Ecosystems to be Convened through Global Climate Technology Providers Joining the Initiative.
In pursuit of the vitalised global partnership venture, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) programme will invite and attract climate technology innovators from across the globe. The GCF programme will convene leading climate technologies being developed and deployed in Canada, China, Denmark, Finland, France, Germany, India, Indonesia, Italy, Japan, Korea, Malaysia, Mexico, Netherlands, Singapore, South Africa, Sweden, Switzerland, Taiwan, Thailand, Turkey, United Kingdom, United States of America, and Vietnam. VCs, accelerators, and other startup support intermediaries (e.g., CATALYST) will be invited to participate in the presence; global innovators in climate space will be sourced to ensure that cutting-edge technologies flow into the ultimate destination – the five emerging ASEAN markets as the test-bed.

### Concrete Examples of Networks and How They are to be Leveraged for the Programme
- **[KDB, Accredited Entity]**: Global presence of KDB through its subsidiary, KDB Asia LLC in London and Hong Kong. The GCF programme will leverage KDB’s sourcing global innovators in climate space through regular Global NextRound events (KDB signature iR events), alongside leveraging its Global Partnership Fund (GPF)-funded Fund scheme investments in global VCs.
- **[V2]**: The GPF will leverage its connection to VCs based in advanced climate responsive ecosystems, namely, Collaborative Capital, DCVC (based in San Francisco, US), Mekong Kapsa (based in Vietnam), Skyflower Ventures (based in Boston, US) and PITANGO and Carfurem.

The Programme’s Theme/Goal of Technology Transfer Shall Pitch Its Prominent Role at the UNFCCC COP23 Summit. The programme is designed to address the challenges of developing countries in overcoming the barriers to technology adoption and transfer of technologies at the 27th session of the Conference of the Parties of the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (COP23) in Bonn, Germany. This will be a monumental year for this programme that goes beyond the BAU climate business model and embraces an ambitious agenda to address the global challenge of climate change. The programme will focus on building innovative capacity to combat climate change. The programme will be officially showcased at the COP23 in Bonn, Germany.

### Socioeconomic Context for the Programme Countries [1]

Climate patterns in the region are somewhat consistent with defined dry and wet seasons, generally substantial annual rainfall amounts, consistently moderate to high temperatures with intermittent extreme heat events, and frequent tropical cyclones. The region is home to a diverse range of ecosystems and support several large transboundary rivers (i.e., Mekong River (Cambodia), Tonle Sap River (Cambodia), Red River (Vietnam)) whose floodplains provide delicate ecosystems to cultivate rice and other crops. In all, the region has experienced significant climate change impacts in recent years, including increased technology in response to the countries' high climate vulnerability and aspirations for low-carbon and climate-resilient sustainable economic development.

*Need for Adaptation: The region is considered one of the most climate vulnerable regions in the world.* [2] The climate hazard trends in the target countries show increases in rising atmospheric temperatures, changing precipitation patterns, sea level rise, ocean warming, and cyclones. Population growth rates are high (1.5% to 3%), with a significant proportion living below poverty lines. Increased exposure to these climate hazards combined with pre-existing vulnerabilities that communities experience from limited access to water and energy resources, low and frequent rainfall, and population density, insufficient healthcare systems, unsustainable agriculture, inadequate water resources and infrastructure, and

Need to act now: High rates of urbanisation and economic development coupled with an increase in energy access have led to a greater dependence on fossil fuels and higher emissions in Southeast Asia. In Vietnam, for example, rapid economic growth has propelled a steep increase in vehicle ownership—rising from less than 1% per capita in the early 1980s to 15% per capita by 2016. Emissions representing around 11% of the country’s GHG emissions are in total. With Southeast Asia experiencing the world's highest deforestation rates—at a 12% annually—Southeast Asia experienced the most rapid increases in carbon dioxide emissions from land use change and forestry.

To meet the Paris climate pledges, ASEAN nations will have to reduce emissions by 11% by 2030 as compared to current trajectories. Present lagging progress for needed investment and emissions rate of change, the International Energy Agency (IEA) concludes that making improvements in regulatory and financial frameworks is essential to achieve this goal. For example, the levelised cost of energy (LCOE) of solar PV in Indonesia could be around 40% lower if its investment and financing risks were comparable to advanced economies.

## Prioritisation of Climate Responsive Technology Needs

### Step 1: Climate Vulnerable Sector Prioritisation
Identify the target sectors via climate vulnerability situation analysis.

### Step 2: Climate Technology Prioritisation Criteria Setting
Develop criteria aimed at identifying technologies for prioritization.

### Step 3: Criteria Weighting
Consult with key stakeholders to determine weights for each criterion.

### Step 4: Climate Technology Rating and Scoring
Rate and score each criterion per technology with a pre-determined scale.

### Step 5: Climate Technology Prioritisation
Identify top priority technologies in each sector with sensitivity analysis.

## Philippines - The GP will provide support for low-carbon energy generation and low-carbon transportation as it is a key enabling factor in the solution, along with the government adopting supportive energy policy and regulatory framework with incentives to increase the use of electric and hybrid vehicles.

### Water stress
- Water stress in the region is projected to affect nearly 185 million people by 2050.
- Sea level rise is predicted to be between 36 cm and 74 cm, leading to coastal flooding and saltwater intrusion into aquifers occurring in highly populated, coastal cities such as Jakarta (Indonesia), Manila (Philippines), and Ho Chi Minh (Vietnam).
- Simultaneously, carbon-intensive growth trends will continue.
- Energy demand is projected to increase by more than 50% between now and 2030.

### Most likely Scenario in the Absence of Intervention
In the absence of technological intervention for a shift from fossil fuels towards low-carbon development and increased climate resilience, climate-driven impacts will continue and worsen. By 2100, rising temperatures in the region will reduce agricultural yields by up to 50% in some areas, leading to food insecurity and increased pressure on water resources due to stress.
- Water stress in the region is projected to affect nearly 185 million people by 2050. Sea level rise is predicted to be between 36 cm and 74 cm, leading to coastal flooding and saltwater intrusion into aquifers occurring in highly populated, coastal cities such as Jakarta (Indonesia), Manila (Philippines), and Ho Chi Minh (Vietnam).
- Simultaneously, carbon-intensive growth trends will continue.
- Energy demand is projected to increase by more than 50% between now and 2030.

### Technological Leapfrogging Is Necessary for Quick, Strong, and Enhanced Climate Actions
Urgent climate action is required. The most promising technologies need to be deployed throughout the five countries due to the state of climate urgency. Many climate technologies available today, like renewable and lithium-ion batteries, are not yet mature enough to be competitive in their markets to reach current climate goals.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Conclusion: Technology Leapfrogging via Technology Transfer

Technology leapfrogging is achieved with technology transfer from industrialized countries to late-comer economies. The low-carbon and climate-resilient technologies that are needed for the transition to a sustainable development path are not only available, but also proven frontier technologies, skipping the technological evolution process that can take decades of experimentation and innovation. This leapfrogging opportunity is particularly relevant in the context of the Green Climate Fund, where technology leapfrogging opportunities for partner countries to get, customize, apply, deploy, and scale best practices and innovative solutions are a core element.

## Significance of Technology Transfer Globally Recognized but Gaps Between Knowing and Doing

Achieving the objectives and goals of the Paris Agreement requires technological innovation that inevitably entails a widespread transfer of mitigation and adaptation technologies addressing the needs of developing countries. The technology gap between developed and developing countries is not only significant, it is necessary. Decades of global discourse have generated a consensus that technology transfer is a core element in achieving the climate change objectives set out by the Paris Agreement. Despite this recognition and some trials, barriers have continued to affect a broad set of processes covering the flow of innovative climate technology solutions being transferred to emerging markets, surely those of the ASEAN region.

### (1) Addressing Supply-Sided Controversy over Intellectual Property (IP) Rights

In reality, the problem is not so much about the availability of technologies but rather about the willingness of IP holders on whether developed economies have a commanding position over patents in certain sectors. In fact, there are many examples where technology suppliers and/or patent holders are reluctant to engage or simply refuse to license their patented technologies to a third party, while expressing fears of unreasonable compensation from the license that could weaken their competitive advantage. This is particularly true for developing countries which have limited real-world experiences of others in foreign countries whose bargaining power enforced technology transfer on terms that were not always favorable to them.

### (2) Addressing Demand-Sided Barriers via Ecosystem Building

Bringing climate technologies into transformational and scalable solutions in the low-emission development pathways due to barriers on the ground—though at different levels of severity according to varying stages of development. They are: lack of institutional capacity; weak governance and policy frameworks; information heavy bureaucracy that frustrates effective inter-ministerial coordination; institutional capacity on technology transfer; limited access to finance, including from multilateral climate funds; lack of capital and dominant private sector engagement; insufficiency of market readiness; a small-scale

Since early stages of idea brainstorming and concept designing, KDB has taken a proactive approach to explore partnerships amongst a scattered set of similar interventions at both national and regional levels across partner countries. For example, project partners engaged the Global ClearTech Innovation Programme (GCIP) in order to identify opportunities for co-financing and joint implementation of spending of funds. KDB plans to activity connect and find synergies with other global or regional initiatives such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the International Solar Alliance (ISA), the Clean Energy Access Programme, and GGGI Global and Regional Greeninnovations Program, and share lessons learned as well as synergetic.

Moreover, I will leverage the bargaining power of the UNFCCC under which 187 Parties have committed to climate technology transfer. It particularly proposes a compulsory licensing framework using standard terms as a patent protection and safety tool, under which global players will voluntarily engage in technology transfer. The framework also aims at preventing access to patented technologies without abusive practices sometimes committed by patent holders, such as the practice of distrust, secrecy, and patent abuse, such as excessive pricing.

In sum, the programme will (i) address supply-side addressed over intellectual property (IP) rights; (ii) improve supply-side opportunity through joint venture nurturing and critical growth funding; and (iii) minimize fragmentation and inefficiency in harmony with on-going interventions.

## B.2 (a): Theory of change narrative diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

### Key Concept Beneath the Surface of a Paradigm Shifting Transition

The UNFCCC (2018)1 spotlights the importance of supporting developing countries to source, digest, and deploy climate technology solutions in order to achieve their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The world is now amid rapidly escalating climate crisis. Against this backdrop, the programme has conceived its key profound concept: "technology transfer via a joint venture (JV) as a result of collaborative R&D". There are two reasons for this approach. First, the scale and complexity of climate change responses are so extensive that it is unlikely that those wishing to innovate can achieve all even if they are even the most advanced in their respective fields. Second, the programme will adopt a business model (B stands for business) module – further advanced than the existing collaborative R&D or RDD (2nd stage for demonstration) mode, taking one step forward to embrace both business modelling and investing.

### Addressing Double Sided Barriers on the Part of Both the "Receiver" & The "Provider": The Programme

The programme will address double-sided barriers on the part of both the receiver and provider low-emission and climate-resilient growth pathways, based on the premise that the right intervention mechanisms can help to overcome these barriers. This is particularly important in order to prevent further into emerging markets. In short, this transfer initiative pays special attention to the hurdles that global innovators of the relevant supply chain in industrialised economies face, in addition to those confronted by the developing countries. The programme will address these double-sided barriers on the part of both sides not only recipients but also providers, the programme will tackle the joint set of demand and supply barriers. This is particularly important for the latter as they are often more advanced than their host countries. Under the programme designed with four components, Components 1 and 4 end address demand-side barriers while the remaining two components manage supply-side obstacles as follows:

1. Supported by the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and implemented by the United Nations Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO).
2. UNFCCC: 2018. Climate Technology Innovators and Accelerators. Bonn: United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

(Components 1 and 4 shall address demand-side barriers of the five different countries with varying forms and depths of intervention as the severity, facets, and dynamics of such barriers differ between countries: e.g., from a complete impasse and near-total lack of an ecosystem to a massive but fragmented ecosystem. In all cases, the objective is to create a climate-resilient economy, at least five partner countries will enjoy acceleration activities that best fit individual local contexts. In fact, the current proposal builds on the success of the Silicon Valley-based Green Climate Fund (GCF) which was born over the course of supporting ICT start-ups in Silicon Valley. The two components will devise and deploy customized acceleration modules for the recipient, and will do its best to integrate them into the existing ecosystem.

(Components 2 and 3 shall address supply-side barriers that have barred global technology providers, talented innovators, and private investors from carrying out technology-intensive transactions with developing countries. These barriers include but are not limited to: a lack of infrastructure, a dearth of barriers including, but not limited to, high upfront costs due to immature supply chains of a nascent market not integrated into the global trading system; difficulties in teaming up with the right local business partners; and systemic discrimination against foreign entities, and commercially supplied country risk factors.

Diversified Mitigation/Adaptation Potentials via Joint Venture (JV) Business Intervention that Leads Technological Leapfrogging in MDC Countries. Initially, transit JVs will be supported throughout R&D&I acceleration activities to help the five partner countries overcome barriers to access and adoption of climate responsive technologies. Over the course of the businesses being mature in market ecosystems to be established, they will become a key driver for the development of an indigenous technology ecosystem space, be scaled, and eventually replace outdated rival businesses that feature emission-heavy, inefficient, and polluting technologies.

By immediately translating technology-based concepts and ideas into climate actions, JV businesses will accelerate the adoption and stabilization of the next generation of climate responsive technologies so that the transferred technologies will soon become competitive in individual markets, thereby moving straight towards a market-driven transition. The JVs will be supported by the GCF to build and nurture an indigenous mainstreamed entrepreneurship ecosystem with a singing technologically skilled, capable, and passionate female entrepreneurs and women-led SMEs as the key innovation agency that will propel bright climate solutions after mainstreaming transformed technologies and applying them into locally customized businesses.

Case-specific ToC Scenarios on What Paradigm Shifting Changes to be Derived from JV Business Deployment and its Consequences. The programme will see newly created JVs with investment incentives, technology transfer, and capacity building for the five partner countries. It will also investigate and adaptation outcomes in a society with an emergence of female talents (Outcomes with Co-benefits of TCoC), which will lead to a paradigm shifting innovation in the climate responsive entrepreneurial ecosystem of the five countries (Impacts of ToC). # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## Theory of Change (ToC)

### Background and Paradigm Shift
- Technology-enabled climate solutions are foundational for least developed countries.
- Gender mainstreamed entrepreneurship ecosystems are established.
- A paradigm shifting transition towards low-emission climate resilient economies with an enhanced technological foundation is achieved.

### Outcomes and Co-benefits
- Restored GAG emissions from fossil fuels with locally owned assets.
- Reduced GHG emissions from energy sector.
- Improved livelihoods of climate vulnerable communities through business with locally owned assets.
- Gender-balanced employment in the renewable energy sector.
- Emergence of tech-based enterprises, especially women-owned and off-grid businesses.

### Outputs
- Strengthened local capacity for technology transfer and innovation formation with gender mainstreaming.
- Climate-resilient agricultural practices.
- Private capital invested in climate solutions.
- Enhanced coordination of climate solutions across sectors.
- Climate-tech innovations and business models that are scalable and replicable.

### Programme Activities
1. 3.1. Set up a Regional Centre for Technology Transfer and Innovation (RCTTI) with local entrepreneurs and institutions to support the national IV programme.
2. 3.2. Adopt global tech transfer mechanisms.
3. 3.3. Facilitate technology transfer through RCTTI.
4. 3.4. Achieve scale with climate solutions.

### Barriers & Role
- Weak awareness of climate technology transfer and innovation.
- Lack of finance (both supply and investment).
- Lack of capacity for technology transfer and innovation.
- Regulatory restrictions for foreign entities in the renewable energy sector.
- COVID-19 impacts.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

Designing the Programme that Addresses Both Demand and Supply Side Barriers to Empower the ASEAN Climate Innovation Ecosystem for Climate Technology Transfer and Dissemination Acceleration. The programme shall address the systemic process of climate technology innovation that underpins a range of interacting actors with business accelerators anchored. It is structured with four different components using a two-actor model (entrepreneurs and ecosystem stakeholders) to address both demand-side and supply-side barriers (left side bar panel). Individual projects and activities will closely function in order to reinforce synergies by bridging the gaps which have kept all the lines separate from a multi-modal innovation leap.

### Designing Barrier-based Action Plan

#### Pressing Barriers to be Addressed
- Scattered small-scale international/international start-up support tools that are mostly grant based and not aligned with ASEAN national level policy and regulatory engagement.
- Lack of qualified entrepreneurs with climate entrepreneurship and business acceleration skills, with a gender bias in terms of limited opportunities by gender and others (geography, psychographics, etc.).
- Lack of qualified entrepreneurs with climate responsive technology uptake capacity (tech diligence, market analysis, product concept, communication skills).
- Lack of sophisticated acceleration infrastructure in a local space e.g. ASEAN Business Incubators or business accelerators vested in climate.
- Perceived high risk and low return to enter risky developing markets.
- Difficulty in attracting qualified local partners under a fair standard competition format.
- Expensive foreign technologies.
- High upfront costs in a nascent market.
- Lack of access to adequate R&D and business acceleration opportunities.
- Difficulty in accessing finance for climate actions and tech entrepreneurship.
- Disruption of the economy due to economic slowdown, market fluctuations.

#### Activities as Solutions
- **Activity (1.1)**: National Sustainability Management Unit set up National Sustainability Management Unit with the Steering Committee with the government level engagement.
- **Activity (1.2)**: Launch flexibility exit sourcing strategies to identify and attract qualified local entrepreneurs of different gender focus (e.g., recruiting female students from STEM fields, women entrepreneurs).
- **Activity (1.3)**: Other targeted outreach activities specifically sensitizing to rural/near rural JV candidates ready to partner with global tech entrepreneurs.
- **Activity (2.1)**: Set up the Global Acceleration Advisory Service to provide technical and advisory services of various business areas.
- **Activity (2.2)**: Launch global sourcing strategies to attract qualified local entrepreneurs or partners trained/qualified local entrepreneurs.
- **Activity (2.3)**: Set up and execute a technology transfer and business modelling in a legally permitted way through the use of joint ventures, collaborative R&D&BC acceleration services.
- **Activity (2.4)**: Set up and execute an investment fund with co-investment for raising investment capital on a global scale.
- **Activity (3.1)**: Execute equity investments with strategic portfolio management as per investment criteria set by the Steering Committee.
- **Activity (3.2)**: Formulate exit strategies and achieve exits with a reasonable rate of return.
- **Activity (3.3)**: Formulate the best workable local ecosystem network framework

### Component 1 – A Starting Point for NOL Countries to be the Climate Tech Capable Agent.
The first component is this climate technology transfer initiative's starting point that sources promising local entrepreneurs and accelerates their capacity so that they could be equipped with the proper awareness and resources to compete in the global market. The second component will focus on scaling up the initiative. Shortlisted local companies will be supported in order to satisfy the National Climate Entrepreneur Acceleration Program (NCEAP) Investment Criteria that requires qualifications NOL countries' local entities are not able to be equipped.

### N-CEAP Investment Criteria for Local Entrepreneurs

| Criteria | Eligibility Criteria | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. **Locally Owned Entities** | Entrepreneurs with valid business registration in NOL countries; or | The entrepreneur must have a minimum of 50% equity ownership in the entity, and be part of the SMU Steering Committee (composed by representatives from GCF NDAs and line ministries in NOL countries). |
|  | In case that a vulnerable NOL country needs transfer of certain climate technologies but does not | The entrepreneur must have a minimum of 50% equity ownership in the entity, and be part of the SMU Steering Committee (composed by representatives from GCF NDAs and line ministries in NOL countries). |

## Selection Criteria

### Scored-based on the entrepreneur's score on N-CEAP

#### Technology Uptake Capacity
- Local entrepreneurs deemed to have capacity for foreign technology uptake and digestion in terms of staffing expertise – a minimum level of 10% of total workforce is composed by women.
- (1st Round) To be assessed by the Technical Advisory Group (TAG) and/or the NOL GP team via the Regional SMU.

#### Gender Mainstreaming
- Women-led SME candidates shall be prioritised for selection.
- SMEs with at least one woman participates in the Board of Directors or in the management committees such as ESG committees, among others (CEO, COO, CTO, etc.) and/or
- SMEs where more than 30% of the total workforce are females.

#### Funding Contribution
- To be consulted and assessed by gender experts of the SMU based on the criteria outlined in Annex 2 of Annexes 6

## NOL Country-specific Business Acceleration Component in Proposition 1. This first component differentiates specific contents of the National Climate Entrepreneur Accelerator Programme (N-CEAP) from the general framework programme, which is designed to be a flexible and scalable structure.
Under the general programme framework (above table), the detailed nature of sub-activities will vary according to the country-specific context and the needs of individual beneficiaries. Consequently, all NOL countries will benefit from the N-CEAP, though the specific content may differ across different five countries. Assessed country-specific uniqueness is as follows (further details in Annex 3).

### Cambodia
Given its crowded local incubation landscape, the Cambodian leg of the programme will focus on later-stage acceleration and JV readiness. The Cambodian SMU will partner with existing local associations and partners, including Cambodia Investment Club, Cambodia Women Entrepreneurs Association, Platform Impact, Young Entrepreneurs Association, UNIDO, and...

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

## Indonesia - The Indonesian SMU will be set up in collaboration with the relevant Government of Indonesia's agency or ministry, consisting of the GGGI implementing team and representatives of the host country's government. This SMU will provide a platform for capacity building and training to effectively attract qualified technology entrepreneurs with high changes to form JvVs with global innovators. The SMU will aim to match Indonesian teams up to shortlist at least five potential climate techpreneurs for global partnership.

### Laos - Given a very nascent nature of the relevant ecosystem, the Lao leg of the programme seeks to build capacity and awareness among key stakeholders in the country, including the young entrepreneurs association of Laos. Strongly supported by the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment, GCF I-NDA in partnership with Lao National Chamber of Commerce and Industry will provide training and mentoring for 100+ climate techpreneurs from across Laos and reach ca. 300 local firms in relevant industries.

### Philippines - Given that the Philippines has a wide pool of active local and international climate experts, it is proposed that the Philippines be consulted for the N-CEAP design to ensure it necessary for addressing climate related issues on the ground. The Philippines' leg of the programme will focus on building capacity among key stakeholders in the country, including through Component 1, while strengthening local ecosystem stakeholders’ capacity centred around the Philippine Business for Climate Impact Network under Component 2.

### Vietnam - Given a large number of start-ups and entrepreneurs who recognize a desperate need for foreign technologies but cannot afford to buy due to realistic hurdles, which is different from situations of other participating countries in a severe vulnerability, the programme will propose that Vietnam be consulted for the N-CEAP design. The Vietnamese government could uptake transferred technologies from global peers after 3-6 months' N-CEAP provision and an advanced technology assessment.

## Component 2 - Best Available Business Acceleration from a Global Team with Multi-Experts

The second component aims to source and train global innovators and technology providers as per Accessible Investment Criteria. The business acceleration programme will be established by matching local teams (N-CEAP graduates) globally, and domiciling nominally as a result of N-CEAP under Component 1) and sourced global innovators, and to eventually achieve climate-responsive technology transfer by translating IT technology solutions into climate-ready businesses of the matched teams.

| Criteria | Eligibility Description |

| **Awareness of the Programme** | Global innovators must be aware of the Green Climate Fund programme mandate that climate (responsive) technologies are to be applied for business deployment across from the five (5) NDL countries, in alignment with the GCF I-NDA. |
| **M1: Energy generation and energy efficiency** | M1.1 Buildings, cities and appliances |
| **M3: Buildings, cities and industries and appliances** | M4.1 Vehicles |
| **A1: Most vulnerable people and communities** | A1.1 Buildings, cities and appliances |

## Selection priority given to global entrepreneurs with investment capacity and/or willingness

In order to support ASEAN-global teams to satisfy the Acceleration Investment Criteria above, the programme will deliver on collaborative R&D activities overall as below.

### (1) Sourcing and Shortlisting of Global Technology Providers with Climate Responsive Solutions

By leveraging the global innovation network consisting of VCs, accelerators, and other start-up support intermediaries with global presence, NHs (as Executing Entity of Component 2) will launch a series of market entry readiness training sessions for global climate tech innovators with advanced climate responsive ecosystems, including but not limited to Canada, Singapore, Denmark, Germany, and the Netherlands. The teams will be selected based on their potential to be sourced to engage in the programme to scale up their impactful technologies into flow into the five ASEAN countries. This selection process will be guided by a series of diagnostic assessment on the technological suitability, market viability and scalability potential (both in terms of technology fitness and climate outcomes) by the GP team.

### (2) Tailor-made Consultation and Guidance on Business Strategy

With shortlisted global teams on board, a series of tailor-made consultation sessions will take place to go through their business models and strategies by closely examining risk factors, business projections and technology transfer capacity. Such baseline analysis will provide a solid ground for the teams to identify potential opportunities in the five ASEAN countries.

Such consultation will also aim to help shape the go-to-market strategies of global tech providers by offering deep dive sessions on respective local markets of the five NOL countries, including on-site presentations from relevant stakeholders and agencies. This will further inform their business expansion exercise. Through this process, each team will have strategized measures to secure market access in the five ASEAN countries, with a series of tailored actions being laid out. Actions, while their business models with identified viable technologies will be confirmed, primed for proposing to local companies with technology needs subject to the JV establishment.

### (3) ASEAN Market Entry Readiness Training & P/c Support

Based on previous consultation sessions validating core climate (responsive) technology as well as local market readiness, a series of tailored training and general climate tech innovators will be provided with a series of activities enhancing their readiness for the ASEAN market. Training on principles of business development, market entry strategies, and other relevant topics will inform the general business ecosystem of the target NOL countries. Furthermore, similar practices that include technology transfer through bespoke schemes such as JV partnership (onshore target set-up as a benchmark, allowing the teams to examine potential issues in business expansion.

Upon formulating tailor-made market entry strategies, the programme aims to leverage local networks of extensive relevant stakeholders and partners, particularly state agencies, to facilitate substantive engagement with the five ASEAN countries. This will be done through climate tech ecosystems, in close partnership and intensive discussions with the Regional SMU under KDB guidance.

### (4) Technology Transfer Modalities and Business Development

With the global climate tech innovators identified, accelerated and ready to expand into the target NOL countries, a series of business matching consultations will take place, to undertake a genuine technology transfer through bespoke schemes i.e., JV partnership (onshore target set-up as a

## Locally initiated JV between a local company and a global climate technology company contributing its technology transfer or asset including both cash and in-kind) and/or technology licensing agreement, with the latter being subject to an equity holding (e.g., 5% equity upon investment). In addition to aforementioned commercial screening, consulting sessions for building a genuine technology transfer through other likely recommended business models are to be offered as well. The following is a list of such business models: Joint Venture (JV), Technology Transfer guidance, following similar procedures involving feasibility studies on business and technology; e.g., local company will have the opportunity to license or acquire global climate technology on its own, with no need for additional technology sourcing/licensing; rather, capable of employing the technology in its own operations, including production, consumption, etc.

In order to assess compatibility between the two partners, the matchings between arrangements and local and global entities will first entail feasibility studies covering business plan, profitability, financial sustainability, and climate change. Furthermore, aside from a business perspective, technical aspects will be looked into as well; in an aim to review the potential commercialization of respective climate tech-driven solutions.

### (5) Consultation on Climate Advisory and Investment

In order to align each business model with viable climate outcomes, a professional climate advisory service will be provided to guide businesses with adequate guidance and framework on capturing climate outcomes via Project Selection Tool based on GCF. Additionally and through Task 4A (see below), the project selection tool will be used to identify the potential fit of such technology transfer models as well as to conduct due diligence for the business model. The results of such E&G-related factors related into their operation and monitoring of supply chains.

### (6) Investment Attraction and Facilitation Support

Upon the set-up of technology transfer in local/global partnership formats, the programme will offer dedicated support services aimed at attracting investment by not only sharpening the IR resources but also providing consultation to rebuild business strategies considering the investor’s needs. In addition, for those entities that are interested in exploring new markets, if necessary, the learners will further be mentored by global and local accelerators and venture capitalists to strengthen the market linkage and build relationships for potential investment in the future.

In parallel with such support given to the businesses, the GCF and local partner entities of Complementary Actions will also provide a regional demo day to showcase the progress made at the accelerated learners’ potential and expand the network of climate tech-driven investment space. As part of support, a regional demo day to a targeted audience of investors and industry stakeholders as both an opportunity for them to learn about the progress made by the learners and to pave the way forward.

### Component 2 as the Channel of Global Innovators Joining Climate Action

The term of the second component is to invite global companies that have not paid attention to the UNFCCC/SBM strategic goals which, reframe their technology capacity as per above listed business acceleration services, and subsequently join the climate action. The GCF will provide a platform for such companies to engage, including but not limited to strict E&G, gender, IP risk requirement resolutions, thereby unlocking their climate responsive tech expertise to fit the target NOL countries. For this, the NHS centered at the Team Net Hub (TNH) will be responsible for identifying and engaging with global innovators to identify investment opportunities in partnership with more than 100 global CEF/VC partners in Singapore, the US, Europe, and Asia-Pacific regions, and so on. Once such global innovators identified, the second component will support them to be ready to satisfy the Acceleration Investment Criteria below and eventually CTF Investment Criteria.

ASEAN-fit Global Acceleration Teams to be Optimised with Kernel Professionals in Component 2.

A global acceleration team will be optimised by experts of experience including IP and patent, accounting, tax law, financial planning, business valuation, market funding, and professional mentoring services. A series of workshops on risk management, and gender mainstreaming, will be organised to offer collaborative R&Ds consulting services. The objective is to ensure that the teams are well equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge.

Based on the above, it can be concluded that the global acceleration team will be optimised by kernel professionals who have the expertise each business needs – i.e., Collaborative R&Ds acceleration services to be co-designed/anchor operator with business patenting, etc. The programme will also provide professional mentoring/R&D support to teams in order to validate the sustainability of each team’s core business model pertaining to climate outcomes, and to do so on a global scale.

In order to build its expansive relationship with global stakeholders nurturing and advancing innovation ecosystems, the programme, specifically NHIS, as Executing Entity of Component 2, shall make proactive collaborations with global acceleration partners with different expertise and focus areas that are proven capable of delivering high-quality services. The programme will also establish a long-term strategic engagement partnership. With the multi-disciplinary team on board, Component 2 shall proceed with which includes: (i) identifying potential JVs to be established in NOL countries; (ii) developing business plans for JVs to devise technology-enabled climate solutions, enabling selected forward-looking businesses to advance their scalable capacities in five emerging markets, and finally realising a commercialisation mission of the JVs in the respective markets; and (iii) providing professional mentoring services to finalise their mid- to long-term business plans tailored to the local setting.

Component 3 – Climate Technologypreneurship Fund (CTF) with GCF Catalytic Riskless Equity Capital under the Risk-Adjusted Investment Framework. Component 3 is at the very core of this programme that intends to provide a risk-adjusted investment framework for CTF anchor investments and finance graduates from Component 2 that have proven climate technology applied businesses most suitable to invest in. The CTF will be structured as an equity fund with a focus on providing capital to support technology transfer into NOL countries to grow small and medium sized enterprises (SMEs) to expand climate responsive businesses while addressing a missing middle corporate structure caused by a wide gap between start-ups and large corporations.

The CTF adopts a risk-adjusted investment framework to cover various risks that arise from diverse portfolio companies in multiple NOL countries. Direct capital injection is the default mode of investment but, if needed, additional layer of a special purpose vehicle (SPV) may be structured to accommodate complex structures and/or to provide greater flexibility for investors who are looking for more convenience. The SPV differentiates itself from a BAU Fund of Funds (FF) that is an expensive vehicle intended to provide capital to early-stage climate technology businesses. The CTF will adopt tiers of acceptable risk and capital configuration ratio differ country by country, even amongst industries within a single country, thereby necessitating deal-by-deal adjustment via navigating, calculating, and managing associated risks. For instance, a project with a low risk may be structured as an equity investment as default, while a project with a higher risk may be structured with an 85/15 debt-equity capital configuration ratio. In any case, projects that require a 50/50 debt-equity capital configuration will be subject to further scrutiny.

It shall deal with the problematic finding of climate technology graduates that most graduates from initiatives with one-off seed grants have failed to attract further investment capital and progress to more advanced stages. In recognition of the finding, the fund shall invest in JVs or other forms of entities that establish a strategic partnership between an NOL country’s SMEs and international partners. Such case barriers bar the JV model and prevent from achieving the programme objectives. Only applicants

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 31

## Local Ownership of Innovation

- Clear evidence must prove that a proposed technology-enabled climate solution is not one of the following:
  - A first-of-its-kind in NCD countries.
  - More cost-effective than existing solutions.
  - The only viable alternatives are not available in the domestic market.

## Additionality

- Clear evidence must be provided to demonstrate that a proposed solution overcomes existing barriers, contributes to market development, and is financially sustainable (including knowledge and capacity), and applies best technology(ies).

## EAS Safeguards (E3S)

### Negative Screening
- Businesses defined in IFC Exclusion List NOT eligible for investment.
- Businesses with adverse impacts on indigenous peoples NOT eligible for investment. By filling one of the categories below:
  - Enterprises that result in loss of lands and natural resources subject to traditional ownership or customary use or occupation.
  - Enterprises that result in relocation of indigenous peoples from lands and natural resources subject to traditional ownership or customary use or occupation.
  - Enterprises that may potentially impact cultural heritage, where the commercial use of the cultural heritage of indigenous peoples would need IFC approval.

- E&S risk categorization
  - Category A1: Businesses (Category A1) are NOT eligible for investment

## Cultural Heritage
- Cultural heritage includes but is not limited to natural areas with cultural and/or spiritual value such as sacred groves, sacred bodies of water and waterways, sacred mountains, sacred trees, sacred rocks, burial grounds, shrines, and other places that hold significance in relation to the identity, ceremonial, or spiritual aspects of the affected indigenous peoples' lives. (GCF IPP, Para 63)

# Selection Criteria — Scoring Priority

## Business Feasibility

- **JV dimension (horizontal)**: The new team must be able to operate the business.

- **(Transition Dimension) Commercial viability**: Local market fit

- **Financial sustainability**: Bankability

- **CFF criteria**: CFF will assess whether it proves business feasibility, in case NOL countries need the business urgently.

## Co-benefits & Sustainability

- **Social co-benefits**, such as improvements in health and safety, access to education, cultural preservation, improved access to energy, etc., and/or improved quality of life and access to other public utilities such as water supply.

- **Environmental co-benefits**, including increased air, water and soil quality, biodiversity conservation, etc.

This will be consulted to KDB/GCF.

CTF will prioritise the applicant enterprises and activities that meet the following conditions:

- Specialised application of indigenous technology for climate tech R&Ds; and/or

## Balanced Portfolio

### Component 4 (Technical Assistance), TA - Ecosystem Building in Pursuit of a Genuine Country Ownership and Sustainability.

Component 4 will provide the country-specific TA activities to contribute to the overall objective of building an effective, efficient, and sustainable ecosystem with similar grant funding priority/multipurpose-building projects of other international development agencies.

This component aims to prepare or develop each country’s pioneer scouting and acceleration training organism and ecosystem to nurture top talents on the ground and build on existing local platforms and initiatives for the sake of the programme's harmony with the local governance. Almost every programme component will be designed in a way that it can be scaled up, replicated, and/or adapted to other contexts. The programmes will demonstrate different forms, levels, governance, and maturity. The programme will team up with such beneficiaries as government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector entities, etc., for efficient operationalisation and staying on track even after the GCF KOB exit, i.e., it shall minimise reliance on external funding and ensure sustainability.

In particular, each country has its own Intellectual Property (IP) policy related regulatory framework, though all of them are relatively vulnerable and require improvement with international support. Targeted five countries can be divided into two groups on the basis of what KBG has researched (see Annex 5 for a detailed list):

* **1st group comprises Indonesia, the Philippines, Vietnam, all of which are at the initial stage of IP framework development, reflecting aspects such as technology license validation and IP enforcement.** These countries have made significant progress in developing their own advanced IP frameworks to provide comfort for global tech providers; these governments' continued efforts to improve their IP regulatory framework will be a key factor in attracting foreign investments. The improved regulatory framework will advance thanks to the governments’ strong willingness, though their policy-making capacity is still relatively weak. In addition, there are no specific provisions on technology licensing and commercialised IP assets, e.g., compulsory licenses to override IP rights have been issued by the government. The programme shall support their direction of development, while addressing the existing bottlenecks.

* **2nd group consists of Cambodia and Laos, generally classified as the least developed countries. Due to a relatively smaller size of economies and trade volume, there seems comparatively less information on the IP regulatory framework in these two countries.** However, the programme’s PRF outcome will serve as a starting point for a long regulatory development journey towards a more advanced IP framework in this highly vulnerable group.

The programme will also use the case-by-case experience that they would perceive the concept of the IP rights and technical procedures in a practical way. This will help local JVs to better understand how to protect their intellectual property, enhance providers and enhances their access to needed technologies. This will lead countries to seriously consider the regulatory recommendations delivered from Component 4, and to materialise TA deliverables on the ground – with a focus on strengthening country ownership in executing optimised and line management.

### Country Ownership Built Sustainability Management Unit (SMU), as the Core Decision-making

As part of the overall programme structure, the Country Ownership Built Sustainability Management Unit (SMU) with an aim to strengthen country ownership in executing optimised and line coordinated-on-the-ground activities fit to individual NCL countries, and to concentrate allocated resources on the most critical areas. The SMU will be established at the national level for each NCL country. Regional SMU at the GGGI Seoul HQ; and (2) National SMU at each NCL country’s GGGI Country Office. These SMUs are not separate legal entities but rather a functional unit within the GGGI structure, not a separate legal entity) as are follow in brief: (1) country ownership; and (i) effective programme management.

- The strengthening of country ownership: This creation of mechanism through which NDA and line ministries can make the decision-making intervention due to reasons (e.g., the technology needs evolve, considering the late implementation after board approval and F&A signing), which led to the SMU being established. The SMU will be a functional unit within GGGI structure, not as an entity but operational based on such feedback from the PSC under the principle of country ownership.

- The channel of the effective programme management: Given the multi-country targeting and the programme’s cross-cutting nature, it is essential to have a strong and stable SMU. The SMU will act as a core channel of the effective planning, resource management, communication, and coordination between various stakeholders in four components for the sake of the optimal investment outcomes and climate impacts.

## Regional SMU - KDB Advisory Management & NII Engagement for Investment Linkages

The core decision-making of the two components will be advised, guided, and determined by the Regional SMU meetings on an as-needed basis. The Regional SMU will also provide guidance to the KDB team at the Regional SMU meetings. KDB shall advise and approve the major decisions made in the Regional SMU meetings.

The Regional SMU team’s partner local entities (local bodies in the relevant countries) will be responsible for:

- Ensuring the use of existing platforms and the eventual integration of the relevant resources into the ecosystem of the NOL countries in preparation for investment linkages.
- Identifying and engaging a sufficient number of competent property qualified service providers – e.g., service provider with proven track record, strong local presence, and/or latest company selection, and synergistic strategy amongst any one component, as the control tower of the Regional SMU that gives guidance to the NII.

The Regional SMU will invite NII affiliates in the decision-making process as for local private sector partnership, company sourcing, and acceleration activities (Activities 1.2 and 1.3) that require a close linkage with other subsequent components. At the Regional SMU meeting chaired by KDB, NII representatives will be invited to participate in discussions on: (i) the selection of suitable companies and local market expertise with established resources; (ii) will participate in the procurement and evaluation process for the selection of service providers; (iii) provide a detailed project description with qualifications wanted from an investor’s perspective, evaluating candidates’ qualifications, and selecting the best candidate; (iv) will be invited to engage in discussions on how to better engage discussing promising pipeline/portfolio company sharing with that selected local top-liners, and approve the final selection of local JV candidates at demo days that eventually determine the finalists in Component 1. The Regional SMU will also provide guidance for the NII’s participation in the activities in Component 1, in addition to its own list of companies introduced by the NDAs and line ministries and its own criteria for selecting the best candidate. The Regional SMU will be responsible for establishing an institutional system, but NII has the prior decision-making authority and veto rights in case such procedures and outcomes are not fit into investments planned afterwards in Components 2 and 3, therefore, the Regional SMU has been found in most of one-off used grant provision events without any follow-up investment at scale.

GGGI will set up different forms of the in-house National SMU fitting into each NOL Country’s unique context and needs. The GGGI National SMU will be responsible for the activities in Components 1, 2, and 4 in each country as a core implementation unit under the direction of the Regional SMU under KDB supervision and advisory guidance. The rationale of the five National SMUs is the different nature of the NOL countries’ needs and contexts. GGGI will also support the International Climate Entrepreneur Accelerator Programme (N-CEAP). As differentiated N-CEAPs should be designed and adapted to each country’s context, GGGI will provide a standardised framework for designing country customised N-CEAP provision with the support of both local governments and technical experts, thereby necessitating the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and Technical Advisory Committee (TAC) under the National SMU.

- **Project Steering Committee (PSC):** A high-level decision-making body. The PSC will be composed by representatives from the local government (or NGO), GGGI, academia, and relevant public and private sectors; the composition varies as per NDA instructions as below. The duties of the PSC

## INCLUSION OF HIGH-LEVEL MINISTERIAL COORDINATION, OVERALL POLICY GUIDANCE, AND NEED-BASED INSTITUTIONAL ARRANGEMENTS FOR TIMELY AND EFFECTIVE IMPLEMENTATION.

### TECHNICAL ADVISORY COMMITTEE (TAC)
An ad-hoc technical advisory body will be established with working groups to address specific needs of project experts, technicians, and/or private sector representatives to address technical and operational issues that arise during project implementation in a timely manner, if necessary.

See below the tentative five National Hubs that have been formulated as a result of consultation with NCEF partners. The establishment of these hubs is important for climate technology-enabled business acceleration activities. All the participating local partner entities will be involved in the process of establishing the hub and selecting the lead implementing partners as a result of extensive stakeholder consultation and feasibility studies, though it may be subject to change due to local ecosystem dynamics including the ministerial reshuffling.

In Components 1 & 4, the SMU aims at local companies' compliance with N-GEF investment criteria in terms of gender mainstreaming. In Component 2, only local graduates screened as per the investment criteria in Component 1 can enjoy collaboration with global commercial partners. The SMU will also be responsible for screening and gender mainstreaming awareness – i.e., qualifications which local companies are rarely exposed to. The SMU will perform the following functions: (i) provide technical assistance and work; (ii) undertake and oversee delivery of all outputs in a timely and cost-effective way; (iii) coordinate communication between NCEF, NCEF partners, and other stakeholders; (iv) collaborate with NH in charge of Components 2 and 3 activities, among others. NH will approach and communicate with SMU on a regular and on-the-ground need basis. Often, SMU will contribute its resources and in-kind services to the project.

### CROSS-FUNCTIONAL & MULTI-DIRECTIONAL COLLABORATION BETWEEN COMPONENTS
The programme's success requires immense flexibility between components and agility in intuitive decision-making because the components are interdependent. The NCEF has adopted a Joint Venture (JV) model as the mainstream technology transfer partnership model, as prescribed in investment criteria: a default deal flow from Component 1 (locally driven readiness) and Component 2 (global market access).

Nevertheless, a scenario may spin out of the lack of available local partner entities in certain industrial sectors or unpredictable barriers that hinder investment approval and execution despite the strong needs. In such cases, the technology provider may be excluded from the JV model, resulting in a very weak collaboration with the project implementation entity condition. Another example is a foreign company that has already partnered with a foreign technology provider, in search of other methods of earning risk capital to establish its presence in the local market. In such situations, alternative deal flow arrangements could be considered. Furthermore, if the investor's gender capacity is proven insufficient due to a lack of understanding of gender mainstreaming and/or gender equality, it may be recommended to consult with related experts (Component 2) for impact assessment and gender mainstreaming trainings, a prerequisite for fund capital injections as shown below deal flow chart.

## 4.3. Promote sustainability via the National SPMs per unit

### Obtained with a set of policies for executing and managing on-the-ground programs, contracts, logistics, etc.

The Korea Development Bank (KDB) Accredited Entity As an accredited entity, KDB shall run and supervise the programme overseeing the implementation process. The programme will be implemented but closely associated by working hand in hand partner entities. KDB will lead, oversee, and manage the programme, while the partner entities will provide technical support. This has fostered the economic growth and industrial advancement of Korea over the last seven decades. In particular, KDB has been a leading policy bank for promoting innovation and providing banking services for promising start-ups and SMEs with technological prowess since the market began to take a shape during the late 1950s. Meanwhile, as a leading policy financing bank, KDB has set up an internal infrastructure for supporting start-up ecosystems in Korea. The KDB Infrastructure for Start-up Ecosystem Building supports them in growing into global unicorns for the country's sake of innovative growth through new industrial engines.

### KDB Infrastructure for Start-up Ecosystem Building

Above all things, the bank's flagship venture funding program called KDB Next Round is an example of KDB's efforts to building a start-up ecosystem. IR rounds are held three days a week in the auditorium on the ground floor of the bank's headquarters in Seoul, classified by the development stage of start-ups. The programme consists of 10-minute pitch sessions for start-ups every Monday and Wednesday, and 25 IR rounds for Series C to Pre-IPO on Fridays - hence, covering the complete lifecycle of a start-up from seed funding to IPO. KDB Next Round has been held in Seoul since its inception in the midst of live streaming services. It has so far generated around USD 4 billion in total funding through more than 100 rounds as of August 2018, as of December 2020.

### Start-up Supervision and Guidance as a Service

Start-up supervision and guidance as a service will be primarily provided by Climate Business Team of KDB HQ while leveraging its in-house three-tier venture investment platform and global start-up VC acceleration and investment networks i.e., Singapore Venture Desk, London Venture Desk, and HK Green Growth Desk. The KDB HQ's Climate Business Team will also provide mentorship to the centres will support the HQ to be equipped with information on timely trends about global acceleration and股权投资等。The four components of the programme are designed to work in tandem to serve as the control tower that aims at seamless interlinked management amongst the four (different) components of the programme.

### Emerging Entities of Countries 1 & 4: KDB, Global Green Growth Initiative (GGGI)

Gearing GGGI's Local Presence, a Noticiable Track Record as a "Greenpreneurs Program" Initiator, and Experience to Manage Non-refundable GGGI Grant Proceeds. KDB developed a strategic partnership with GGGI amidst the wake of the pandemic crisis in appreciation of its in-country presence and experience in managing non-refundable GGGI grant proceeds. The programme aims at promoting NDC fulfillment. Gearing GGI's established local presence, KDB-GGGI partnership has ensured that the programme is well-positioned to deliver on its mandate. As a result, all 10 of the NDAs had stopped their administrative function in mid of COVID-19-caused national lockdown. In a nutshell, the programme has been able to minimise the impacts of COVID-19-imposed international travel restrictions and build constructive stakeholder relationships with the NDAs and potential implementing entities.

partners, with advantage of the five countries' GGGI membership. Such a strategic partnership with GGGI will strengthen the programme’s alignment with the country ownership principle, through close communication with NDA, line ministries, and influential market players across the five partner countries.

Above all things, GGGI Greenpreneurs Program is an incubator supporting young people to devise social enterprises that are aligned with the SDGs. It has been implemented in 10 GGGI member countries. It consists of web-based training modules, connecting with mentors and subject matter experts, business plan development, and provision of seed funding for winners. Since 2016, the programme engaged, on average, 200 young entrepreneurs every year. Over a total of four years, and has supported 57 teams from 20 different countries, including Indonesia and Cambodia. GGGI has also been working with local partners to scale up the programme by partnering with start-ups and MEMs in diverse sectors in GGGI member countries with various interventions.

In addition, GGGI has been closely working with various NDAs for the GCF Readiness Programmes as a Delivery Partner under “GCF-GGGI Framework Readiness and Preparatory Support Grant Agreement”, where GGGI has supported 10 countries to develop their national climate change strategies, build capacity in green and climate finance and strong relationship with public and private stakeholders in both domestic and international markets. As of December 2022, GGGI has contributed USD 8.6 billion in green investment commitments for its members and partners over the course of 2015-2022. In 2023, GGGI has contributed to green investment mobilisation valued at USD 1.8 billion, and has delivered USD 47 million in climate finance.

### Executing Entity of Component 2: NHI Investment & Securities (NHS)

#### Strengthening a Linkage between Components 2 (Grant Component for Acceleration) and 3 (Equity Component for Investment)
The programme pursues a close link between Components 2 and 3 because the two components are complementary in nature. The two components together have great significance for the sake of achieving desirable climate impacts. Feasibility studies including situation assessment, gap analysis, and stakeholder consultations identify a key problematic issue that most of the projects identified under Component 2 are not ready to be scaled up due to lack of investment at the initial phase of investment capital needed for scale-up, market entry, and commercialisation. In consideration with this, it is important to have a close link between Components 2 and 3 so that the two components can work together in order to achieve the desired climate impacts. Both stage accelerators and acceleration investors are involved in the designing and programming of climate technology projects and acceleration services customised to individuals/VCs with different technical needs since the initial stage.

In order to achieve this purview, NHS is to be invited into the CTF Investment Committee (IC) as an active member. The IC will provide guidance on investment opportunities for Component 2. By virtue of Component 2, NHS will minimise undesirable cases that seed grant-supported enterprises cannot acquire the required funds are ineligible for component 3 and get lost. This will enable a close link between multi-stakeholder platforms (Component 1) and project portfolio management (Component 3), which will foster the programme’s effectiveness in terms of resource mobilisation.

In order to allow accelerated high-potential VCs with measurable climate impacts to successfully grasp investment opportunities, NHS Investment & Securities will serve as a control tower and focal point to take on a role in the CTF. The IC will provide guidance on investment opportunities for Component 2. This is not limited to creating acceleration work plans, facilitating partnership arrangements with local/governmental agencies, and providing technical assistance to Component 1 and accelerators under optimised communication and resource management, and tracking and reporting performance to KUL in accordance with the GCF principles.

### INVESTMENT PARTNERS

As the CTF GP (fund manager), NHARP bears all authorities and responsibilities pertaining to the CTF general operations. The authorities related to investment are managed by NH ARP, and the CTF Investment Committee (CTF IC) is responsible for approving investments in line with the CTF's investment policy. The CTF IC will be appointed by NH ARP and will consist of two members: one member from NH ARP and another from KDB. The CTF IC's investment decision is to be made by affirming or vetoing deals from the two representatives, professional advice from climate impact and compliance managers with veto rights. While the role of the Climate Impact Manager (CIM) is to provide professional advice on climate impact and compliance for all investment deals, the managers will be given veto right during both preliminary review committees and investment committee meetings.

In addition, KDB is to sit in the CTF Advisory Committee (AC) in order to ensure the CTF's investment activities properly aligned with the GCF/KIB objectives which have been aimed at since the programme commenced. The AC will also be responsible for any departures from or changes to pre-agreed investment objectives, policy, and/or restrictions.
