# CTF Investment Decision Making Process

## Pre-Investment (8-5 weeks)

| Stage | Duration |
| --- | --- |
| Deal Assessment (2 weeks) | - All potential deal pipeline (from inbound/outbound) to be reviewed<br>- Deal log to be presented in weekly investment review meetings<br>- The due diligence macro, industry and company specific attributes to be reviewed. |

## Preliminary Review Committee (IC)

- A preliminary committee will be called, composed of compliance and a respective climate impact specialist, to provide an initial recommendation to investment professionals.

## Due Diligence (5-3 weeks)

- Detailed analysis on the business model, growth potential, sector, competition, founders, existing shareholders, etc.
- Rigorous modeling of projected financials to be added
  - On-site or virtual meeting with top management
  - Nth-wise research capability in Southeast Asian region
  - Review terms and conditions that relate to KDB/ICF standards, including project readiness, mainstreaming, and climate impact measurement

## Execution (2 weeks)

- Investment Approval – Investment Committee (IC) (2 weeks)
  - An IC meeting will be held to approve the deal, prepared by investment managers.
  - A. Called when investment managers come to a firm decision
  - IC responsible for investment decision making subject to approval from NH

## Risk Management Monitoring

- Quarterly review on financial, commercial, and operational performance of investments (e.g., ESG, gender, and climate impact outcomes) performance measurement, etc.
- Investment Committee will monitor the progress of investment managers to consider disposal in significant matters, among others.

To strengthen the organic connectivity of the programme and companies to ensure more effective execution. NH Investment & Securities along with NHARP Fund to establish "Team NH". This team will form an execution Task Force for Components 2 and 3, clearly defining the roles and responsibilities of participating parties. The Team NH will also be responsible for building a strong local partner network and capitalize on professional resources for the programme success including a success of the fund.

B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1200 words, approximately 2 pages)

Make It Work for NCD Countries across ASEAN: Dire Need of Investment in Climate Responsive Technologies

The report by PwC (2023) reveals a drop of investments in climate technology start-ups and ventures back to the level of five years ago. The report emphasizes that much larger amount of innovation funding is critical for the needed technologies to be scaled up, especially in NCD countries across ASEAN which are facing challenging circumstances.

Climate Technology Investment Trends (Yearly)

| Value of deals | Number of deals |
| --- | --- |
| USD80bn+ | 1,500 |
| USD60bn+ | 2,000 |
| USD40bn+ | 3,000 |
| USD20bn+ | 4,000 |

It is amply recognised by sources that investments in climate technologies are heavily concentrated in some developed markets and China (PwC, 2020). In reality, such disproportionate capital distribution is not only a matter of geography but also reflects the global power dynamics. The gap between inflows and investments in climate responsive technologies, and the gap is being further exacerbated by the unexpectedly prolonged post-pandemic emergency.

This unfortunate situation is further compounded by the global economic slowdown coupled with the geopolitical tensions that are raising interest rate hikes according to the World Bank, in its recent Global Economic Prospects report (2022), a prolonged period of soaring inflation and frail growth is expected, persisting high energy prices, and supply chain disruptions. Investors, financial institutions, governments, and enterprises are hesitant to enter into markets that have yet to recover from the utterly catastrophic pandemics, halting the planning of investments into developing and emerging markets. At all in while, the decline in venture capital funding for climate tech is evident across developed economies, and economic hardship, the recent downward trajectory of venture funding deals is evident across the globe.

# Financing Barriers Holding Back Climate Technology-enabled Solutions from Flourishing across the ASEAN Bloc

Finance poses a strong barrier in keeping technology-enabled climate solutions from reaching commercialisation. Low-carbon and climate responsive technologies are in need of not only longer investment horizons but also more stable funding to overcome the four valleys of death, which include health-related benefits, prospective energy savings, and evaded ecological harm (Pigato et al., 2020).1

One of these valleys is the Valley of Death I, which represents the period from concept to commercialisation. Unfortunately, such funding needs become more acute, chronic in fact, when technologies are vulnerable to be tracked down by the developers world wide with a potentially higher risk. A more serious issue is gasping for breath with no market traction – in other words, not commercially proven – technology-driven climate solutions, and the level of acuity has increased significantly over time.

The programme extends over three challenging facets that drive entrepreneurs forward into a corner: i.e., nearly set up JVs or partnerships with little to no track record (facet 1) on emerging markets (facet 2) for technology-intensive actors (facet 3). At the junction, GCF has an inevitable role as the world’s largest climate finance mechanism. The programme is designed to address the Valley of Death I and II, where technology-driven climate solutions are highly inefficient, but so desperately needed. The programme, by enabling entrepreneurs to overcome these two valleys, will in turn contribute to the economic stabilisation of the ASEAN region and beyond in the long run.

The Programme Shall Invite Long-Standing, Patient Funding Needed to Bridge Four Valleys of Death in Accelerating Climate Responsive Technology Business Deployment. The programme will bridge the valleys of death by providing a structured approach for entrepreneurs to navigate through technology business blossoms: (1) start-up formation; (2) product development; (3) market validation; and (4) commercialisation. The programme is designed with a 5-year plan to address the first and second valleys of death with grant proceeds via which entrepreneurs benefit from startup JV formation and market-fit product invention under a collaborative R&D8 growth platform.

## Climate Technology's Four Valleys of Death

| Valley of Death I | Valley of Death II |
|---|---|
| Startup Formation | Product Development |
| Market Validation | Commercialisation |

Rendering and Invigorating ASEAN Venture Capital Financing with Catalytic First-Loss Capital

A catalytic first-loss mechanism is a tool to render venture capital investments appealing and crowd in private equity capital but growing climate technology-themed investment opportunities. Real-world experiences reveal a commonly practiced venture capital market trajectory that private parties, such as venture capitalists, are often the last to invest in an opportunity. This trend has been pre-ordered by institutional investors of a stronger bargaining power – prior to the commencement of risk structures and terms. The GCF’s catalytic role is vital as the world's largest climate financier under the global climate architecture; no alternative distinguishes itself with catalytic finance at scale.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

## Post-Exit Sustainability: The New Development Paradigm Shift

An annex to post-exit sustainability lies in the programme's core concept itself - JV (or partnership) formation. Most of the conventional international development cooperation projects used to subcontract global technology companies and/or service providers, which often results in a lack of ownership by the recipient country. This is due to the form of service fees. In particular, one-time basis grant-funded initiatives are quite often denouncing to technical assistance and capacity building. The programme therefore devises a proposal of replicating this direct relationship with locally owned JVs as an alternative to the conventional consulting firms and technology advisory providers.

The programme's sole private investment component is Climate TechPartnership Fund (CTF) under component 3, which will bolster the confidence of ASEAN-Global JVs (or partnerships) in ensuring sustainability from contract acquisitions of technology solutions to replication or common businesses at scale. Similarities of programmes such as the CTF are that they are structured and architected with a long-term structure of the CTF under which the GP will orchestrate components to optimise support measures for business development, including but not limited to: (i) capacity building; (ii) technical assistance; (iii) auditing mechanism. The JVs will undergo additional due diligence by the GP at a transaction level to ensure that they are compliant with all relevant laws and regulations, including but not limited to, and adhere to the necessary anti-money laundering, anti-terrorism, anti-bribery, and anti-corruption, and FCPA laws, among other compliance requirements. In addition to actively engaging with each of the elements above, the JVs will also be required to provide regular updates on their progress and working opportunities for the programme's ASEAN participants and potential investors and stakeholders.

With this, the fund-backed model is designed to ensure that the JVs are sustainable beyond the life of the fund centred on a fundamental proposition that bankability is key to post-exit sustainability.

## C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer

### C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building?

Yes ☑ No ☐

### C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?

Yes ☑ No ☐

The overall objective of the programme is to support the five ASEAN countries in building capacity in order to be able to uptake climate responsive technologies from the global supply chain, self-deploying and/or co-developing them with local partners. In other words, the kernel of this proposal is technology development (customisation) and transfer. In particular, the programme takes a market-driven approach in the belief that success – i.e., self-sustainability – can be achieved only through impact-investing-oriented acceleration and consulting services.

In fact, the local markets have witnessed an oversaturated supply of early-stage start-up support services. The GCF has been working with KDB to address this issue. However, these provisions have not led to increasing investments at scale; a problematic status quo. With that in mind, the programme requests grant and equity proceeds of the GCF to deploy the funds aggregating USD 10 million. This amount will be used to support the development of a range of interacting actors, above all things, devised business models to materialise in the nascent markets. Below is an indicative list of the activities that are envisaged under this component. These are personalised for individual JV terms. Additional detail is provided in both the Demand-driven and Supply-driven Feasibility Studies of Annex 2.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section assesses the performance of projects/programmes against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's **Initial Investment Framework**.

## D1. IMPACT POTENTIAL (MAX. 500 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

### OUTCOME ESTIMATION VIA VIRTUAL DEMAND-SUPPLY TECHNOLOGY MATCHING

As specific technologies are selected for funding, their specifications will be identified after R&D (or partnership) matching – i.e., little information available on technical specifications, performance costs, and other relevant factors. The GCF's Initial Investment Framework (IIF) sets out the criteria of the CTF with the total capitalisation of USD 200 million plus the additional programme costs that support the technology selection process. The IIF also provides a framework for assessing the impacts results from ex-ante technology match between demand and supply assessments and adjusted allocation of investment volumes to virtually matched technologies. See Annexes 17, 22, 23 and 24 for detailed information on the methodology used in this assessment.

The programme will support new ventures and their technology transfer activities over 39 individual technologies across seven technology groups in five countries. The CTF aims for equal funding distribution among all seven technology groups. The total amount of USD 10 million of GCF funding will be distributed across the technology groups and individual technologies. At the same time, the programme will support new ventures through a phased approach from the concept designing and development stage to evaluate a specific portfolio of investments and technology interventions within the technology groups.

The dimensions of the sectors, measures, and ventures will not be known until R&D/D acceleration activities are completed. As such, it is difficult to predict the value of these mitigation and adaptation impacts is extremely difficult to predict at this juncture. As a result, the calculated ex-ante mitigation and adaptation impacts will be revised as more information becomes available.

The programme will support new ventures through a phased approach from the concept designing and development stage to evaluate a specific portfolio of investments and technology interventions within the technology groups.

### EX-Ante IMPACT ESTIMATES – MITIGATION

Benchmarks were identified from publicly available information, including those used in the GCF’s 2015 funding proposal or specific to Southeast Asia were selected. In some cases, only global average or project-specific benchmarks were available. An effort was made to find the most recent benchmark data available.

Sources of benchmark data include, in order of preference:

1. Benchmarks based on aggregated cost and emission impact data from multiple projects.
2. Cost curves from the US EPA, IEA, and other trade and technology associations and NGOs.
3. Marginal emissions abatement cost curves from NGOs, trade and technology associations, and similar organizations.
4. Peer-reviewed literature.
5. Documents from the implementation of individual projects or programs, including GCF funding proposals, case studies, CDM project design documents, and other similar sources.

For each technology group, two individual technologies were selected. Where possible, technologies with the broadest applicability across the five countries were selected. For each individual technology, two benchmarks were identified using the criteria described above.

In some cases, additional calculations and assumptions were required to transform the available benchmark data into the appropriate units. In other cases, additional data, such as the grid emission factors for the host countries, were needed to complete the calculations. In all cases, the assumptions used are documented in the Technical Appendices (which can be downloaded online). Wherever benchmark development required the selection from multiple values or a range of values, the most conservative impact estimates (i.e., a higher USD per tCO2e of mitigated emission) were selected.

For each technology group, an average of the benchmarks representing the selected individual technologies was calculated. The total mitigation impact was then estimated by applying a series of assumptions from the Funding Proposal about the distribution of the total funding amount in the proposal:

- It was assumed that 50% of the total funding would be distributed to mitigation technologies, with the remaining half allocated to adaptation and resilience projects. This resulted in an allocation of $100M for the CCF funding to be allocated towards the GCF adaptation result areas.
- It was also assumed that the fund would be equally shared between the host countries (20% each).
- An impact multiplier of 3.2x was applied to the mitigation funding allocation to account for the fact that the fund will take on only a partial stake in the ventures and that their capitalization will grow during the fund life.

### Ex-ante Impact Estimates – Adaptation

The methodology developed for the ex-ante estimates is a bottom-up approach based on a benchmark study of approved project examples (full proposals approved by the Green Climate Fund). The methodology includes some examples of mitigation technologies that explicitly generate adaptation benefits, and others where adaptation co-benefits are generated as part of the overall mitigation or ex-ante impact estimate. From reviewing actual financial projects, it was possible to identify a range of figures for the average cost per unit of adaptation (e.g., US$100/tonne CO2eq) and the average efficiency gain across multiple projects set to operate on a programme-level benchmark figure.

The methodology also takes into account the potential for co-benefits from adaptation (technical/venture accelerator co-benefits) and plus the venture fund capital, applied by 2.2 – the venture capital model multiplier, as explained in annex 22).

This resulted in the estimated impact—the number of direct and indirect project beneficiaries. Disaggregation into gender is not possible at this stage but could be seen as a reference point rather than a minimum target or projected ceiling, given project specific intervention and consideration of co-benefits and adaptation impacts.

The steps used to implement this method are as follows:

1. Identify a representative sample of project examples for project technologies (refer to Section III of the Fund Proposal and Annex 23 for details on technology groups and individual projects).
2. Calculate each project example's efficiency figures (for direct and indirect beneficiaries).
3. Calculate the average cost per unit of adaptation (e.g., US$100/tonne CO2eq) with adaptation technology group (or mitigation technology group with adaptation co-benefits) included in the KIDs funding proposal.
4. Calculate the number of project beneficiaries (direct and indirect beneficiaries) for all technology groups considered in the steps above (aggregate figures from steps 1-3 above).
5. Divide this figure by the total number of projects to be funded under the R&D Programme (based on a targeted programme and capital allocation of 50% to adaptation and 50% to mitigation). This gives an estimate of the average project-level ex-ante impact estimate—the number of direct and indirect beneficiaries—at the programme level.
6. Apply figures from country statistics on the percentage of female population to identify the number of female direct and indirect beneficiaries.

The document discusses a Green Climate Fund proposal for funding climate technology-led interventions (CTLIs). The model's characteristics and boundaries are outlined in Annex 22 of the spreadsheet.

### Model Boundaries

- **Adaptation Outputs**: Delivered through financed CTLI investments, including capacity building to deploy and maintain technologies.
- **Model Assumptions**:
    - Total fund investment: USD $220,000,000 (programmatic and investment capital) multiplied by the venture capital financial multiplier of 2.2.
    - Allocation between adaptation and mitigation: 50% each.
    - Country-by-country allocation: Equal distribution among five countries based on World Bank data.
- **Project Examples**:
    - Indirect beneficiaries: For project examples that did not disclose beneficiary numbers, the average difference (21%) was applied to find indirect beneficiaries.
    - Direct beneficiaries: Calculated from project examples that disclosed such information.
    - Adaptation co-benefits: Represented by adaptation technologies with significant demand according to country data.

## Innovation and Additionality Tool (IAT) Scoring Methodology with a Robust Project Selection Investment Tool Annex 21. As per the adjusted scenario simulation, the best impact scenarios are 1,138,106 CoEs and 1,180,861 direct beneficiaries (ca. 50% of female ratio). By programming the project in a way that it can be scaled up to 2 million CoEs, it will address an unexpected demand-supply match challenge, the fund's real-time risk tolerance, and investing time horizon. While paying heed to such potential discrepancies, the programme shall take three-layered approach: (i) CTF Investment Committee will review project proposals and provide guidance and acceleration support for JVs candidates to extract measurable impacts; (ii) CTF Investment Committee will monitor the progress of projects and provide feedback on lessons learned from submitted projects. Once a technology-enabled climate project is determined, the CTF will monitor invested JVs selection and performance in close collaboration with the GCF and other partners related to KDB, which will be verified by periodic site visits of KDB, both in communication with the GCF and in collaboration with executing entities and other engaging partners.

D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Paradigm Shifting Fundamental Transition Sought

The programme shall initiate, support, and drive the five countries to grapple with innovative triggered transitions towards low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathways, away from climate-damaging and highly inefficient technologies. In order to achieve this, it does intend to go beyond one-off action, ensuring a paradigm shift in the five countries’ energy sectors, through customisation, scaling, and replication of transferred technologies.

It is believed that the programme will contribute to a genuine paradigm shift in three fashions. The first and most obvious is the creation of new business models for power generation, transmission, and distribution – what it refers to, why it is important and how both public and private sectors can shape the relevant ecosystems together: In fact, consultations during the PPF implementation have already identified a number of potential opportunities in this regard. Second, such heightened awareness will allow the emergence and growth of new business models, including those that are not yet visible on the horizon, such as energy clusters, VCs with contextual expertise, in the medium to long term phase. Further, the process may give rise to new forms of collaboration between public and private sectors, as well as between ASEAN innovators, akin to that observed in Silicon Valley. Lastly, the programme will prove the verifiability of the concept of a “paradigm shift” by demonstrating how it is possible to encourage the community, including those not participating in the initiative, to move together.

### (1) Cambodia

There are barriers to education and access to information, particularly for women, due to prevailing social norms related to gender, and there is limited education in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics. This has led to a lack of understanding of climate technology and its economic, social, and environmental benefits. To harness the potential of Cambodia’s young population as innovators in climate change mitigation and adaptation technologies, greater support is needed in the field of technology transfer.

### (2) For Indonesia

The programme will kick start the advancement of traditionally under-tapped renewable energy sources, such as geothermal power, in a country with an extensive landscape. This truly remains a niche area, and thus has not garnered much attention from major investors. Thus, the programme’s investment vehicle, CTF, will revamped already attracting private sector investments to Indonesia’s renewable energy sector by moving away from the current business-as-usual carbon lock-in pathway while replacing climate-damaging technologies with low-carbon alternatives, such as geothermal power, and with alternative solutions enhanced by transferred technologies.

### (3) Laos

The programme shall support the country in developing a national-level strategic direction towards innovative, sustainable, and climate-resilient economy, with a strong emphasis on enabling the private sector to play an active role. The programme shall create successful business cases of JVs or partnerships which should be able to demonstrate how the technology can be used for economic growth, job creation, and inspire policy makers to brainstorm contextually workable solutions for a true paradigm shift.

### (4) For the Philippines

Much-needed interventions will be carried out, considering that the country has relevant institutions and passionate groups of players in the market but limited awareness on how to effectively use technology transfer as an enabler of the innovation leap. The programme’s packaged interventions will gather young and fast learning innovators, who are already active in the energy sector, and will take a holistic approach on what parts of the climate technology commercialisation value chain should be further developed.

### (5) Vietnam

Even for the emerging economy with vibrant moves amongst pioneering peers, the climate technology ecosystem still remains quite small and scattered with only a handful

## Potential for scaling up and replication: The programme's basic concept is expected to have a high potential for scaling up and replication. A successful pilot funding will pilot acceleration plus cumulated J/TVI technology transfer model. Through a successful pilot and lessons learnt from this intervention, the programme will be able to attract further funding from various sources including the governments, MDBs, climate funds, bilateral donors, etc., to scale-up the programme. The programme will also leverage funding for venture incubation/acceleration and TVI investment as well as multi-country aggregation of resources. The replication of this model in other countries that are key in devising and implementing similar programs in Africa and Latin America.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning: Introducing brand new terms and concepts, the programme will be a unique opportunity to share knowledge and learn from each other. The market-driven intervention also embraces TA activities that will contribute to national acceleration ecosystems and business ecosystem development (see Annex 4.3, for example). This grant-based supportive intervention aims to enhance the awareness of the relevant space, and promote and strengthen national/regional networks for climate technology innovation and entrepreneurship.

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment: Component 4, a grant-supported TA element, will contribute to strengthening entrepreneurial ecosystems for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The programme will also provide regulatory network frameworks that is key to acceleration, building capacities of national accelerators and incubators, as well as providing technical assistance on policy and regulatory recommendations. These activities will allow the emergence and growth of ecosystem-based innovation clusters, with a focus on South-South cooperation in ASEAN innovation clusters, and VCs with contextual expertise, in the medium to long-term phase. This will also attract the private sector and international partners to give rise to an ASEAN-unique ecosystem culture and model of shared values amongst ASEAN rooted innovators.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies: The TA component will provide policy and regulatory recommendations which are fitting into each country under Sub-Activity 4.2.3. This will contribute to creating an enabling environment and business ecosystems for climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts throughout the programme lifespan. Government officials and policy makers will actively involve through workshops, training sessions, etc., in order to co-operating a high-level inter-ministerial discussion and drafting policy recommendations.

## Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national strategies: The overall contribution of this component that will roll out of the programme will accelerate a genuine transition towards climate-resilient development pathways by strengthening national climate entrepreneurial ecosystems and by transferring

## D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Gender Co-benefits through the Emergence of Gender Mainstreamed Innovation Ecosystem with Talented Female Entrepreneurs

Besides the primary climate responsive impacts, the programme also aims to create a gender mainstreamed innovation ecosystem in the five partner countries. As becoming sustainable does not happen overnight, this ASEAN region is still at an early stage of development in terms of its technology ecosystem and market. Therefore, ensuring positive externalities are so comprehensive that it is actually beneficial for all stakeholders involved. The programme will focus on developing a technology and applied industry, and a technology generally brings about different kinds of benefits and impacts to the society. In this regard, the programme will ensure that gender mainstreamed technologies are prioritised by all the five countries as shown in Table 2. Such advanced technology-enabled agricultural projects can improve efficiency in agricultural land use by applying sustainable practices (environmental), increase income generation for smallholder farmers (economic), and provide access to markets and services for women and girls (social). Climate-affected vulnerable communities (social), increase income generation of climate vulnerable farmers (economic), and ensure the inclusion of women in the value chain such as processing and distributing benefits to women equally (gender co-benefits).

### Alignment with UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

In the overall ecosystem it is to be covered under the programme, the technology transfer that expects alignment with all of the 17 SDGs at both national and local levels. However, there are some overlaps between SDGs and co-benefits are collectively exhaustive and not mutually exclusive. Nevertheless, the programme will focus on those co-benefits that have a greater potential to contribute to overall benefit potential as well; e.g., SDG 10 (Reduced Inequalities) is excluded below, but the programme's endeavours to promote economic inclusion of all regardless of sex and ethnicity will contribute to eradicating inequality.

### Alignment with SDGs and Potential Co-benefits

| **SDG** | **Co-benefit** |
| --- | --- |
| SDG 2. Zero hunger with food security & nutrition | Emergence of women-led/owned SMEs under gendered ownership and management structure |
| SDG 3. Good health and well-being | Girls’ access to education, healthcare services, clean water and sanitation facilities |
| SDG 4. Quality education | Women’s active participation in climate change adaptation and mitigation activities |
| SDG 5. Gender equality & empowerment | Increased income amongst girls and women in gender-vulnerable areas vulnerable to climate change impacts |
| SDG 6. Clean water and sanitation | Employment in skilled technology and engineering jobs |


---
**Page 68**
---

In Pursuit of Market-oriented Sustainability – JV as a Key Agent The programme has a desire to grow its full financial and intellectual supports to grow them as pioneers with a mission of greening the economy. The joint venture (JV) or partnership will be the market-driven agent for sustainable development, activating a number of co-benefits such as local job creation, expanded role for women in society, improved access to finance, enhanced social inclusion, and better living conditions, and local resource development – e.g., in terms of human resources, raising potential for underutilized natural resources. The programme will also aim at ensuring that all the above-mentioned co-benefits can be resolved, a vibrant gender-balanced society with talented women and girls’ aspirations, and social inclusion. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

The Philippines' needs arise due to (i) absence of a centralized repository of information and indicators and data on SMEs regarding climate change adaptation and mitigation; (ii) investment in technology entrepreneurs is limited, with only 12% of the total number of firms; (iii) impact of the pandemic, community quarantine, and the resulting disruption in the supply chain; (iv) lack of access to finance for technology entrepreneurs; (v) limited support for entrepreneurs; and (vi) limited support on climate innovation activities. Filipino entrepreneurs are increasingly adopting new technologies and innovative practices throughout the programme, witnessing improving market conditions and other enabling circumstances for the climate technology innovation level.

Vietnam is in need of responding to climate-relevant natural hazards like heat waves, droughts, floods, and storms, which are depleting non-substitutable natural resources, like water, at an alarming pace. Meanwhile, bottom of the pyramid groups are disproportionately affected by these events, including those living along the Mekong Delta, the Red River, and the Central Coast. Although investment in climate action is urgent to minimize adverse impacts on vulnerable communities, there has been a lack of access to finance for technology entrepreneurs which has constituted institutional bottlenecks for climate innovation. The proposed funding gap for technology entrepreneurs will enable climate innovation to trickle benefits down to all, including the most vulnerable, in the economy.

D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Country Ownership at the Heart of the Climate Techentrepreneurship Initiative

Ensuring ownership of the five countries is at the centre of the innovative proposal which cannot materialise unless it is owned by each country. To this end, two courses of action have been adopted to strengthen the principle of country ownership and accountability: (i) national strategy and policy review; and (ii) stakeholder consultation and local ownership.

#### (1) Country Planning and Strategies Review

An extensive literature review was conducted in the course of extracting a prospective list of priority technologies by country (Table 2). In addition, the National Technology Assessment Roadmap (NTAR), Technology Needs Assessment (TNA), comprehensive studies on primary policy documents were explored to be aligned with each country's directions and priorities, but including not limited to the following:

**Cameroon**

- Rectangular Strategy IV 2018–2023
- National Strategic Development Plan 2019–2025
- Green National Growth Roadmap (2016)
- National Policy on Green Growth (2017)
- National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) 2014–2023
- National Climate Change Strategic Plan 2014–2023, and National Science, Technology and Innovation Strategy

**Indonesia**

- 2020–2024 National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN)
- GCF Country Programme Document (CPD) 2018–2025
- NDC Implementation Roadmap Summary (2019), NDC Adaptation Roadmap (2021), NDC Mitigation Roadmap (2023), and NDC Update Report (2022)
- A review on climate change references throughout the country's development plans

**Laos**

- 5th National Socio-Economic Development Plan 2021–2025 (NSEDP)
- National Strategy on Climate Change
- National Growth Strategy (2017), and
- National Adaptation Programme of Action to Climate Change (NAPAC)

## Philippines
Republic Act No.10271: Philippine Green Jobs Act of 2014; Republic Act No.9729 Climate Change Act of 2009; Republic Act No.8591: Magna Carta for Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs); Republic Act No.10364: National Climate Change Act of 2014; Republic Act No.11337: Innovation Start-up Act; and Republic Act No.11235: Philippine Innovation Act (PAI), Republic Act 10174: Sustainable Energy Development Act.

## Vietnam
National Strategy on Climate Change (2011); National Green Growth Strategy for the Implementation of the Paris Agreement (2016); National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) for the period 2012-2015; Plan for the Implementation of the Paris Agreement in Vietnam (2017); and National Strategy for the Implementation of 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, Viet Nam's National Climate Change Strategy.

## Local Ownership-centred Governance Structure as a Result of Wide-ranging Stakeholder Consultations
KDB has closely engaged with multiple stakeholders from governments, ministries, relevant public entities, state-owned enterprises, VCS investors, start-up companies, and other private sector entities. This resulted in the establishment of the SMU governance structure in partnership with the GGGI equipped with a firm local presence, since the concept designing is based on demand-driven FS of Annex 2 for consultation methods and a full list of tapped stakeholders.

Such wide-ranging stakeholder consultations resulted in the SMU governance structure that ensures strong country ownership. The NGOs of the five countries will function as members of this SMU governance structure. They will be responsible for the oversight, monitoring, and course of executing country-specific activities, while supporting partnership coordination with entities such as government agencies, private sector entities, SOEs, etc. Undoubtedly, this governance structure will ensure throughout the implementation period of the programme (see Demand-driven FS of Annex 2 for SMU governance details with information on engaging entities by country).

## D.S. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)
### GCF First-loss Funding as the Catalyst for the Scale-up of Capital in the ASEAN Market
First-loss equity capacity of the GCF is critical in enabling the CTF to meet expected results directed at catalyzing private sector investment in the region’s ASEAN markets. In the absence of the GCF's first-loss equity capital, the CTF will face difficulty in mobilizing private sector investments during a period of economic recession when investment flows have been exceptionally low. With the GCF, the world's largest climate change fund, the private sector can be secured, complementing the GCF mandate on financing climate action.

A successful mobilization with unprecedented volumes of resources will incite keen attention to the initial closing that throughout the region with few precedents. Leveraging the initial achievement, the CTF and its partners will continue to seek out innovative solutions in the region where KDB would be a catalytic financier who will re-direct investors' interest in this move to spur additional private sector investment. The GCF's first-loss equity capacity is also the most viable avenue for catalyzing the largest volume of private-oriented VC finance, thereby achieving the CTF’s goal of scaling up capital in the region. Despite the fact that KDB's investment leveraging power, the GCF will remain significant throughout the fund's lifespan. Such approach would be a key enabler to the creation and growth of regional venture investor market especially in volatile time of economic downturn and stagnation.


---
**Page 71**
---

Co-financing Efforts for the Optimal Efficiency and Effectiveness.

Institutional, corporate, and private investors are being sought to participate in the programme as limited partners. The consortium, and the GP in particular, is utilising its network and credibility as a successful general manager of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to attract potential co-investors for the Climate Technopreneurship Fund (CTF). Potential co-investors for the CTF can be compensated through: (i) equity participation in the form of shares in the CTF; terms and conditions are confirmed and fixed; (ii) global investors with previous experience in committing to funds of similar nature, who may be willing to commit to a limited partnership structure; (iii) public sector institutions across the five NDC countries; (iv) private and local enterprises that are currently operating or planning to do so in at least one of the five NDC countries; (v) public or private institutions and investors with experience in financing technology development, such as those involved in developing technology on Carbon Capture Utilization and Storage); and (vi) financial investors, impact investors, and other institutional investors that are aligned with the CTF’s investment objectives. It is anticipated that the programme will attract the majority of limited party investors after the technology providers have been identified so that the potential industries of the CTF can be better defined.

As part of the ongoing investment attraction efforts, KDB plans to organise a series of GCF-themed climate technology showcases and networking events (KDB NextRound), targeting potential investors globally and corporate venture capital firms equipped with a presentation of exemplary technologies in the five NDC countries. See below for details on the CTF’s investment strategy.

Counterfactual Baseline Comparison. At the outset, it should be emphasised that without the GCF's participation, the baseline is zero because private financiers, not to mention global technology providers, would have low levels of incentives to strengthen building innovation ecosystems in NDC countries. The World Bank Group (WBG) has observed that, in spite of the region’s growth of digital and climate technologies, there are significant gaps between the demand for investment and the ecosystem exist—e.g., lack of climate-focused start-up support intermediaries, absence of legal incentives to encourage innovation and technology, in light of this, the GCF or the CTF, with the GCF's funding support, targets to execute innovative projects that will address these gaps. The contents and structured into specific stages: spawning seed, early (Series A), and growth (Series B and above) stage opportunities for achieving a positive internal rate of return. See below.

## E.7. Monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Reporting to the GCF: As an accredited entity, KDB shall carry out monitoring, reporting, and evaluation duties to the GCF in the forms of the initial annual performance report (APR), final annual performance report (FPR), programme completion report (final APR), final independent evaluation report (IEP), and annual financial statements. The audited financial statements will be submitted through a strategic two-track governance system of Executing Entities (EGGs) for Components 1 and 4, NHIS for Component 2, and the Global Acceleration Advisory Secretariat for oversight and supervision, along with independent third party audits to ensure equity and transparency.

| Local Governance Pillar (Component 1 & 4) | Global Governance Pillar (Component 2 & 5) |
|---|---|
| National SBMU 1 | General Partner (Component 6) |
| National SBMU 2 | |

**Reporting to the GCF:** As an accredited entity, KDB shall carry out monitoring, reporting, and evaluation duties to the GCF in the forms of the initial annual performance report (APR), final annual performance report (FPR), programme completion report (final APR), final independent evaluation report (IEP), and annual financial statements. The audited financial statements will be submitted through a strategic two-track governance system of Executing Entities (EGGs) for Components 1 and 4, NHIS for Component 2, and the Global Acceleration Advisory Secretariat for oversight and supervision, along with independent third party audits to ensure equity and transparency.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 86

local offices across NCL countries and HQS are to function as important partner entities accountable for reporting necessary information to GCF. The program staff utilise its Executive Entities' strong local and global presence and capacity to undertake monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements in compliance with GCF standards. KDB's site visits, if possible with the GCF Secretariat (i.e., Portfolio Management Division), will be carried out on both an annual and a random basis, to verify reported information and confirm alleged alignment with key indicators and three-year plans.

### Financial Reporting to Investors

Reporting to KDB: The CTE is to monitor performance of invested portfolio companies and offer financial information to limited partners on a quarterly basis during the fund's lifespan. At the JV corporate level, portfolio companies are to submit reporting on business performance, climate impacts, and ESG/gender safeguards compliance as committed. Based on expertise of investment trends and insights from GP and portfolio company management, the CTE will monitor progress against targets through both routine and random manners to achieve proper planning, evaluating, and reporting milestones as planned under the programme.

### Transparent Information Disclosure of Invested Sub-Projects for GCF Stakeholders via a Systematic Reporting

For more specific details on the CTE's systematic reporting, the GP shall submit each (a) Annual Financial Statements (including audited accounts prepared in accordance with applicable financial standards and including certain information on Related Party Transactions); and (b) Quarterly reports within sixty (60) business days of each quarter date a report comprising (i) details of investments purchased and of investments disposed of and/or sold during the reporting period; (ii) details of any changes to the terms of existing investments; and (iii) details of any other significant developments in respect of investments. With the importance of proper reporting acknowledged as an essential responsibility on sub-projects to be financed and managed in a sense of transparency, the CTE will ensure that all relevant information is made available to GCF stakeholders, including NDA and line ministries. The programme is committed to provide multi-functional reports if deemed necessary in compliance with the GCF standards.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## 1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

The risks outlined below are from the programme level for the Green Climate Fund. At an operational level, each of the JV (or other partnership) candidates for the CTF will undergo a stringent risk assessment and management review, as well as technical due diligence services provided by the GCF Secretariat and/or independent consultants/representatives 2 and 3 of the programme. The JV (or partnership) candidates will review the results of assessed risk and enact appropriate mitigation measures prior to being submitted to the Investment Committee for review.

| Selected Risk Factor | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Technical and operational | High | Medium |

### Description

The programme may face planning, implementation, and execution delays due to the on-going COVID-19 pandemic, associated waves of high numbers of infections, and related travel restrictions and delays in communication and coordination.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The three mitigation measures below will bring the impact from medium to low-medium.

1. To reduce the delay and lost time in its communication with local stakeholders, KGB has early on established a strategic partnership with the GGGI, to not only mobilize each of its local offices and native staff in each of the five countries with the respective language skills and cultural background, but also to establish an extensive network within each of the countries' regional and national governments and ministries.
2. KGB has also converted many of its meetings with the programme's continuous stakeholders (e.g., local government officials, civil society organizations, NGOs, GPF, contractors, etc.) to virtual platforms (e.g., Zoom, WebEx, Google Meet, etc.) to leverage the resources available to encourage timely communication and efficiency within and across country borders and time zones. Preparations for the transition from face-to-face meetings to virtual platforms are ongoing.
3. As the pandemic continues through 2022, the programme plans to incorporate more local consultants who are already on the ground in the respective countries to conduct additional stakeholder consultations and the JV (or other partnership) candidate due diligence services for the local candidates.

| Selected Risk Factor | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | Technical and operational | High | Medium |

### Description


---
**Page 92**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 86

The programme may face difficulties in identifying appropriate technology companies to establish investments (in forms of JVs or other partnerships), given the nascent nature of low-carbon and climate-resilient solutions and the difficulty of technology commercialisation.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The three mitigation measures below will bring the impact from medium to medium-low.

1. Executing entities (esp. NHIS and HIF ARIP) will team up with global players who will support all the possible aspects of the programme, including technology innovation, leveraging a strong global network and relevant partnership foundations.
2. Coupled with relevant investment and company operating experience applicable to the fund, the GCF will be able to provide technical assistance in areas such as identifying climate sensitive companies and securing actionable pipelines. At the same time, the GGIF can strengthen the existing network by bringing in local ecosystem stakeholders across the ASEAN region.
3. A set of criteria encompassing technical feasibility and a minimum threshold of mitigation and adaptation impacts will be set up to validate appropriate candidates.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description
As the programme plans to provide tailor-made acceleration and consulting services to JV (or other partnership) candidates or JVs themselves based on individual diagnostic analysis, the executing of the acceleration services across components 1 and 2 may not meet the urgency and timeline of limited partner investor needs.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The two mitigation measures below ensure the impact remains low.

1. During initial discussions, the GCF will share with potential investors the overarching goal of the programme and the expected outcomes. If there is a clear understanding that for this programme, and ultimately the sought-after paradigm shift for the ASEAN region, to be successfully achieved, patient capital is required.
2. HIF ARIP will ensure that it has access to a diverse portfolio of companies across different growth stages to participate in the programme and CTF. The targeted allocation of investments across series, A, B, or investment types described in further detail in Annex 2 (Supply-driven FSI), is designed to provide diversity in terms of technology maturity and timeframes of return. With such endeavours, by creating the best possible climate solutions going to market, the GCF and HIF ARIP programme shall narrow the gap with timeline of limited partner investor needs as much as possible.

Selected Risk Factor 4

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 89

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

## Description

As a 11-year life investment instrument with diverse cross-sectoral investors and stakeholders, the CTF may face challenges with the fund's long-term governance in light of volatile economic conditions and complexity over the duration, diverse interests, and geographies of its investments.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The three mitigation measures below ensure the impact remains low:

1. The GP will have government mechanisms and strategies aligned with national long-term economic development plans of respective target countries, leveraging its extensive experience managing private investment vehicles for public and private institutional investors.
2. The GP will establish a robust governance framework for portfolio companies, including legal provisions, monitoring and reporting requirements of the environmental and social management unit (ESMU) in line with the GGGI's ESMU guidelines.
3. The CTF shall leverage the GGGI's solid local presence and network across the ASEAN region.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

## Description

The programme presents challenges associated with the investment portfolio over limited access to information and potential miscommunication by portfolio companies, as well as difficulty of attracting follow-on investments.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The three mitigation measures below ensure the impact remains low:

1. The GP will establish communication channels with the investment portfolio to implement regular reporting practices in mitigating risks related to insufficient and/or misleading information provision.
2. The GP will assist the investment portfolio with its sustainability guidelines to guide them towards addressing environmental, social, and governance issues.
3. In close communication with the GP, the SMUs (GGGI) and the Team NH (centred on GGGI) will offer technical assistance in areas of expertise to ensure the programme success - not just for their own components: a local presence and stable network with key local partners for the GGGI; and diplomatic actions as a government agency.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 90

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Given the likely structure of the CIF in Singapore and investment flow into and out of the respective programme countries with different local currencies, the impact of foreign exchange will primarily be upon repatriation in the form of dividends or upon exit. After the GCF Board approval, the programme in the medium term will have access to a range of financial models which can include local currency to USD long-term swap rates, for instance. While using these rates could provide adequate comfort that project financiers are able to cope with any potential future exchange rate fluctuations, the final decision on the type of financial model is proposed after a customized due diligence at GCF’s own discretion. This ensures the impact remains low.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MILET | Low | Low |

Description
Risks associated with money laundering, terrorist financing, or other prohibited practices, throughout the process of mobilising and transferring funds via multiple layers of partner entities with weaker anti-money laundering controls and a lack of visibility.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The three mitigation measures below ensure the impact remains low:

1. Given KDBL equipped with a strong internal compliance system under a strict regular guidance of the Korean Financial Supervisory Service (FSS), it is ensured that all investments made by the programme adhere to the GCF’s Anti-Money Laundering (AML) and Countering the Financing of Terrorism (CFT) Policy in place, as well as other compliance mechanisms to mitigate the risk of money laundering. All partner entities and their respective subsidiaries and/or affiliated entities are subject to the same policies as well.
2. The CIF will adopt a best practice governance mechanism by undertaking investments only after thorough screening and due diligence that include AML/CFT and Know Your Customer (KYC) procedures to prohibit the consideration of an investment with potential and present money laundering risks, including those listed on the UN Security Council’s watch lists. Above all, strictest attention will be paid when it comes to investment countries in the Green List.
3. Lastly, agreements will be made with each partner entity that sources of capital for their investments into the CIF do not come from any prohibited source.

The Programme has developed its own Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS), encompassing both CTF and non-CTF components. CTF component purpuses maximisation of social benefits and minimisation of adverse environmental impacts. Non-CTF components (categories 2, 3, 4) are extension of the fund’s environmental and social (E&S) risk management and due diligence. As such, no new E&S risks will be created by the Programme. The ESMS is designed to ensure that CTF projects as they will reduce reaction rates in the CTF approval stage in Component 3. Non-CTF components also aim at ensuring that the programme meets its own E&S performance management framework regardless of the fund application results. Importantly, ESMS of the programme is aligned with the revised Environmental and Social Policy of the GCF (Adopted in BIM-2018/16).

In case a specific issue poses potentially high risks to investors and host countries, necessarily involving vulnerable groups such as the indigenous communities, the deprived, and women and girls, the EAS Risk Management Committee (ESRMC) will be convened ad hoc, inviting the representatives of the stakeholders involved. The ESRMC shall review the matter in accordance with the provisions of the ESMF Covenant, so that the CTF is required to consider the cessation or cancellation of investment agreement, the CTF senior management shall be activated to address the concerning red flags. In the latter case, an exit strategy will be early developed and implemented.

The programme is organized as a fit combination of: (i) preparatory stages for the CTF application (Components 1 and 2); and (ii) CTF investment Component 3 from (approval, approval, implementation of the project, and monitoring). The programme is designed to support simultaneous or parallel activities on the ground for local ecosystem capacity building (Component 4).

Accelerating ESS Capacity Enhancement of Shortlisted JVs in Investment Readiness. Given that most of the local applicant companies and some of global investors might not be properly equipped with a strong understanding of ESMF, the CTF will provide technical assistance to the shortlisted JVs at the JV level entities in strengthening their preliminary ESMs and climate impact status by providing diagnostic-based advisory support. In addition, upon request, the applicant JV have access to readiness-stage ESS, which is provided through a combination of: (i) capacity building workshops; (ii) technical assistance; and (iii) advisory services as part of non-CTF components are extension of the fund’s E&S risk management and investment strategy. The CTF will also provide support in developing an integrated approach for generating co-benefits of capacitating the candidate JVs in ESMs management performance, regardless of the fund application results.

Information Disclosure & Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM). For Category B projects, PAs may be required to develop a separate stakeholder engagement and commitment plan (SECIP) including the information disclosure plan in accordance with the GCF IDP and ESMF: the frequency and level of disclosures will be determined by the nature of the project and the needs of the EAS/ESMF. The SECIP is tuned by the CTF E&S and Gender Compliance Team’s (E&G) review, consultation, and capacity building support. Also, all of the JVs are required to develop and operate the GRM in accordance with the GCF IDP and ESMF. The GRM will be reviewed and approved by the CTF E&S and Gender Compliance Team. A high-grievance will be reported to the E&S Risk Management Committee for their attention, and an expert panel will be convened to provide recommendations on how to address the grievance.

Depending on the nature of grievances and findings of the result (audit or internal investigation), the fund’s decision may be disclosed to the public through a website and other accessible means to country stakeholders for the sake of transparency and accountability.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 93

## Gender Benefits
Gender benefits of the proposed programme have two prongs: On one hand, the very act of investing in women entrepreneurs and female-led enterprises will reduce their economic vulnerability and enhancing their resilience. Another prong is the programme design (through gender mainstreaming) that ensures that all projects are implemented with a gender lens (see Table 3); only JVs or partnerships screened by the CTF Gender Compliance Team shall grasp the investment opportunities at scale.

As per the CTF investment criteria, the programme deliberately targets female technology leaders and persons in leadership positions. Further, headed or more than 50% employees being women firms as prioritised beneficiaries of the CTF funding. Selection of the local candidate firms for the CTF will target at least 30% of the portfolio companies to be headed by women. The programme also targets that at least 50% of the fund portfolio companies become either gender-friendly or committed to establish and fulfil their own gender mainstreaming action plans as an investment agreement condition (outcome statement of the programme). This is in line with the CTF’s objective of creating a conducive environment for supporting the creation of enriched gender-inclusive tech-driven entrepreneurship ecosystem.

## Integrating Gender Considerations into the Entire Programme
Motivated by the above, and to ensure that the programme is truly gender-sensitive from its inception, the programme has been designed, advanced, and sophisticated with a strategic gender focus, driven by a team of KSB, GGCI, and external gender experts. It has resulted in programme components and activities being gender mainstreamed at all levels, including but not limited to: (i) encouraging their proactive engagement surrounded by the region’s innovation leap; e.g., under consideration are the inclusion of female students in STEM subjects, recognising realistic constraints of forming women-led SMEs (JVs) in component 2 as a result of feasibility studies.

Above all things, the programme plans the establishment and operation of an independent EAS and Gender Advisor to ensure that the best gender risk management mechanisms in close communication with ESS experts, on which the climate technology acceleration initiative will be excellently operational with female leaders accounting for 50% of the programme beneficiaries and creation of lots of women-led SMEs (JVs) comprising at least five countries, now.

## G.3 Financial Management and procurement
509 words approximately | page

### Adequate Financial Management at the Programme’s Core
As a legally authorised accredited entity under the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) effective since pre-accreditation, KSB will supervise and oversee the whole capital flow of the programme, pursuant to the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) to be made with each project. The FAA umbrella setting and control at the contracting parties, structure of multiple legal and contractual arrangements as depicted in the Capital Flow with Legal and Contractual Arrangements of Section B, is to be able to direct the alignment of the FAA umbrella setting and control at the contracting parties. Meanwhile, KSB shall receive, channel, hold, administer, monitor, review,


---
**Page 98**
---

and record KDB’s proceeds in an independent GCF account in accordance with the AMA and F&A, so as to avoid a mix of investments sourced from KDB or third parties.

In addition to KDB's overarching financial oversight and supervision at a programme level, the programme builds partnership arrangements with global/local investors through intermediation of the PPF team, and will ensure that all transactions are conducted in accordance with the GCF procurement guidelines. All sources of capital may come from countries in the Grey List and prohibited sources under AML/CFT regulations. The GGGI and KDB have entered into a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) and related covenants, with the executing entities. In particular, the programme has endeavoured a tie with local partners to ensure that all transactions are conducted in accordance with the GCF procurement guidelines and the proceeds at a project level. Designated local partner entities will function as safety nets to address conventional financiers' heightened concerns over traditional lack of transparent capital flows due to international sanctions and other restrictions, and will be responsible for ensuring that funds reach receiving countries. Legal or contractual agreements to enter into between the GGGI and local acceleration partners are subject to review by KDB’s legal counsel. The programme is committed to maintaining a transparent and accountable management of financial resources in compliance with the FAA, in terms of information not limited to, information disclosure, reporting, anti-money laundering, and counter-terrorism financing.

Executing entities will follow KDB's guidance for financial management. In fact, KDB once had operated and managed the GCF Project Preparation Facility (PPF) proceeds in partnership with GGGI and its global/local partners. The PPF was subject to an independent audit by a reputable international auditor and transparently confirmed with a clearance from the GCF Secretariat. In short, as for the team-up, such operations are subject to KDB’s guidance and oversight. The PPF team (and its local counterparts) (and its constituent members), and the gap team all are equipped with an internal oversight mechanism that is in line with KDB’s policies and procedures. The programme will also provide technical support system to maintain various financial transactions in different forms in line with the global standard, which is transparently displayed under several review mechanisms by multiple supervisory layers.

**Procurement Guidance:** Adhere to KDB’s GCF Procurement Policies. Please be Aligned with the GCF Standard. Upon launch of the PPF in July 2021, KDB will issue a request for proposals (RFP) to receive competitive bid offers. The two-month long open tender process was carried out in compliance with the GCF procurement guidelines and the GGGI’s internal policies. The RFPs were reviewed by an independent bank under scrutiny by oversight authorities as well as civil parties, which abide by the GCF procurement guidance re-conformed by the GCF re-accreditation assessment in 2021-2022. The contract was awarded to a consortium of leading financial institutions and international development partners with technical and sustainability experts, whose bid met the requirements in the RFP; in addition, NH ARPP was assessed and certified as per KDB’s internal guidelines.

The programme will procure services of the pre-selected consortium members along with the GGGI and its global/local partner entities. As to country-specific selection of local partner entities, during the PPF phase, the GGGI advertised terms of reference (ToR) as per procurement policy, approved by the GGGI Board, which was subject to a public consultation and internal review. The ToRs were also subject to consultation. In rolling out activities on advancing climate technology business and the respective execution partners, the programme will procure services of the pre-selected consortium members exclusively procured upon the discretion of the pre-selected consortium members as per KDB guidance, to facilitate the programme execution. For instance, in case technical expertise on a specific climate technology (e.g., weather forecasting) is required for the project implementation, and such expertise is not available through process, advisory services from specialised technical agencies may be procured on an ad hoc basis.

G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme proposals are subject to disclosure unless they contain confidential information as defined by the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is only to be disclosed if it can be shown that such disclosure would not prejudice or harm the document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF; no information is being provided in confidence.
- With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full and must be redacted to protect confidentiality. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of how each portion was marked and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy; and
  - Redacted copy to be published on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Very truly yours,

BENJAMIN E. DIOKNO
Secretary of Finance
