# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP240

## Summary

### Brief on the programme
This proposal is a resubmission from the thirty-seventh meeting of the Board (R37). The Collaborative R&D&B Programme for Promoting the Innovation of Climate Techntrepreneurship targets the transfer of innovative climate techntrepreneurship acceleration ecosystems in five member states of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations: Cambodia, Indonesia, Lao People's Democratic Republic (LPR), Philippines (Phil.), Viet Nam.

This research and development bank (R&D&B) programme will globalize an incubation and acceleration programme for global climate techntrepreneurs. The programme aims to establish a c. USD 201.2 million Climate Techntrepreneurship Fund (CTF) to be managed by the Asian Development Bank (ADB). It is funded by various technical assistance initiatives targeted at building capacity on the ground, including policy support to enable technology transfer and commercialization of climate techntrepreneurs and incubate local companies to enable them to receive and scale up their innovations in the five member states.

The programme is visionary in its attempt to showcase a comprehensive intervention to facilitate climate technology innovation and commercialization as part of the mandate of the Conference of the Parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change secretariat (COP) to the UNFCCC) in promoting the uptake of clean technologies available to entrepreneurs in South-East Asia.

### Targeted programme size
USD 226.7 million

### Proposed GCF involvement
USD 104.47 million (USD 83.75 million in equity; USD 20.72 million in grants)


---
**Page 111**
---

Recipient of GCF assistance

For the equity component, which will create a tech-entrepreneurship fund, the finance will flow directly to the Climate Tech-entrepreneurship Fund through the Korea Development Bank (KDB). KDB will be the recipient of the grants and will distribute the funds to the executing entities (EEs) and local partners responsible for delivering the technical-assistance activities.

The programme has four components which will be managed by different EEs.


| Component | Executing entity | Funding (USD) | Type of source funding (USD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Country-driven acceleration funding | KDB, Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI) | 8.6 million | Grant |
| 2 | Global acceleration for collaborative R&D | NH Investment & Securities (NIIS) | 9.5 million | Grant |
| 3 | Climate Tech Fund (CTF) | NI Absolute Return Partners (NH ARB) | 201.2 million | Equity |
| 4 | Technical assistance & project management costs | KDB, GGGI | 7.5 million | Grant |
| **Total** | **226.8 million** | **201.2 million (equity)** | **25.5 million (grant)** |

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Impact potential
Scale: Medium

The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) recognizes and welcomes KDB taking its first step in responding to the call by the UNFCCC to promote technology transfers in developing countries. This is especially impressive as KDB has been a leader on this issue globally and has played a significant role in technology transfer from and to the Republic of Korea.

2. Given the call of the COP to the UNFCCC and the role of the Republic of Korea in the successful transfer of technology for both the Republic of Korea and countries worldwide, this is a welcome step.
3. The expected impact target of the programme will also be received: (i) mitigation (avoiding 10 million tonnes CO2 equivalent; and (ii) adaptation targeting 74,035,687 direct and indirect beneficiaries over the lifespan of the programme, with 78 per cent of the funding expected to go towards adaptation


---
**Page 112**
---

measures by 22 per cent towards mitigation. The funding proposal states that 50 per cent of the beneficiaries of the proposed programme are assumed to be women.

4. The ITAP notes that this programme aims to be a market-making intervention, which, by its very nature, means causality is difficult to prove in terms of climate-impact outcomes. Therefore, the ITAP considers the paradigm shift potential of this programme to be its most significant factor.

The ITAP further notes that since 2013, the programme has become more focused owing to its prioritization of sectors for each country. The funding proposal includes a table listing 7 eligible prioritized technology needs and 35 technologies that can address these needs. The ITAP notes that the General Partnership (GP) document writes the additional work performed by NHR ARB, the General Partner (GP), in targeting technology needs, which will increase the relevance of the programme to each country and increases the chances of success.

6. A more tailored financing approach according to the different markets, and the different types of competition between technologies within only two or three technologies in each market, will have a higher chance of success.

The programme is complex in both its design and execution; but (i) has significantly improved the long-term alignment of the executing entity, NHR ARB, and its parent company, MHS (the limited partner [LP]), through respective contribution targets to the CTF of US$ 10 million and US$ 25 million.

More specifically, in terms of complexity, multiple interfaces existed in the proposed programme between the executing entities which have never worked together before, and which have very different operating statements. This is a natural result of each entity’s experience – this naturally raises execution concerns. Such concerns are not insurmountable; however, they must be managed carefully to ensure that the work will be managed. The plan of the GP is to have multiple coordinating management units between the executing entities and the LP, between the executing entities and between the investment trusts and government. Some level of coordination is important in order to avoid duplication of effort and to ensure that the challenges recognized in this programme between the EEs seems to arise from a failure of design. Component 1, as the lead entity, will be responsible for the overall management of the EE and housed within the GP, which can then directly hire/partner with local and international incubators in different countries and in different sectors, or build its own internal coordination unit.

An important indicator of alignment of interest is to have at least one of the EEs having at risk capital from their proposed programme arrangements. In order for the EE to understand the complexity of the programme and the challenges ahead, the EE will have enough capital at risk that it will put the right resources and time into fitting which will ensure that the EE can deliver on its commitments in a timely manner.

The ITAP welcomes the intention of the GP to contribute at least USD 2 million to the CTF and for the interest of the parent company, MHS, to invest up to USD 10 million as an LP.

8. Execution risk is time and timelines

(a) The EE will not overestimate efforts for achieving 40 investments and entering these within 11 + 3 years. Considering that day 0 is GP approval, it would be followed by fundraising and building out the team (1-2 years); identification of both global technology needs and local technology needs (1 year); engagement with relevant to local government regulation (1-5 years); identification, incubation and acceleration of successful technologies (3+ years); scaling up of investment (1-4 years); scale up of investment (4 years), and then exit (2-3 years) – a total of 12-22 years. Some


---
**Page 113**
---

2.2 Paradigm shift potential

* Technology transfer towards developing countries is a visionary goal and the IFAP congratulates the AE on its high aspirations. However, one of the main barriers identified for successful technology transfer is the lack of clarity regarding intellectual property rights in licensing frameworks.
* The solution proposed is to make this framework compulsory. But it is not clear who will make it compulsory and who will enforce it. It remains unclear to the IFAP how such a compulsory framework would be implemented, what the penalties would be for non-compliance, or whether it would make it compulsory for the respective governments.
* It is also unclear how such a framework would be enforced in practice. The IFAP suggests that technology providers should be assured that their intellectual property would be protected.

In addition, it is unclear how the necessary local licensing and regulatory changes would happen if the proposed framework were to be made compulsory. This could lead to significant changes.
* The IFAP acknowledges that the funding proposal package development has relied heavily on national governments to provide the necessary enabling environment for technology transfer. However, there seems very generic: everyone is in favour of the transfer of technology in principle, but there is no information provided about how this would be implemented or what specific terms and conditions their licensing requirements would be adopted by the governments of the participating countries.

Ultimately, the programme may face difficulties in identifying appropriate technology companies to support. This is because the IFAP has not yet identified any such companies. Given the fact that low-carbon and climate-resilient solutions are in the early stages of development and the complexity involved with them.
* Notwithstanding the lack of specifics, the IFAP commends the addition of three different modalities for accelerating technology transfer in South-East Asian countries, which could be more appropriate for different stages of the target companies and countries.

2.3 Sustainable development potential

Creating an ecosystem to support the acceleration and incubation programme will spur the emergence of local climate technology companies. The programme aims to provide support for 60 locally owned and registered climate technology entrepreneurs, and it has potential to create a wider range of employment opportunities in the local markets.


2.3.1 Social and environmental co-benefits

The programme will focus on nurturing and supporting the creation of an enriched gender-inclusive technology-driven entrepreneurship ecosystem. At least five local gender experts are expected to be involved in the programme, which is expected to help generate greater gender expertise on the ground.
* The programme will target female technology leaders and gender-focused entrepreneurs who have a strong track record or potential as climate technology candidates. This has the potential to increase women’s participation in the climate technology industry.


---
**Page 114**
---

2.4 Needs of the recipient

* The needs of the recipients have been very thoroughly researched but not necessarily assessed in a comparative manner. A number of studies and analyses (e.g., those in the funding proposal) are broad and merely conclude that the needs of the five participating countries are classified into the following three overall categories (although the needs vary by country): (i) building capacity for climate change adaptation; (ii) strengthening immature climate technology and innovation ecosystem.

* In many cases, particularly where the technology transfer is complex or fragmented, it remains unclear whether technology transfer is the right solution for many of the small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in most of the countries targeted. Many of the SMEs may benefit more from climate financing programmes that are more directly aligned with sophisticated and complex technology transfer agreements in order to access finance.

* The programme will support a range of grant-financed activities for the five different countries, concentrating on: (i) establishing a regulatory framework tailored to climate technologies; (ii) addressing the financing gap through crowding in private sector investment into climate technologies; and (iii) strengthening the innovation ecosystem by enhancing awareness and providing capacity-building assistance.

* Cambodia: The corporate structure and business ecosystems of Cambodia is oriented around SMEs, which account for 95 per cent of the country's employment, 99.8 per cent of its companies and 58 per cent of its gross domestic product. There is a weak institutional framework in place to support climate change adaptation efforts, although have faced challenges, including a lack of skilled human resources and poor access to finance. The programme will focus on the middle "backbone group" of SMEs, which are too high risk to be attractive to global investors but can be supported through programmes in various ways, such as training local talent to make full use of technology.

* Indonesia: Following the unprecedented challenges posed by the coronavirus disease (COVID-19) pandemic and the economic downturn that followed, only 20 per cent in 2020 only 13 per cent of the financing needs outlined in the nationally determined contributions (NDCs) were met. The programme will focus on strengthening private sector engagement with investment to crowd in and assist with climate technology deployment is essential for Indonesia to meet the commitments made in its NDC.

* Lao People's Democratic Republic: The country is seeking to improve the overall domestic capacity of its institutions, which have been weakened by a number of factors that have undermined the country’s long-term development, exacerbated by climate change and the COVID-19 crisis. The Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic is committed to improving the institutional framework for climate change adaptation through strengthening regulatory and institutional arrangements. The programme will assist in the government's efforts to improve its capacity to manage climate change risks, enhance trade at the corporate level, and deriving an innovation-driven management framework for the country’s existing resources at the institutional level.


---
**Page 115**
---

### Philippines

The Philippines' needs arise as a result of (i) the absence of a centralized repository of information and indicators and data on SMI regarding climate-change adaptation and mitigation; (ii) investment gaps; (iii) the limited availability of capacity-building resources for climate change adaptation and mitigation; (iv) the COVID-19 pandemic, community quarantine, and the resulting disruption in the supply chain and distribution of goods and services to small-scale farmers, fishers, artisans, and microentrepreneurs; and (v) limited support on climate innovation activities. The country's enterprise development programme will address these needs by providing support throughout the programme, with the aim of improving market conditions and other enabling circumstances for the climate technology innovation leap.

### Wetlands

Wetland ecosystems are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as heatwaves, droughts and floods, which accelerate the overexploitation of non-renewable natural resources, such as water, at a faster rate than possible. Meanwhile, people who are at the frontline of these changes are often the most vulnerable and exposed to risks; primarily those residing in the Mekong River Delta, the Red River and the Central Highlands of Vietnam. The programme will support the development of tools to minimize adverse climate impacts, the limited availability of climate-responsive technologies, and institutional bottlenecks for climate business deployment. The programme will therefore aim to ensure that these so-benefits will permeate down to all, including the most vulnerable in the economy.

## 2.5 Country ownership

### Scale: High

The FIP would like to highlight the expansive country ownership plans of the AE.

- Country-specific analyses have been conducted through an extensive literature review, covering each country's NDC and technology needs assessment, as well as other national policies and strategies related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- A number of strategic early programme reviews have been conducted, including two feasibility studies (technology supply-driven) and a stakeholder engagement plan and a gender assessment have been completed.

The AE has held extensive stakeholder consultations with the government, ministries, relevant agencies, civil society organizations, private sector representatives, start-up assisting service firms, and international donors as well as civil society organizations since the concept design stage. The wide-ranging stakeholder comments resulted in the creation of an SME programme steering committee to offer high-level policy guidance and strategic advice over the course of executing country-specific activities and will support partnership coordination among the key stakeholders.

## 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium

The estimated cost per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent for the total requested GCF amount dedicated to mitigation (24% of the total GCF request) of USD 22.9 million (including co-investment ratio).

- GCF funding could catalyze private investments of USD 120.75 million from KDB, general partners, limited partners, Korea International Cooperation Agency, Asia-Europe Meeting Secretariat, and other potential technical intermediary entities.
- Overall, the co-investment ratio on the total GCF investment is 1:3.16.


---
**Page 116**
---

20. The mobilization of investors is difficult to assess given the fundraising risk associated with a GP which manages a similar fund of USD 15 million and whose capital came mainly from its parent company (i.e., the GP does not have significant experience fundraising from outside sources). This risk can be mitigated by having the GP commit to making regular disbursements in line with fundraising milestones, which mitigates fundraising risk for competitors (i.e., other GGGs).

21. However, there is no similar tranching of the grant capital awarded to Component 1 or any performance-based milestone that the executing entity (GGG) needs to meet to access further GGG funding.

22. The upstream work of component 1 is essential for the creation of the appropriate pipeline for the CTP. According to the information provided by GGDD within the written justification, the GGGs will be responsible for sourcing local companies together with top-tier local VC/Private equity firms with abundant project pipelines. This is a key difference from the current model where the executing entities - GGG and KOS - do not include enough investable companies for CTP, while private equity firms are not included in the pipeline. The GGGs will be responsible for sourcing and that they are mitigating this risk by creating coordinating committees to facilitate the multiple interfaces. GGDD itself will be the main coordinator and GGDD the executor of the pipeline: building a strong network of local companies and VCs, which is essential as the number increases the likelihood of execution risk down the line. Greater accountability upfront needed on the GGGs’ part in terms of their ability to source companies for inclusion into CTP and its GP, with more power being given to the GP to define and agree terms for the roles of other components within the pipeline. This will also be a key area where GGDD will assess the ability of the GP to replace such entities if they do not perform.

23. The TAP welcomes the involvement of GGGs in this proposed goal as its extensive work with target countries has provided it with valuable experience and cooperation. GGDD has a strong track record of driving legislative, administrative, and regulatory change in diverse developing country contexts. In addition to the TAP’s experience, GGGs have also been involved in countries that adopted green Asia, Africa, Latin America and the Caribbean, Middle East and North Africa, and Central Asia. The TAP has worked with a number of these countries and some states with key ministries (including national designated authorities in the programme's target country) as the proven delivery partner for the GGG Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme. This supports the target governments’ capacity, including the regulatory set-up and policy design.

24. However, GGGs have limited experience of scaling up their own companies, which scale up and raise substantial capital to be able to meet the enormous climate challenges of the target countries. GGDD has an average annual total budget of USD 100 million (only a fraction of this is spent on scaling up companies). The TAP has not had access to sufficient additional capital into the companies it has trained and incubated. Although the TAP achieved some success in terms of time to market, it was difficult for the TAP to evaluate GGDD’s performance.

25. NHGGS and NHF ARF are the GP of CTP on the other hand, have such experience and are the right partners to assess what their pipeline should look like. Rather than go through an intermediary, NHGGS and NHF ARF can hire directly flexible, country-specific incubators with a proven track record in scaling up companies. They also have their own incubation team. Moreover, it would be more cost and time efficient, outcome targeted, and specialized for the CTP. The TAP has worked with a number of successful venture capital company such as GGDD with large overhead costs, and without strong track record in choosing and scaling local venture companies which become commercially successful.


---
**Page 117**
---

### III. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

10. Lastly, having the EE of the incubation of the pipeline (Component I) as a different entity to the main fund (CTF) [component II] as an pre-ordained step by KfW may be too inflexible. These two EEs have never worked together, they have different commercial orientations, requiring them to be separate entities. This will require a new set of governance rules and costly coordination investment strategies which will change and adapt with time, requiring unnecessary complexity in the programme structure. The EE of Component I is also more expensive than anticipated, which undoubtedly increased the level of GCF capital/fees required to run this program. This decrease in efficiency has been estimated at USD 15 million.

12. The proposed KfW programme has been one of the most ambitious programmes the ITAP has evaluated and the ITAP commends KfW for the programme's most challenging and inspiring approach. The ITAP is therefore recommending that the GCF Board approve the revised proposal for funding at B36, which makes this a solid proposal.

15. The ITAP commends the AEF for improving the programme since its initial review during the field mission in 2017. This includes the inclusion of a new component (Component I) and a number of sectors and most importantly the engagement of a fund manager (NH ARF) with relevant expertise to manage the programme. The ITAP recommends that the GCF Board approve funding at USD 15 million as opposed to the current proposed fund (CTF) of USD 200 million.

16. Despite the above-mentioned welcome improvements, there are significant items from the KfW proposal which need further clarification and/or improvement in terms of the programme's execution and, especially, the accountability of its executing entities is particularly important for ensuring that the programme will be successful and contribute to the success of technology-transfer initiatives.

17. The programme is highly complex and high risk with multiple executing entities which have to work together— all told together as one AE whose main role will be coordinating a complex and expensive institutional set-up which could have been simplified by creating a single entity for the entire programme. The ITAP recommends that KfW consider hiring the support they need directly & flexibly, rather than having GGF be an intermediary. In this regard, the ITAP commends the AEF for its willingness to hire NH ARF as a fund manager. To mitigate this concern, we have outlined a recommended condition below, which attempts to address potential non-performance issues and ensure that the programme achieves its funding performance milestones put on NH ARF by GCF as a discrete capital based on fundraising milestones.

18. The ITAP's additional condition would similarly ensure that the incubation capital is invested in a timely manner and that the pipeline of projects is developed at an accelerated percentage (30%) of investable companies for CTF, evaluated every two years. It aims to ensure that the EE of Component I is not delayed or put on hold until the GCF has committed its full capital.

19. In summary, such a vision's concept needs to marry complexity and innovation in goals on the one hand with simplicity, focus and deep expertise in implementation to mitigate execution risks. The programme must be able to attract a diverse range of investors, including private sector entities, and the varied profile of the companies within each market, the broad spectrum of technologies and sectors, and the wide variety of countries where the technology will be deployed. It is recommended, and the multitude of executing and advisory agencies to the programme highlight a high execution risk which could undermine its promise in terms of funding, investment execution and achieving the stated climate outcomes.


---
**Page 118**
---

## As such, the ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal conditional

### (a) Condition to be met prior to the first Disbursement of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds in respect of Component 1:

Delivery to GCF by the Accredited Entity, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of evidence that the amount of disbursement requested is not greater than the amount of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds budgeted for Component 1 of the Funded Activity.

### (b) Condition to be met prior to the second Disbursement of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds in respect of Component 1:

Delivery to GCF by the Accredited Entity, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of evidence that the amount of disbursement requested is not greater than the amount of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds budgeted for Component 1 of the Funded Activity.

### (c) Condition to be met prior to the third Disbursement of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds in respect of Component 1:

Delivery to GCF by the Accredited Entity, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of evidence that 30% of the beneficiaries under Component 1 (as defined for the purposes of this funding proposal) have received a Disbursement.

### (d) Condition to be met prior to the fourth Disbursement of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds in respect of Component 1:

The following covenant is included in the Funded Activity Agreement: If the Accredited Entity has not satisfied the conditions for second or third Disbursement of GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds in respect of Component 1, respectively, within two (2) years of the date of the previous disbursement, and if the amount of the requested disbursement is greater than the amount budgeted for Component 1, it shall consult with the GCF Secretariat and submit a restructuring proposal in respect of Component 1.

# Introduction

## Programme Overview

The Collaborative R&D8B Programme for Promoting the Innovation of Climate Technopreneurship is a multi-country project aiming to support entrepreneurs with climate technology-enabled solutions that will accelerate the transition towards low-carbon and resilient transformation in developing countries, underpinned by the success of pilots in Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, the Philippines and Vietnam. Comprised of four key components, the Programme is structured as follows: Component 1 (Climate Technopreneurship) and Component 4 (Technical Assistance) focus on acceleration readiness and the ecosystem for local climate technology firms, respectively; while Components 2 (Innovation Acceleration) and Component 3 (Climate Technopreneurship Fund, CTF hereafter) are oriented more towards investment-ready JVs. (For more details of the proposed Programme, see the Funding Proposal.)

## Gender Assessment Objectives

This gender assessment aims to provide a rapid assessment of the gender situation in Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, the Philippines and Vietnam, identifying gender equality and women’s empowerment constraints, opportunities and recommendations to ensure gender inclusiveness and gender empowerment. The assessment was carried out by the relevant national legal and institutional framework. Through the desk reviews on the existing policies and strategies related to gender equality and women's empowerment (GKE) and the GGGI (EE of the Component 1 and Component 4), representatives of NGOs and NFPs, as well as key stakeholders in the private sector were interviewed. The assessment was conducted by the Gender Advisory Consortium Group for Component 2 of the Programme/. The general situation of gender equality, gender policies and strategies, and the enabling environment for women's entrepreneurship (or generally business entrepreneurship), particularly of the women were examined. This assessment also presents the gender landscape of identified priority socio-technologies that are likely to be funded through the CTF, with a focus on promoting gender equality and empowerment, as well as promising intervention points through the Programme design.

This assessment also informed the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the Programme. GATs in the GAP will be developed to ensure the mainstreaming the overall execution of the Programme in accordance with the GCT Gender Policy 2019.

## Key findings

In a nutshell, the report finds that women and girls (e.g., in remote rural areas) in all the five target countries—Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, the Philippines and Vietnam—are more vulnerable to climate change, disproportionately affected and, to a varying degree among the countries, largely isolated from the benefits of climate adaptation. The findings also indicate that, while there is potential for innovative scale-up of enterprises related to climate adaptation and mitigation technologies will benefit women as much as men, if not higher, as they are more vulnerable to climate change-related challenges and risks, and have a greater need for access to climate-smart solutions. For example, women’s roles in agriculture, where female farmers are a major (although mostly unrecognized and informal) workforce, are likely to be significantly impacted by climate change, and their livelihoods could be substantially improved through the Programme (such as water supply and waste management, climate-resilient seeds, etc.).

In all five countries, the women’s entrepreneurial ecosystem is limited to small and microenterprises. Compared with their male counterparts, women-led businesses and enterprises are often characterized by lower profitability and contribution in majority informal sectors. Access to formal credit systems is


---
**Page 124**
---

often limited due to the respective countries' social and cultural norms and (in some locations) religious precepts. In some countries, STEM education often favors male students and technology-based entrepreneurship is not encouraged for women.

Nevertheless, there are signs of improvements: In Vietnam and the Philippines, more women are taking ICT and engineer careers, which had been dominated by men. In Lao PDR, women are leading agents for ASANM projects. In Cambodia, women have become leaders in microfinance. In Indonesia, women participate in the agricultural sector, whose rich know-hows and experiences need to be tapped for gender mainstreaming and entrepreneurship in agricultural sectors.

**Recommended Action Plan**

The Programme has an explicit goal of proactively inviting women climate technology and business entrepreneurs in the five countries and ensuring the funded IV activities shall clearly mainstream gender into their implementation process in line with the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of this Programme (Chapter 3 of this report).

The following actions were integrated into the designs of the Programme:

1. **Gender Component in CTF’s Investment Criteria**

**[CTF-funded Enterprise dimension]**

All the EIP investment/activities are requested to develop concrete gender performance indicators and generate tangible gender-related results.

The minimum requirement for CTF-supported IV enterprises are:

- Gender-disaggregated impacts to be presented, monitored and evaluated;
- A (simplified) Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan *to* be prepared, submitted as part the CTF application/registration form; *An annex to this form (Annex 3 of Annex B)* needs to be filled in and submitted.

Overall gender performance shall be monitored and evaluated through a prioritization/scoring system (Component 1) against the submitted GMCP and GAP.

- Prioritization scoring system for a winning gender-balanced portfolio.
- Higher gender scores will be weighted higher in selection and investment in cases where scores are identical or other criteria

2. Methodology

This Gender Assessment is largely based on desktop research and close consultation with the key stakeholders including the Accredited Entity (KBR) and the Executing Entities (GGGI for Component 1 and GCG for Components 4 & 5). The assessment also draws from the findings of the baseline study, as well as potential delivery partners (Acceleration Advisory Consortium group for Component 2 of the Programme). The following sections are some of the key consultation activities carried out in relation to the GA and GAP preparation:

Assessment: Analysis and Structure

The following sections include each of the five countries’ legal and institutional framework and development priorities, with a focus on gender equality and women’s empowerment. Areas with gaps and high potential for women’s participation are also highlighted with a view to identify effective intervention points to address these gaps. The analysis in this section provides views on areas of economic vulnerability, climate entrepreneurship (or business enterprises) in general, in case the information on the former is lacking) from a gender perspective and identifies root causes for the prevalent gender inequalities.

Country assessment has been made through a dynamic process: A series of consultation meetings with KBR (AE), GGGI (Global Green Growth Institute), GCG (EE for Components 1 and 4) and NHI and NHF ARFF (EE for Component 5). The Acceleration Advisory Consortium group for Component 2 of the Programme. All the EEIs of the Programme Components were regularly consulted throughout the assessment process. In addition, a number of key stakeholders from the Acceleration Advisory Consortium group for Component 2 of the Programme. All the EEIs of the Programme Components were sought from the inception stage of the assessment preparation. Extensive discussions have been made with the GGGI and GCG EEIs to ensure that the findings are in line with their respective strategies, who have also coordinated country stakeholders in the review and feed back processes (See Appendix 4 below for more activities carried out). An EE of Components 1 and Component 4. GGGI will oversee executing GAP for Component 1 and GCG will oversee executing GAP for Component 4. The findings are intended to be used beyond ASEAN level.

The last section of each country assessment includes the gender analysis of some of the key sectors/sectors identified in the baseline study, as well as the potential impact of funding through GGGI’s pre-feasibility study (country demand analysis). Potential effects of the investment on gender perspective and feasibility of implementation are also discussed. The findings from this section will be used to feed into the Study presents key recommendations for integration into the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of the Programme.

# Country Specific Assessment

## CAMBODIA

### Legal and Institutional Setup and International Commitment of Cambodia

International Agreements on Women’s Rights: Cambodia has enacted several international agreements and signed several international declarations related to violence against men, women, and children.

- **CEDAW**: The Kingdom of Cambodia ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1985.
- **UNDRIP**: The country also ratified the United Nations Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP).
- **ASEAN Declaration**: At the regional level, the country signed the ASEAN Declaration of the Advancement of Women in 2015.
- **ASEAN Region**: The country has also committed to the implementation and implementation of women’s equal participation in all fields and at all levels.

### Institutional Arrangements

For the promotion of gender equality, the government of Cambodia established several institutions. These include the National Council for Women, the Technical Working Group on Gender (since 2004 and chaired by MOWA), with the UNDP and the Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) as partners; the Women’s Groups in the government line ministries; and Women and Children’s Consultative Committees (WCCCs).

At the national level, Cambodia has made progress in addressing domestic gender inequality through legislative initiatives, national action plans, education programs, and establishing resources for prevention of violence. Noteworthy are:

- The Constitution of the Kingdom of Cambodia states that “all forms of discrimination against women shall be abolished.” The exploitation of women in employment shall be prohibited.
- Men and women have equal rights in all fields, especially with respect to marriage and family matters.
  - Article 45
- The 2005 Law on Domestic Violence and the Protection of Victims
- The Law on the Suppression of Human Trafficking and Sexual Exploitation in 2008

### National Action Plan for Prevention Against Violence Women II (2014–2020) (NAPVAWII)

It was adopted by the Royal Government of Cambodia in 2013. The plan includes institutional operational plans addressing domestic violence, rape and sexual violence, and violence against women with increased risk.

### National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP) 2019–2025

This includes a specific section on “The Promotion of Population Policy and Gender Equity,” in the national category outcome of Human Resource Development.

### The Rectangular Strategy for Growth, Employment, Equity and Efficiency Phase IV: 2018–2023

It is also included as part of the Political Platform of the Royal Government of the Sixth Legislative of the National Assembly and specifically mentions


---
**Page 129**
---

gender equality, in the first rectangle on Human Resource Development, enhancing the need for mainstream women’s social-economic situation and strengthening their role in society.

The Neary Ratnakun strategy plan (2019-2023) is a five-year strategic plan for gender equality and women empowerment. The Cambodian government has been working hard to reduce gender gaps in education, employment, and leadership positions through expanded education, technical, and vocational training for women; and utilizing social media and other communication tools to raise awareness on gender equality issues.

### Women and Climate Change: The Cambodia Climate Change Strategy Plan 2016-2035

The Cambodia Climate Change Strategy Plan 2016-2035 includes the objective of reducing the vulnerability of women, children, and people linked to climate change impacts. Cambodia’s MOWA has formed a Gender and Climate Change Committee in 2016 with the Asian Development Bank (ADB) as its secretariat, which has the key role in gathering information on gender and climate change, conducting studies on the impact of climate change on women, and building climate change capacity in the ministry's departments. In line with the updated Cambodia’s Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), the government aims to increase the proportion of women’s participation in climate action, by pursuing ranges from 15% to 70%.

In collaboration with relevant ministries, such as the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF) and the Ministry of Environment (MoE), Cambodia has developed a mechanism for gender mainstreaming with climate change nexus at policy levels. Political commitments in this climate change agenda include the National Climate Change Strategy Plan 2016-2035, the Second National Climate Change Action Plan 2017-2020, and the Third National Climate Change Action Plan 2021-2030.

### General Situation Analysis of Cambodia

Cambodia is a predominantly rural society, with a population of approximately 16 million people (2019), where 51% are women. The country has made significant progress in reducing poverty and increasing its gross domestic product but has remained at a remarkable rate reaching 7% in the period of 2018 and 2019. However, Covid-19 crisis hit the country’s economy hard and its GDP decreased by -3.1% in 2020.

The economic sectors most affected were agriculture, manufacturing, construction, and tourism.


---
**Page 130**
---

and the real estate sector. Trade restrictions and international travel bans heavily have affected the overall economy.

With the higher portion of the population having been vaccinated, COVID-19 confirmed cases have significantly decreased from the end of 2021[1]. However, there are up-and-downs during 2022. Still, the confirmed patients are few. As the global COVID-19 situation has been eased, Cambodia’s major industries such as garment and footwear, tourism, and construction have started to recover. The economy also has been doing well since late 2021. In that sense, the World Bank predicts 4.8% of economic growth in 2023[2]. However, the country is still facing challenges with inflation, supply chain disruptions, declining in exports is projected, and economic growth in 2023 is not projected to be as good as in 2021[3].

Cambodia scored 6.593 on the Human Development Index (HDI) in 2021, falling into the medium human development group with a rank of 148 (out of 202)[4]. In the same year of 2021, the Gender Development Index (GDI) was 0.653[5], which means gender gaps in human development are substantial, knowledge and living standards are low compared to other countries in the upper middle income group 3 countries with a score of 0.958[6]. The OECD’s Social Institutions and Gender Index 2019 (SIGI)[7] which focuses on the discrimination against women in social institutions such as family, marriage, and property rights, Cambodia ranked “low” in terms of gender discrimination in social institutions (score 0.31); in particular, the country scored low for gender equality in the family domain[8].

Gender inequalities in Cambodia are also reflected in governance, education, and employment. This is largely due to (among others) gender roles, cultural/religious/social norms.

Gender equality in the labor market in Cambodia has been fluctuating over time and worsened since 2016[9]. The female labor force participation rate decreased from 58% of the total population in 2013 to 80% in 2020 and 2021 respectively[10]. Women in Cambodia continue to face a gender pay gap with an average per capita income of $4,796 compared to $6,045 for men. Considering a large portion of women’s labor is not recorded in official data as most are working informally and temporary forms of employment, the reality may be even starker. Women are mostly employed in the primary sector (agriculture) with 81% of female workers in this sector[11]. However, only 20% of the agricultural workforce while they own only 15.4% of the recorded agricultural land area [2017 Cambodia Agricultural Census Report][12]. Women are also more likely to be engaged in unpaid domestic care work, which affects the time available to engage in paid work. Marital status is the most important

determinant of women’s wage employment, given the fact that married women are 38% less likely to be in paid employment.10

The literacy rate for the population aged 15 years and older in Cambodia is slowly increasing reaching 74%, a 20-point increase from 54% in 2000. The gap persists in persons with 88% of males reaching the adult literacy rate compared to 80% of females (2021)11.

Physical and sexual violence against women is reported to be persisting. As indicated by the Cambodia Demographic Health Survey, approximately 35% of women aged 14–49 were exposed to physical violence at least once in their lifetime, and 6% of them had experienced sexual violence at least once since they were born or before marriage (2017)12. The Cambodian government has reported that Cambodia has over 260,000 victims of human and sex trafficking, including minors.

Because of persistent gender inequalities, COVID-19 had a disproportionate impact on women and girls in Cambodia. Women and girls were more likely to be employed in informal sectors with low pay, poor working conditions, and insecure and informal jobs, or live near and in poverty as have been worsened by the pandemic. The ability of being engaged in paid work has also been constrained by the closure of schools during the pandemic period, which resulted in a decline in female enrollment rates at all levels of education, especially for girls.

During the same period, education quality for girl children and women was equally exposed in rural areas due to the lack of access to internet and digital devices. The government has reported that 10% of schools were closed during the pandemic period, which resulted in a decline in female enrollment rates at all levels of education, especially for girls.

Restricted movements such as quarantine, isolation, and closed-schools during the peak of the pandemic were reported to have increased gender-based violence against women and girls. The same trend was reported globally. Women and girls in Cambodia also faced escalating risks of domestic violence due to tensions in the household and lack of access to support services.

* Climate Change and Gender in Cambodia

Cambodia is particularly vulnerable to climate change, with variable and extreme weather patterns such as severe rainfalls and droughts, ranking 120 most climate-vulnerable country in the Climate Risk Index from 2009-2018. The estimated loss and damage from climate change was $1.5 billion dollars in 2015 alone (UNDP, 2016). Women are disproportionately affected by climate change as a coping strategy during natural calamities and emergencies.

Women in Cambodia are increasingly seen as more vulnerable than men to the impacts of climate change because they have less access to land and water resources, and a higher dependence on natural resources. Women, especially in times of crisis and post-crisis, also end up being overburdened by the responsibility of caring for their family members including children and the elderly.

Following the 2015 Paris Agreement, there is an increasing awareness about women’s vulnerability to climate change from the perspective of the four major sectors, i.e., agriculture, renewable energy, water and waste management. The role of active gender mainstreaming in these sectors is expected to enhance women's resilience to climate change.

Women and Agriculture. Climate disasters have been found to be particularly damaging to the agricultural sector, rural communities, and therefore rural women, which represents 74% of the

# Gaia Consult Inc.

## Vulnerability of Cambodian rural women

### Women and Water: Sustainable and reliable water supply and sanitation services can improve health outcomes for women in Cambodia. However, only about one-third (34%) of urban households had improved water supply. Only 50% of rural households had access to improved sanitation facilities. The lack of safe drinking water and sanitation facilities increases the risk of waterborne diseases and other public health problems.

### Urban-rural divide in access to basic drinking water services and safely managed sanitation

- **Urban population:** 98% of urban population can access drinking water services, and 56% of them have managed drinking water services.
- **Rural population:** Only about one-third (34%) of rural households had improved water supply. Only 50% of rural households had access to improved sanitation facilities.

### Data on mortality rate attributable to unsafe water, unsafe sanitation and lack of hygiene is not available in Cambodia

### The impacts of drought, flood, sea-level rise, and extreme weather conditions have a significant effect on environmental sustainability and human well-being. In general, the lack of accessible water nearby also burdens children girls more than boys (especially those responsible for fetching water). This will demand more time and physical labour from girls and women.

### The increased water variability has the highest impact on vulnerable people and women, causing the loss of water resources and changes in water use patterns.


---
**Page 133**
---

quality, while droughts affect freshwater security. Improving access to modern industrially processed water service to remote rural areas will significantly benefit female farmers requiring extra water and sanitation services. This is especially true for women in urban slums who are more likely to be affected by water scarcity and even survival of the low-income households and especially girls and women in slums area.

### Women and Waste Management

The waste sector is responsible for approximately 21% of GHG emissions from Cambodia's total economy. The rapid increase in population, coupled with urbanization, presents a challenge to the country with increasing levels of waste generation. Waste composition is also changing with an increasing amount of plastic and other non-biodegradable materials being generated. This creates a need for additional waste segregation, treatment and reuse infrastructure and management systems. Rapid economic growth has led to increased consumption and production in Cambodia's major cities and this has resulted in greater waste generation in Cambodia's major cities and waste collection and management is increasingly recognized as an important issue. The majority of Cambodians still dispose of their waste by burning it or dumping it in public places, streams, and deserted locations, posing public health threat and soil, water and other environmental pollution risks.

While there is a general lack of data on the actual amount of recycled waste in Cambodia, recycling activities are increasing as more people become aware of the benefits of recycling. This includes the business potential targeting both organic and inorganic waste. Both men and women have a low level of awareness about the importance of recycling.

### Informal Female Workers as Garbage Collectors and Dismantlers

Informal female workers act as garbage collectors and dismantlers in urban areas, such as Phnom Penh, are the key actors in waste recycling in a sector largely lacking central organization. While this provides previous opportunities for informal women to earn income from collecting and selling recyclable materials and hazardous materials, and unregulated suboptimal recycling practices pose serious threats to occupational safety and health of these workers. The lack of awareness about the importance of recycling and domestic recycling activities in Cambodia are limited due to the lack of recycling industry, recycling infrastructures and a market for recyclable materials and recycled products. Therefore, some of the recyclable materials collected by informal women are not properly sorted or processed for recycling purposes.

### Climate Technopreneurship and Gender in Cambodia

Cambodia's entrepreneurship ecosystem is behind the lower middle-income average as well as Cambodia's ASEAN peers, globally ranking 135 out of 137 countries in the 2018 Global Entrepreneurship Monitor (GEM) report. Given the relatively recent development of climate technopreneurism, specific country data related to the field are not available. The empower initiative of UNDP and UN Women, aims to support women's empowerment through entrepreneurship and innovation. This is done by providing training in GIZ and partners such as SHE Investments, Nexus for Development and SNV to implement pilot interventions.

### Social and cultural constraints: Women’s economic empowerment and progress in Cambodia are hampered by societal norms and traditional gender relations.

Even though women constitute a majority of the population (51%), they earn less than their male counterparts. Buddhism, the country's major religion which accounts for 97% of the population, teaches that women are inferior to men and that it is a sin to be born as a woman. According to this idea, women need to cultivate more merits in the present life so that they can be reborn as males in their next life.

### Access to Formal Education: Markets Less: Women lack access to higher education and abovementioned social norms favouring men reduce Cambodian women’s opportunities to network and acquire the knowledge needed to formulate their business, which is one of the greatest obstacles to their access to markets. In general, women in Cambodia usually have less access and time for networking and building relationships with other entrepreneurs or potential customers, which are important for their business development.

Women are less likely to formally register their enterprises because they do not want to pay taxes on their income. They often opt to keep their businesses informal and micro- or small-scale due to the lack of incentives for formalization and the high costs involved (e.g., taxes).

### Lack of Financial Literacy: Women face a significant challenge for all SMEs in Cambodia, and many women-owned SMEs have been funded from scratch using their own savings without any external funding. Microfinance institutions are not a familiar name for Cambodian women; although organizations like SHE Fund exist, they are not widely known or used by that role. Slightly more women than men borrow from a bank or an MF. Using informal sources and personal savings is the most common way of financing their business.

### Lack of Financial Literacy: Women face a significant challenge for all SMEs in Cambodia, and many women-owned SMEs have been funded from scratch using their own savings without any external funding. Microfinance institutions are not a familiar name for Cambodian women; although organizations like SHE Fund exist, they are not widely known or used by that role. Slightly more women than men borrow from a bank or an MF. Using informal sources and personal savings is the most common way of financing their business.

Only 20% of women entrepreneurs run large businesses, and their profits are on average lower than men’s. Lack of financial literacy also

aggravates women’s vulnerability in terms of collateral availability and cost of funding, which often require specific support and mentoring to fulfill the banks’ requirements.*


Innovation is an important aspect of the success of many entrepreneurial enterprises, and so it is for climate change. The Global Innovation Index (GII) developed by INSEAD and the World Intellectual Property Organization (WIPO)* reflects this in its Global Innovation Index: The country ranks 109th out of 131 countries (2021), being at the bottom among ASEAN countries.


The severe underrepresentation of women in techentrepreneurship is felt across many developing economies where common structural factors affect women’s participation in the tech industries. For Cambodia, the number of female entrepreneurs pursuing climate change and renewable energy opportunities is very low compared to those pursuing career-advancing opportunities in the tech ecosystem.* Young women who complete post-secondary education are more likely than men to pursue careers in STEM fields (40% vs 25%). Female researchers in R&D (per million people) account for only 26.83% (2015) compared to their male counterparts.* Researchers suggest that the ecosystem is dominated by men, particularly mentors and start-up founders.* Women who participated in the study were male.


The CTF’s investment criteria explicitly include the promotion of the businesswomen-owned SMEs* and encouraging women entrepreneurs to develop gender mainstreaming plans. Through this, the proposed Programme aims to shift the traditional cultural norms imposed on Cambodian women and facilitate their access to climate change-related markets and financing.


The Programme will address each of the above-mentioned constraints in the following manner: First, the CTF’s upfront adoption of the gender mainstreaming component as part of its Investment Criteria (see Table 1) will ensure that women entrepreneurs are not only included but also prioritized for funding by inducing change in the public perception on the women’s roles in climate technology and entrepreneurship. Strong promotion of women’s skills within the SME enterprises as well as the leadership roles of women in these enterprises (e.g., through mentorship, training, and capacity building) as well as prioritization for CTF approval at Component 3 will also strengthen women entrepreneurs and women-led enterprises’ access to networks and accelerators, enabling women to have better access to business markets and financing.


• **Priority Sectors and Gender Implications in Cambodia**


Areas for priority intervention through the proposed Programme in Cambodia are, among others: Climate-smart agriculture/Water resource management/Infrastructure retrofitting to address the shifting climate.

The agricultural sector is a key driver for Cambodia’s economy, making up an estimated 21.1% of its GDP (2019). It is a female labour-intensive sector (74% of total workforce). Engaging women to learn new skills and technologies in agriculture can be particularly beneficial for Cambodian female farmers’ economic empowerment.

Since the COVID-19 pandemic, however, technologies for the agricultural sector have grown, along with government support. This has led to an increase in interest among women farmers during the pandemic. There is growing attention to innovative technology solutions in the agriculture sector. Agtech start-ups are reported to be on the rise and are being supported by the government.

Several start-ups in Cambodia are working to innovate agriculture, by taking farmers to the agri-chain through online platforms. By providing online platforms to compete woman-led agricultural enterprises will significantly increase the female farmer’s income generation potential.

Clean and safe sanitation is a critical issue for women living in urban and peri-urban areas from house chews and ensure their basic health, sanitation, and safety. A number of international organizations and donors, such as the AFDC, JICA, VFD Development Bank, Oxfam and the World Health Organization are supporting projects to improve access to clean water and sanitation services. Most urban waste supply and wastewater treatment projects in rural areas are implemented by international NGOs, which often work closely with women’s groups and local NGOs.

Introducing solid waste and wastewater management technologies has also a substantial potential for benefiting women living in peri-urban and urban areas who do not have access to these facilities, and that waste collection practice is limited. Recycling sector is under-developed and if any, largely reliant on informal collectors. The informal sector is the largest source of employment for women in Cambodia, but it is insufficient to cover the rapid growth in waste generation. In most cities in Cambodia, municipal solid and organic wastes are being collected and disposed without effective environmental and hygiene standards. Women who collect these materials are exposed to hazardous conditions, the lowest income level group mostly by women who migrated from rural areas. Industrial, toxic, and hazardous substances can cause serious health problems for women and children as well as environmental pollution.

In Oct 2018 Waste Management Strategy and Action Plan of Phnom Penh 2018-2035 was established. Focusing on Reduce, Reuse, Recycle and Recover, The Plan aims to promote environmentally sound management of waste disposal and mitigate impact on environment and human livelihood (Action Area). It while acknowledges the informal sector as a major source of employment for women in Cambodia, the informal practices on the ground where vulnerable women being involved, it is desirable to consciously manage this sector and strengthen the waste management systems in Cambodia by empowering the women in this sector.

Promoting women-led start-ups in the sectors of managing organic waste, and recycling plastic would offer the opportunity for GHG emission reduction and pollution mitigation, while economically generating income. One example of such a company is Eco-Plastic, which was founded in 2018. Many consider the example of “Eco-Plastic”, a start-up which is recycling plastic to make asphalt roads. The company was founded by a young Cambodian woman.

# INDONESIA

## Legal and Institutional Setup and International Commitment of Indonesia

### International Agreements on Women’s Rights
Indonesia has ratified and signed several international conventions, treaties, and plans of action on gender equality. Among them are:

- **United Nations Convention on the Political Rights of Women**, ratified by Law No 68/1958
- **United Nations Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)**, signed by the Government in 2000 and ratified by Law No. 34/2007
- **Optional Protocol to the CEDAW**, signed by the Government in 2000
- **International Labour Organisation’s ILO Convention number 106 on Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers for Work of Equal Value**, ratified by Law No.89/1957
- **International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights**, signed by the Government in 2006

### Institutional Arrangements
In 1978, the Government of Indonesia established the Ministry for Women’s Empowerment, which was later changed into the Ministry of Women Empowerment and Child Protection (MoWECP). The MoWECP is responsible to ensure the implementation of gender equality and women’s empowerment. In 1988, Indonesia became a member of the UN Commission on the Status of Women and began implementing the Beijing Platform for Action. At the regional level, the country signed the Declaration on the Advancement of Women in Asia and the Pacific (DAWAP) in 2005. The DAWAP is an agreement to promote and implement women’s equitable and effective participation in all fields and at all levels, and the Declaration on the Establishment of a Regional Centre for Women in ASEAN Region.

At the national level, the 1945 Constitution of the Republic of Indonesia is the basic legal foundation for women's rights in Indonesia. The Presidential Instruction No. 5/2000 on Gender Mainstreaming in National Development Policies and Programs requires all government agencies to include gender considerations when planning, implementing, monitoring, and evaluating of national development policies and programs. In addition, the Law No.14/2008 on Political Participation stipulates that political parties have to have a minimum of 30% women candidates to be elected to the parliament.

Gender considerations are also integrated into the National Long-Term Development Plan for 2005-2025. The quality of life and role of women and children in various development fields are low. Violence, exploitation, and discrimination against women and children are reported to be high.

Gaia Consult Inc.

Plan aims to strengthen the institutions and network for gender and children mainstreaming at the national and regional levels, and increase the availability of gender data and statistics.


In 2016, the Ministry of Women Empowerment and Child Protection (MoWECP) launched MiWiP Programmes in Indonesia. These programmes aim to prevent violence against women, human trafficking, and removing barriers to economic justice for women. The MoWECP supports women’s economic empowerment through various initiatives such as microfinance services.

In Indonesia, legal restrictions on women's ability to sit right and to asset ownership still exist. The Law No 1 of 1974 on Marriage impedes women from serving as the head of a household, creating further discrimination against women in terms of property rights and sons and daughters have different inheritance rights. Indonesia is reported to lack legislation, civil remedies or criminal penalties to protect against sexual harassment. There is no protection under domestic law for legal marital rape.

Religion and customs are also important in Indonesia's legal system, generating legal pluralism. While the civil law system is the foundation, coexists with that of customary laws (hukum Adat atau Adat Recht) and Sharia law. The application of Islamic law or Sharia law should be consistent with the prevailing Indonesian law and regulations. However, inconsistencies are often reported in practice.

**Women and Climate Change:** As the updated submission of the National Nationally Determined Contribution (NDCs) to UNFCCC 2016, Indonesia committed itself to map gender mainstreaming in climate change adaptation and mitigation policies. The government has also recognized the importance and strengthening women's capacity and leadership in climate change has been recognized as a priority for social and resilient livelihood.

The Directorate General of Climate Change Control (DGCC) of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry is responsible for the policy making body on the role of climate change in Indonesia. Also, a number of female officials holds the positions of Directors of Climate Change Adaptation and of Climate Change Mitigation.

| National income per capita. Margin of improvement was greater for women than men. Accordingly, the country’s Gender Development Index (GDI) in the year (value 0.941) measured slightly below global average at that time. In 2018, in the OECD’s Social Institutions and Gender Index 2019 (SIGI) of Indonesia, the level of discrimination against women was assessed as “high” with a score of 41.6%. The SIGI also noted that women were discriminated against in terms of their rights to education, freedom within the family* and *restricted civil liberties**.

In 2020, the female representation in the national parliament was about 20.3% of the available seats, a substantial increase from 14.7% in 2019 (OECD: 2019 rate: 17.4%). Indonesia is behind its minimum target of 30% women elected to the parliament. In general, female representation in public decision-making remains low. Women are underrepresented as political leaders and in senior positions in government. Female decision-makers in private affairs are often males. Only 15.46% of the households nationwide are headed by women.

Since the country’s education rate started to improve. By 2020, the literacy rate for the above: 15-year-olds marked 97% for males and 95% for females.

A significant gender gap exists in employment and evidence of gender discrimination persists in the labour market. In 2019, the employment rate of women in Indonesia was 52.2%, while men had a rate of 78.2%. Women’s unemployment rate was 6.4%, compared to 3.7% for men. The female and male poverty rates were 10.7% and 7.7%, respectively. Women are also more likely than men to work in the informal sector: 81.8% of the women’s workforce is employed in the informal sector compared to 79.4% of men, where a significant number of them are under-paid workers.

The economic recession after the Covid-19 pandemic outbreak affected the Indonesian labour market and the economy as a whole. The unemployment rate for both genders increased from 5.2% in 2019 to 8.7%. Women are disproportionately more affected by the current economic recession, mainly because of the extra burden of unpaid work and their concentration in vulnerable service sectors, such as retail and tourism, which were severely contracting, such as hotels. Low-income women workers who are mostly self-employed, or are employed on informal and insecure contractual arrangements. They are at the highest risk of losing their jobs.

As the global pandemic situation has been easing, the Indonesian economy is also recovering. Indonesia, representatively Bali, is one of the attractive tourism destinations; the tourism sector has particular concerns about gender equality in the workplace. In response to the pandemic, which was not yet finished, President Joko Widodo announced the removal of large-scale restrictions on crowds and people’s movements across the country. The government also relaxed some travel restrictions. Social media, internet or digital platforms enlarged and many firms utilizing those technologies have increased.

### Climate Change and Gender in Indonesia  

Indonesia ranks the 77th most vulnerable country to climate change globally in the Climate Risk Index from 1999–2018. The temperature increase, intense rainfall, sea-level rise, and the threat to food security are some of the major impacts of climate change on Indonesia. In particular, low-lying areas, forests, or coasts are found to be most vulnerable. In particular, single mothers and widowed women are at especially high risk due to their lack of resources.  

### Women in Agriculture, Agricultural Forests, and Fisheries are the three largest economic sectors in Indonesia, making 13.7% of the country’s GDP (2020). Data from the Indonesian Pensioner Union show that around 85% of women work as agricultural workers. The degradation of forests and fisheries is a major issue for agriculture, including farming, livestock rearing, and fishing. The ecosystem degradation from climate change has a significant impact on the survival of rural women who carry a greater share of the burden due to their lack of resources.  

Rising sea levels, combined with weather variability, rising sea surface temperature, and other negative effects, expose fishing households to the possibility of losing their foundation for livelihood, prompting some to migrate from coastal areas in search of alternative sources of income.  

### Women and Water, The JMP global database (2020) reported that 92% of the population has access to safely managed and basic service drinking water, while 86% have access to basic sanitation. However, women are disproportionately affected by climate change and its impacts on water availability for the country. While there is plenty of fresh water, population growth, urbanization, and expanding agriculture are putting a strain on existing water resources, infrastructure, and systems. As a result, 67% of low-income households in Indonesia lack access to safely managed drinking water and only have basic sanitation facilities.  

Women are also the principal water collectors and providers in the household; two out of every five Indonesian women dedicate their reproductive responsibility to women. Water fetching is becoming an increasing burden borne by girls and women with worsening water shortage. Climate Technopreneurship and Gender in Indonesia (2019)

## Lack of Financing: Indonesia makes use more banking services (45%) than women (42%). A larger proportion of men (60%) have access to credit facilities compared with women (38%).

### Women use informal financial services – especially in terms of saving and borrowing – mostly to meet their own needs, rather than for business purposes.

#### Limited Ownership as Assets as Collateral: In Indonesia, men and women do not have equal ownership rights over movable and immovable properties. Most female entrepreneurs do not own the land or buildings on which they operate their businesses. This prevents woman entrepreneurs from accessing (formal) credit for their businesses. Indonesian women are also less likely to be listed in property deeds. Only 10% of women entrepreneurs say that all of women have these assets registered in their names, even though 88% of the households had buildings or land that may be used as collateral.

### Indonesia ranks 87th Global Innovation Index (G20) for the 3rd year in a row. Falling under the lower middle-income group, Indonesia is one of only two lower middle-income-group neighbors in ASEAN, such as Vietnam (44th), and the Philippines (51st). In the current digitalization and SME ecosystem, Indonesia ranks 78th globally.

### The Global Entrepreneurship Monitor (2018-2019) pictured Indonesia with a significantly low relative levels of innovation for women entrepreneurs, with a gender gap of 50% or more compared to men. Over the past decade, the number of female entrepreneurs in Indonesia has increased by only 3%, while male entrepreneurship has remained largely unchanged. In rural areas, younger women are opting out of the labour market altogether, and those who do work tend to be employed in low-skilled jobs. Women’s access to information technology and communications compared to 31% of men. Increasing women's participation in STEM is particularly urgent in Indonesia.

### In 2018, female top managers were 22.1% and female researchers in R&D (per million people) were recorded at only 4%. Women entrepreneurs are paid less than men. This drop was seen across all ages of women in Indonesia, from 54.9% in 2010 to 29.8% in 2021.

The Programme will address all of the above-mentioned issues in the following manner: First, as in the case of Indonesia, it will introduce a gender mainstreaming component as part of its Investment Criteria (see Table 1 above) which will gradually change in the public perception on the women’s role within the business sector. Second, it will provide training and capacity building for women to strengthen their gender mainstreaming by either increasing women’s employment or improving welfare programmes for women. Third, it will promote women’s entrepreneurship through providing them with ready access to business management and improved women’s welfare as a labour condition which will improve the general perception of women’s labour within the formal business sector in the country over time.

There are some structural barriers that prevent women from equal participation in business and technology-related activities. In Indonesia, such as the 2017 Marriage Law that prohibits women to work outside their household heads as beneficiaries to formal credit services, and some religious dogmas regarding active roles of women within the family. The Programme will address these issues by providing training for women on the attention of the country MDA and other relevant government entities of Indonesia as part of Component 4 (Country Specific Action).

Strong promotion of women’s roles within the FDI enterprises as well as the leadership roles of the invested companies through capitalisation support in Component 1 and Component 2, as well as prioritization of women’s entrepreneurship in Component 3 will provide a conducive environment for women-led enterprises to build business networking and partnerships, enabling women to have better access to business markets and financing.

### Priority Sectors and Gender Implications in Indonesia

Areas for priority intervention through the proposed Programme in Indonesia are among others: Health (demonstrated by the increasing number of deaths from malaria and dengue fever caused by rising temperature and extreme weather events) Water harvesting! Domestic waste water recycling! Climate-resilient crop development.

Since 2010, Indonesia’s government has been spending more to support the government’s new health-related strategic goals, including digitalization of the sector. The COVID-19 pandemic since early 2020 accelerated this trend. However, despite these efforts, access to and utilization of public health services in Indonesia is gender-biased and is complicated by other factors such as geographical location, education level, income, and social norms. Women are more likely than men to be affected by non-communicable diseases but are benefiting the least from the health services. In addition, due to societal and cultural norms, some issues that affect women’s health (such as domestic violence) are often not acknowledged or discussed in public. This makes access of the Indonesian girls’ and women’s to sexual and health education more difficult. Through the digitalization of public health services and better monitoring of disease trends, access to health services can be improved for all segments of society.

With increasing water shortage related to climate change-induced extreme weather conditions, rainwater harvesting is increasingly being employed both in urban and rural settings. Being a primary water collection method, it has the potential to contribute significantly towards sustainable water resource management. Treatment of water for safer and cleaner domestic water provision shall also benefit girls and women as they are more likely than men to be responsible for household water supply.

Agriculture, forestry, and fisheries are key drivers of the Indonesian economy and agriculture is a female-dominated sector (70-80%). Thus, the adoption of crop (rice) resilient to drought and flood and technology for maritime development could have a major impact on Indonesian women. Persistent
