Given the importance of women's role in the country, the Programme should use the competitive advantage of women's rural knowledge and should invite them to participate actively in the development process. This would alleviate poverty and food insecurity, especially for rural and coastal households in Indonesia.

## LAO PDR

* **Legal and Institutional Setup and International Commitment of Lao PDR**

International Agreements on Women’s Rights. Lao PDR is a signatory since 1981 to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The most recent Capacity Assessment on CEDAW implementation in Lao PDR was conducted by the Association for Development of Women and Legal Education (ADWLE) in 2016. The Lao Government has also established a National Steering Committee to prepare for the ratification and implementation.

The National Steering Committee on the Preparations for the Ratification and Implementation of the International Covenants on Human Rights;
- the National Steering Committee on the Preparations for the UPR (Universal Periodic Review);
- the National Steering Committee on reporting and implementation of the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination;
- the National Commission for the Advancement of Women (NCAW);
- the National Commission for Mothers and Children (NCCM), the National Committee for Disabled People, the National Committee for Rural Development and Poverty Alleviation and the National Committee Against Human Trafficking.2

At the regional level, the country signed on the Declaration of the Advancement of Women in the ASEAN region in 1985 to promote the promotion and implementation of women’s equitable and effective participation in all fields and at all levels, and the Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women in the ASEAN Region in 2007.

At the national level, Lao PDR has a clear legal framework for promoting gender equality. The revised Constitution of 2003 explicitly states that women and men have equal rights in all spheres - political, social, cultural, and in the family. Women’s equal rights are also stipulated in the Family, Land and Property Law (2015) and the Labour Code (2016).

The Law on the Development and Protection of Women (2004) is the most specific Lao legislation articulating equal rights and access for women. The law provided a framework for several national policies and programmes, including the National Action Plan 2007-2015 as a basis to form the National Commission for the Advancement of Women and Mother–Child.

The Small and Medium Enterprises Development Plan 2016-2020: Endorsed by Prime Minister Decree No. 235PM in 2017, its main objectives are: to promote productivity,

### Women and Climate Change

Lao PDR established the National Green Growth Strategy (NGGS) till 2030 in 2018. Through the NGGS, the country is committed to pursuing sustainable green growth development strategies that are aligned with the Lao PDR’s Vision 2050, the Seventh Five-Year Socio-Economic Development Strategy to 2025, and the Eighth Five-Year National Socio-Economic Development Plan (2016–2020). The NGGS is a comprehensive strategy for sustainable development. The Strategy identified the promotion of the advancement of women’s participation to be an important focus and promotes vocational training, labour skill development and creation of jobs for women, encouraging and promoting the participation of women in economic, political and sociocultural activities.

#### General Situation on Women in Lao PDR

Lao PDR is a country with a population of 7.4 million people (2018). 63% of the population lives in rural areas and depends rely on subsistence agriculture. Lao PDR has enjoyed high economic growth, with a GDP growth rate average 7.8% over the last decade[5] and falling poverty rates from 33.9% to 20.4% between 1996–2016. Despite its progress in reducing poverty, Lao PDR’s natural resources, which has not translated into a commensurate decline in poverty. Women’s access to productive assets is limited and women are underrepresented in decision-making positions and are restricted and subjected to gender discrimination and social institutions.

In 2019, Lao PDR scored 0.631 on the Human Development Index (HDI), ranking 173th out of 189 countries, grouping the country into a low human development category[6]. Despite considerable progress in reducing poverty, many challenges remain for better economic growth and social inclusion.

Lao PDR has a Gender Development Index (GDI) of 0.527, classified as a country with “medium to low” gender equality. The GDI is the average score across three sub-indexes: health, education, and economic empowerment. Lao PDR scored the lowest in the economic empowerment index with a score of 0.262 due to gender discrimination and social institutions.

The political representation of women in the National Assembly has grown by nearly 20% since 1990. It is now around 35%, which is still below the regional average of 47%. Women are underrepresented in senior government positions with a female occupancy rate of 28%.

In Lao PDR, the labour market participation is made up of an equal share of women and men, but generally, women occupy the lower ranges of the labour market. The share of women in wage employment is lowest at all levels as well – ILO data shows that in Lao PDR, 64.3% of female employees are self-employed, compared to 26.0% for male employees and 8.5% is in the industries sector. Of the total employed females, over 87.5% are self-employed[10].

Decision-making of female business owners in managing the businesses is often shared within their family members such as sons and daughters. The gender gap in public contracts is not so high in public contracts and signed by women only 58% and 37% respectively of the total number of contracts.

The gender gap in education has narrowed10. The literacy rate for the population aged 15 years and older in Lao PDR has also improved, reaching 84% in 2020 on average. However, gender disparity persists. Only 67% of women are literate.

Having said that, Laos women are active economic agents, marking 70% of the country’s economic migrants. An increasing number of women are migrating to Thailand as workers mainly in the service sector and agriculture. The majority of these women are from rural areas. Many of them are victims of sexual exploitation. Physical and sexual violence persist; and domestic violence is a silent but widespread problem: 3 in 10 women report being a victim of violence in Laos PDR.

Because of the COVID-19 pandemic, COVID-19 pandemic has significant impact on women and girls in Lao PDR. The COVID-19 pandemic and its corresponding mitigation measures have significantly increased women and girls’ exposure to gender-based violence (GBV). Female workers were more likely to be laid off or lose their jobs than male workers during the pandemic. Women are also victims of sexual exploitation. Physical and sexual violence persist; and domestic violence is a silent but widespread problem: 3 in 10 women report being a victim of violence in Laos PDR.

The effects of COVID-19 pandemic on women and girls in Lao PDR have been exacerbated by the crisis is skewed towards jobs and sectors where women are overwhelmingly represented such as agriculture, domestic work, and informal sector. Women’s access to resources and services, including the effects of severe weather events on the agricultural sector in 2019, must have left a substantial impact on women.

### Women and Agriculture

In Lao PDR, one third of the country's GDP (29%) is generated in the agricultural sector and more than 75% of the population is engaged in agricultural activities. Women in Laos PDR are involved in all stages of production from planting seeds to harvesting crops and raising livestock since 180011. As high as 96% of the total female employment is concentrated in the agricultural sector, women’s contribution to the production of staple food products and livestock production.

They are typically given key responsibility for food security and nutrition within their households. However, Women's high level of participation in agricultural work in Lao PDR is not always a source of women’s empowerment. As stated by the World Bank and ADB12 “gender assessment report for the 4th PRD (2018-2023)”, women’s role in agriculture is significant, but their contribution to household income is low. Women are often engaged in seasonal jobs, often taken by female agricultural workers, are helping to pull some households, in selected areas, out of poverty.

### Lao PDR: Transitioning from subsistence farming to commercial agriculture

This transition has brought benefits for some rural communities, but it has also exacerbated gender disparities. Scaling up for commercialization and mechanization has led to the displacement of women and deprivation of small farming landowners of their productive land, including female land holders who have worsened the vulnerability of small-scale farmers, esp those who cannot find employment off-farm.

As noted by the World Bank’s 2016 working paper on the feminization of agriculture,[1] commercial agriculture is, however, opening new opportunities for women to undertake paid employment outside the family sphere. This can be through participation in “non-farm” activities (e.g., as farm contract farmers or direct wage employees). Formal contractual arrangements to secure fair work conditions are still rare.

Women and Water. About 80% of Lao PDR’s water flow materializes during the rainy season and the rest during the dry season. Most of the domestic water supply in Lao PDR is adequately supplied by means of piped connections, but a significant proportion (about one-third) relies on groundwater from wells or water from public standpipes or wells for drinking. Bathing and laundry washing usually take place in streams or rivers. Women are responsible for collecting water for household use and for livestock during the dry season, particularly in the headwaters of some rivers and areas far from perennial rivers.

General population data show that women make up 51% of the total population. However, there is a legal and institutional capacity to manage in rapid economic development and population growth in recent years. New issues are flooding and drought, which have started to significantly impact the country in recent years. The lack of water storage infrastructure (e.g., dams) and the poor quality of water storage and distribution infrastructure[2] overcame localized temporary water shortages in a country already facing water scarcity.

The Lao Social Indicator Survey 2017 reported that 48.7% of those responsible for gathering water for the family are women along with girls 6-9 years (Table 2). The disparity is even steeper in (esp., remote, without access to piped connections) rural areas where women and girls are responsible for household fetching for the family. This takes significant time away from their education and other activities that could lead to lower jobs, as well as income generation.

# Climate Change and Gender in Lao PDR

Lao ranks 45th in the Global Climate Risk Index (CRI) 2019 and 52nd in CRI from 2000 to 2019. Climate change results in hotter seasons, heavy floods, droughts, abnormal cold and other irregular weather patterns. These events have a direct impact on food security, leading to increased prices of food shortages, familial stress, and even occurrence of domestic violence.

Women are mainly responsible for household water supply and energy for cooking, yet their voices are seldom heard in the national-level and national-level climate adaptation policies and coping plans to climate change.

Lao PDR's entrepreneurship ecosystem is below the lower-middle-income average as well as Lao PDR's ASEAN peers, ranking 102nd out of the 137 countries in the 2019 Global Entrepreneurship Index (GEI). World Bank reports indicate that SMEs in Lao PDR are a major employer, but their productivity is low. The informal sector contributes significantly to GDP; however, only about 45% of SMEs in Laos PDR contribute only about 16% to GDP. Men account for most workers in the formal sector, and women represent more than half of the workforce in the informal sector. Women's employment opportunity remains in the informal sector.

Data is not available on the number of firms and micro-enterprises operating in the informal economy in the country. Informal vendors, such as drivers, handymen, construction workers, etc., and the contribution of the informal SMEs to an economic sector is largely unknown. Informal street vendors take precedence over formal retail outlets for women. Women represent more than 90% of vendors in fresh-food markets throughout the country.

Meanwhile, women-owned SMEs registered in Lao PDR are also predominantly made up of small firms. By sectors, women-owned enterprises mainly function in the retail and wholesale trade, and only to a

Women's economic participation is heavily skewed to the unpaid jobs or informal sector. The number of female government employees is 25 times higher than female employees. ### Access to Business and Markets: Women suffer lower level of education and cultural norms
- women's participation in the households are limiting women’s opportunities to network and acquire the knowledge needed to formulate their business and to access to market, especially for informal sector.
- Women entrepreneurs work longer hours (15.8 hours) compared with men (6.08 hours). Moreover, there are legal restrictions for female entrepreneurs to access land and credit. According to the World Bank Group Country Diagnostic (2013), evaluated there are differences between women and men of the same marital status in 21 areas in the country's legal system.


- Lack of access to reliable and long-term finance and credit poses structural challenges to business enterprise of all sizes in La PDR, even more difficult for the SMEs in informal sectors, where women/business owners are more skewed. It is reported that in 2014, less than half of women-owned enterprises had access to formal financial services compared with two-thirds of men-owned enterprises. Women-owned firms are less likely to grow and compete against other firms in Asia.15 Female entrepreneurs in La PDR are also less likely to have access to credit. According to the World Bank Group Country Diagnostic (2013), women report the lack of access to finance and credit as a key barrier to their economic success.


La PDR ranked 117th out of 132 countries in the Global Innovation Index 2021.

### Women-owned enterprises have been rising in recent years. According to the World Bank's Enterprise Surveys, women-owned firms increased from 45% in 2008 and 2012, to 47% in 2016. The country has a comparatively high proportion of female top managers: 43.1% (2018). While quantity of the women's business enterprises has increased over time, further study is needed.

Few Start-up incubators and accelerators exist in Lao PDR. Women's Entrepreneurial Centre (WEC) is one of them, which provides training and mentoring services.

The Programme will address each of the above-listed constraints in the following manner: First, CTF requires all the Fund-supported JV enterprises to proactively increase female employment (eg. for doors dealerships, women’s clothing, etc.) through a combination of measures such as Gender Based Analysis (GBA) & Gender Action Plan (GAP) (General Treatment, Section 1-2, Entity's Gender Mainstreaming Strategy). This will lead to increased female participation in the workforce and improved women’s work conditions (equitable with male workers in terms of contractual terms and benefits). The Programme will also encourage more women’s participation in the formal employment sector at the country level.

Strong promotion of women’s roles within the JV enterprises as well as the leadership roles of the invested companies through capacitization support in Component 1 and Component 2, as well as privatization of the enterprises, will lead to increased female entrepreneurship. The Fund-supported enterprises to build business networking and partnerships, enabling women to have better access to business opportunities.

• **Priority Sectors and Gender Implications in Lao PDR**

Areas for priority intervention through the proposed programme in Lao PDR are, among others: Climate-resilient crop development with insurance schemes; Livestock disease control & pest management; Sustainable Forest management and protection; Water treatment/water resource management.

Given the high level of women’s involvement in the agricultural sector, investment in climate-resilient technology has a potential to positively impact female farmers and agricultural workers’ livelihoods. To overcome the barriers that prevent women from participating in climate-smart agriculture and green entrepreneurship, the Programme needs to consciously promote female farmers' participation in the funded enterprises and provide them with necessary training.

Enhanced livestock management systems would also benefit female livestock farmers in remote areas when they are provided required technical skill training for disease control and pest management.

As Laoistan girls and women hold higher responsibilities as water collectors for the family, investments in climate-resilient technologies such as rainwater harvesting and improved sanitation and hygiene technologies would contribute to improving gender equality by easing them from their physical chores and time wasted. Wastewater treatment will improve overall sanitation conditions, and this will in particular, positively affect girls and women’s health and safety.

PHILIPPINES

• **Legal and Institutional Setup and International Commitment of the Philippines**

International Agreements on Women's Rights: The Philippines is one of the earliest signatories to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The Philippines ratified CEDAW in 1984. In the last two decades, the Philippines submitted six State reports and subsequently three NGO shadow reports which were accompanied by 13 NGOs to the CEDAW in compliance with the obligation of State Parties.

29


---
**Page 151**
---

The country also ratified in 1987 the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination, and the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1974. The Philippines also ratified in 1990 the Convention on the Rights of the Child, the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the involvement of children in armed conflict in 2003 and the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the sale of children, child prostitution and child pornography in 2002 respectively.

At the regional level, the country signed on the Declaration of the Abolishment of Women's ASIAN Slavery and the Elimination of Violence against Women in the ASEAN Region in 1985.

At the national level, the Philippine Constitution (1973) stipulates equal protection of women in its Article II Section 14 item "The State shall ensure that all appropriate measures to ensure the opportunity of women on equal terms with men and without any discrimination to represent their grievances before the Government." In addition to this Section from fourteen other similar items ["The State] recognizes the basic equality between men and women and the dignity of the human person as enshrined in the law of women and men."

Magna Carta of Women (Republic Act No. 9718): A comprehensive women's human rights law, the Act was signed on June 2009: The Act stipulates that "The State shall adopt all appropriate measures to ensure the opportunity of women, on equal terms with men and without any discrimination to represent their grievances before the Government." In addition to this Section from fourteen other similar items ["The State] recognizes the basic equality between men and women and the dignity of the human person as enshrined in the law of women and men."

The Philippine Plan for Gender-Responsive Development (PGRD) 1995–2002 was adopted in 1995, through which the government has pursued a long-term vision of women's empowerment and gender equality and translates the Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA) into policies, strategies and programs to promote gender equality. This 30-year perspective plan ensures that women-friendly policies take root and flourish.

The National Commission on the Role of Filipino Women (NCRFW) was established in 1975 as the Magna Carta Commission to spearhead the government's efforts to advance women's rights. The operation of the NCRFW was renamed in 2009 as the Philippines Commission on Women (PCW), with expanded mandate and functions, including coordinating gender equality policies and programs and capacitating the government agencies to execute their respective gender mainstreaming agenda.

RA No. 9786 ("General Appropriations Act (GAA) On Programs/Projects Related to Gender and Development (GAD)") was enacted in 2005 to mandate all of the concerned government entities to submit their GAD plan in the GAA. Each plan submitted by the agencies was evaluated based on its


---
**Page 152**
---

The Gender Equality (GE) and Women’s Empowerment (WEE) Plan 2019-2025: This plan is the third iteration of GE/WEE in support of the PVPD 1995-2025. The plan covers the Philippines Development Plan (PDP) 2017-2022 and the remaining years of the PVPD 1995-2025. This Plan set out the following Sustainable Goal Areas (SGAs):


SGA 1: Expanding opportunities for women in (trade, industry, services/agriculture, fishery and forestry) labour and employment.


SGA 2: Accelerated Human Capital Development through investment in gender equality and women empowerment (in health and nutrition education sector).


SGA 3: Fostering Women’s Economic Empowerment (women's participation, leadership and benefits in science, technology, ICT infrastructure, and energy).


While the Philippines’ STEM education is comparatively fairly well compared with the global average and other neighboring countries in the region (making 45% of the enrolment in STEM education being women), there are still challenges to be addressed. The Philippines has been lagging behind and benefiting in the science, technology and innovation (STI) and ICT sector, especially after the COVID-19 pandemic and the subsequent economic downturn due to the global health crisis and the 2019-2025 crisis due to their suitability to remote work.” Thus the Plan, among others, aims to support technology and innovation in STEM education. The Philippines Science and Education Development Program (PSED) is one of the key programs under the STEM Education program and increase the number of women scholarship programs for S&T education.


Other laws related to gender mainstreaming and women’s empowerment include, among others:


- Prohibition on Discrimination Against Women (Republic Act No. 6725): This prohibits discrimination with respect to terms and conditions of employment solely based on sex. Under this law, it is illegal to discriminate against women in the hiring process, promotion opportunities, and other benefits solely on account of sex in considered discrimination.


- Anti-Violence Against Women and Their Children Act of 2004: Act Providing Assistance to Women Engaging in Micro and Cottage Business Enterprises, and for Other Purposes (Republic Act No. 9513): This law provides assistance to women engaging in micro and small enterprises.


- Act to Promote Entrepreneurship by Strengthening Development and Assistance programs to Micro, Small and Medium Scale Entrepreneurs Attending for the Purpose (Repurposed Act No. 6077): The law aims to provide financial assistance to Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) and for other purposes as amended by RA No. 8299 and 9501 (known for short: “Magna Carta for Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEACT). It provides all possible assistance to Filipino women entrepreneurs in the country.


- The Go Negosyo Act or Republic Act No 10644 (“An Act Promoting Job Generation and Inclusive Growth through the Development of Micros, Small and Medium Enterprises”) strengthens the existing MSME law and expands it on the country. by establishing “negosyo

**Magna Carta for Countryside and Barangay Business Enterprises or Republic Act 8430:**
- Provides for the establishment of a Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSME) Development Fund.
- Establishes the Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Corporation (MSMEDC).
- Provides for the registration of small and micro-enterprises.

**105-Day Extended Maternity Leave Law (RA 11239):**
- Fully titled: "An Act Increasing the Maternity Leave Period to one hundred five (105) days for Female Workers with an Option to Extend for an Additional Thirty (30) Days without Pay and Granting an Additional Fifteen (15) Days Paid Leave."
- Effective December 28, 2017.

**Solar Parents Welfare Act (RA 9972):**
- Fully titled: "An Act Providing for Benefits and Privileges to Solar Parents and Their Children. Appropriating Funds therefor and for Other Purposes."
- A law providing benefits such as free electricity, water, and other services for solar parents and their children.
- Includes among others flexible work arrangements, parental leave, livelihood development services, educational and housing benefits.

**Anti-Sexual Harassment Act of 1995.**

The Philippines Commission on Women (PCW), a provincial agency of the Philippine Government that is responsible for implementing the policies of the country's Magna Carta of Women (Republic Act No. 9710), which has a focus area on "Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Sector," is currently working with the Canadian International Development Agency (CIDA) to implement WEEM across the priority regions of the project, to establish need-based measures to back up their gaps and boost local economic development.

Another important initiative funded by the Government of Canada is “The Great Women Project 2” (also named, the Supporting Women’s Economic Empowerment in the Philippine Project), which seeks to improve the economic empowerment of women micro-enterprises (WMEs) and their workers through three main components: (i) capacity building; (ii) improving the enabling environment for women's economic empowerment, which VCPC participates as the lead implementing agency; and (iii) knowledge and capability development. The project will assist 831 WMEs with an estimated 57,000 employees, of which 75 percent or 11,760 were female workers across the regions.

**Women and Climate Change.**
The Climate Change Act of 2009 of the Philippines recognizes the need to identify the "differential impacts of climate change on men, women, and children." The Act aims to incorporate gender perspectives in all national policies, plans, programs, and projects. It also mandates efforts, plans, and programs mandating local governments to carry out capacity building programmes with a focus on women's empowerment.

The Philippine Women's Commission (PWC) in its submission to the UNFCCC Secretariat in 2015 highlights the need to mainstream gender considerations in (ii) research and development (R&D); (iii) planning and policy-making; (iv) knowledge and capability development, and (v) enhancing women's participation in climate change adaptation.

The government has committed since 1995 to allocate five of its annual budget for GAD.

# General Situation Analysis of the Philippines

The Philippines has made significant progress in empowering women and advancing gender equality. Since the Government introduced the Constitution in 1987 affirming women and men equally, there have been numerous laws and policies to promote gender equality.

Based on the 2021 Census of Population by the Philippine Statistics Authority, 56.4% of the total population were males while 83.6% were females which resulted in a sex ratio of 101 males for every 100 females. The average age at marriage was 27 years old for women and 29 years old for men; on the other hand, outnumbered their male counterparts in the older age group of 55 years old and over.

The Human Development Index (HDI) of the Philippines for 2020 was 0.718 which placed the country in the high human development category**. With this, the Philippines ranks 107th out of 189 countries worldwide. The Philippines is one of only two countries in Asia with a low HDI (the other being Bangladesh), with high equality in HDI achievements on health, knowledge and living standards (with a score of 0.652). The Philippines has also been ranked as the second-highest country for the "very high" status of gender-related discrimination reported in SIGI index in 2019. The highest level has been attributed to women's limited participation in the family and restricted access to productive and financial resources (both at 67%).

Poverty continues to be concentrated in specific segments of the population. The poverty incidence among the rural population is higher than that of urban areas, with a poverty rate of 25% for the year 2018. This translates to 3.9 million Filipino living in poverty, i.e., farmers (36%), fishers (26.2%), children belonging to families with income below the official poverty threshold (25.9%), the self-employed and informal family workers (16%) and women from poor families (14.6%) for the year 2018.

The Philippines ranked 104th out of 160 countries based on the Gender Inequality Index (GII) value of 0.453. This is based on the following statistics of the Philippines: 20% of parliamentary seats are held by women.


---
**Page 155**
---

### Women and Agriculture

Filipinas has developed innovative solutions to challenges posed by climate change, such as rice varieties that thrive in coffee and non-coffee areas where rice is most efficient for water conservation and land use—generates more income than other crops. However, the persistence of wide gaps between women's and men's education levels and their participation in agriculture at all levels remains a concern.

### Agrarian Reform Laws

Reforms have increasingly recognized and protected the rights of rural women to land and other productive resources. Nevertheless, implementation of existing land laws and guidelines has been slow due to lack of political will and inadequate institutional capacity. Mainstreaming strategies have blocked gender equality in agriculture and rural sectors, particularly regarding access to credit and extension services.

### Gender Equality in Agriculture

Agriculture is considered a male-dominated sector; however, there are more female farmers registered than female landholders. The agricultural labor force is mainly composed of men at 77% while only 23% are women. Women tend to be less integrated in value chains than men due to lack of mobility and access to markets, resulting from social norms preventing them from interacting with buyers, suppliers, and other actors in the chain. In contrast, men focus more on commercial farming for food consumption, whereas men primarily focus on cash crops. Women farmers are also less likely to be targeted for extension services as many extension agents still do not recognize women as farmers.

# Women farmers have access to irrigation, 29% to seeds, 26% to training, 23% to extension services, 21% to fertilizers and seeds subsidy, 20% to pest control management, 20% to calamity assistance, and 14% to credit. In contrast, women farmers in the Philippines receive only half of this level of support as their male counterparts; food production by women could increase by 25% and total agricultural output by up to 40%.

# A 2021 publication on Gender and Value Chains from GIZ notes that in Southeast Asia, women are also more involved in small trade, particularly in agricultural goods. The Philippine Centre for Postharvest Development and Mechanization (PCPM), under the Department of Agriculture, has been active in its design, fabrication, and commercialization of gender-friendly technologies to provide both male and female farmers with access to machines that will help them increase their productivity.

# Women and Energy: Access to electricity is still a dream for about 11 million Filipinos. In Southeast Asia, the Philippines ranks last among countries in terms of the percentage of people who are energy deficient. Looking at data from the World Bank (2019), the electricity grid is not yet fully accessible everywhere; even for poor families who have access to electricity, paying for electricity is a constant struggle—only 35% of households pay their bills on time.

# The primary energy source in the Philippines is mostly based on fossil fuels such as oil (56%), gas (40%), and coal and others (29%). According to the IEA data of 2018, Renewable Energy sources are barely accessible for most people; they have limited access to solar power. Consequently, the energy sector results as the largest contributor to greenhouse gas emissions.

# Women in the Philippines are the primary consumers of energy and are most affected by the lack of clean, affordable, and reliable energy. This underscores the value of women's perspective in policy-making and decision-making. The importance of women in improving household energy usage depends on family and household decisions; women play an active role in implementing energy projects. Having power at home allows the mother of the house to use time-saving equipment such as rice cookers, electric fans, and refrigerators. Women who live in remote areas or in isolated rural areas means they don't have to travel half a day to the main island to recharge their phones and flashlights. Business and commercial opportunities have also increased because having electricity; as they may work longer hours.

# Figure 2. Philippines Total Primary Energy Supply in 2018

The Philippines has a female chairperson of the Philippine National Renewable Energy Board (NREB) since her appointment by the Philippine President on 28 March 2019. She steers the performance of the Board in its mission to promote and develop renewable energy resources, as mandated under the Philippine Renewable Energy Act of 2008 (Republic Act No. 9513) and in formulating recommendations for new policies to the Department of Energy (DOE) toward achieving the renewable energy targets set by the Philippines. She also works with other government agencies and the private sector to respond effectively to gender issues and to bolster equal rights and shared responsibilities between men and women.

## Women and Water

Results of the 2020 Annual Poverty Indicators Survey show that most families had an improved source of drinking water (97.5%). Residents in rural areas are more likely to have an unimproved source of drinking water than those living in urban areas (84% vs. 63%, respectively). Four in every five families (80.2%) did not practice any method or treatment in ensuring that drinking water is safe for consumption. The proportion of households practicing some form of treatment before using drinking water from an improved source with moundpipe collection time of not more than 30 minutes. Families with basic drinking water service level in urban areas at 89% was higher than in rural areas (90.6%). In 2020, 51% of households had access to a shared sanitation facility and only 47% have an improved sanitation facility not shared with another household. Women and their households also need water for bathing, washing clothes, cooking food, cleaning the house, and other domestic chores as well as for watering plants or gardens. Water is also used in preparing food, cleaning utensils, and washing dishes. Water is also used to prepare food, clean utensils, and wash dishes. Women are more likely than men to be responsible for collecting water from a shared source and for carrying it home. They are also more likely to be responsible for cooking, preparing meals, and doing the household chores. Women are also more likely to be responsible for cleaning the house and washing clothes. Women are also more likely to be responsible for caring for children and elderly family members.

### Climate Change and Gender in the Philippines

The Philippines is one of the world’s most vulnerable populations to the effects of climate change, ranking 2nd in the Climate Risk Index from 1999-2018. When the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR) released its Global Assessment Report on Disaster Risk Reduction in 2015, this was due, in part, to its geographic location as an archipelago with an exposed coastline, and its population of over 103 million people. The Philippines is also one of the most disaster-prone countries; common average, eight or nine tropical storms make landfall each year. The frequent occurrence of natural disasters has led to a high level of vulnerability in the country, which further limits the financial resources and organizational capacity for comprehensive disaster risk reduction.

Women play an integral role in this local disaster risk reduction and management, stemming from their traditional roles as caretakers and providers within households and communities throughout the region. Within the remaining population, women are the key drivers in all aspects of community organizing and development work. Inequality is often worsened by variations in wealth and has a direct effect on the ability to adapt to climate change. For example, Super Typhoon Haiyan (Haiyan) in 2013 affected approximately 16 million individuals, half of whom are women and girls.

Women are increasingly seen as more vulnerable than men to the impacts of climate change; because of their central role in household decision-making and resource allocation.

### Business Enterprises and Gender in the Philippines

The Philippine Statistics Authority indicates that in 2015 there were around 900,000 registered enterprises in the country. An estimated 10% of these businesses were classified as small, and less than 1% were classified as medium-sized. Less than one percent are large enterprises. The vast majority (90%) were either sole proprietorships or micro-enterprises. It is estimated that there are around 28,000 SMEs in the Philippines.

The Philippine entrepreneurship ecosystem is mid-range income average as well as its ASEAN peers, ranking it at number 51 among the countries in the 2019 Global Entrepreneurship Index (GEI), while the Global Innovation Index ranks the country 57th out of 132 countries (2021). The Philippine start-up ecosystem is supported by a range of accelerators and other support providers such as Investing in Women, Philippines Women Entrepreneurs Network, and others. However, there are no available gender-disaggregated data on the nature of businesses (i.e., whether they are start-ups on climate change adaptation or innovation) are not available. There are no available gender statistics on SMEs ownership in the Philippines.

While 73.3% of males are economically active participating in labour force, women are only 50.1%. The only type of workforce activity where:

- the participation of women was higher compared to men in unpaid family work, where the country’s female participation rate is at 48%, while men’s participation was at 7% in 2015.
- Women's participation tends to be generally low across all forms of economic activity. Only


---
**Page 159**
---

1.7% of women in the workforce are employers while male counterparts were 3.5%. Female top-managers are only 29.9%.**


Social and cultural constraints: Although the 2009 Magna Carta for Women (MCW, R.A. No. 9713) provides equal rights of women in all matters relating to marriage and family relations – inclusive of owning, acquiring, and administering property, married women are applied exceptions to these provisions. The MCW states that “the husband’s consent is required” as a husband’s decision shall prevail.” The Philippine Commission on Women is currently working to appeal this provision. However, the lack of enforcement of this law by the government is virtually disadvantageous to women. Prioritization of the beneficiary groups in the government programs also tends to favor men over women.


Access to Business Markets: About 92% of all enterprises in the Philippines were at micro-level, where women were most owners and operators. About 60% of women’s enterprises were under performing due to lack of management/business support and poor access to finance. Many women entrepreneurs are also constrained by cultural norms that prevent them from responding to government efforts in this regard, due to concerns about official interference and the general perception that women are not capable of running a business.


Lack of Financing: The Women in Development and Nation Building Act (Republic Act No. 7192) stipulates equal treatment between women and men regarding opening up a bank account and other banking services. However, women are still discriminated against when it comes to agricultural credits, equal rights to enter various financial contractual relations. Again, married women are subject to the husband’s consent in order to open an account or apply for loans. According to the Philippine Women’s Bank (PWB) Act of 1997, in case both spouses earn wages, the husband is deemed the household head by default, eligible to receive credit and other financial services. The PWB Act also states that women must respect a male partner to co-sign any financial contracts.[30] Women entrepreneurs often seek micro-finance schemes, but it is restricted to be repetitive and complicated in procedures. This makes it difficult for women to access these schemes. In addition, the requirement of providing a bank loan is often inaccessible to women.


The government has taken a range of measures to increase women’s financial literacy and access to credit, including women-owned and female enterprises such as contract standardization, lowered transaction costs, incentive schemes, flexible payment schedules, etc. In addition, the government has also launched several initiatives under the Gender Responsive Py which stands for Gender Responsive Economic Actions for the Transformation of Women.[31]


These barriers discourage women from pursuing their business. QBO and DTI’s Start-up Pinoy Programmes are designed to provide financial support to women entrepreneurs in various sectors, inclusive Start-up landrace in the Philippines for empowering women founders, leaders, enablers, and explorers. Through the Start-up Pinoy Programme, QBO and DTI aim to take meaningful steps towards guiding norms, especially on the role of women in the workplace. Impact Powers Network


---
**Page 160**
---

also a recently launched activity by USAID Philippines which also supports women enterprises as an angel investing network.

The Programme will address the above-listed constraints in the following manner: First, relatively disadvantaged groups such as women, status in the Philippines could be improved when agricultural “women-led enterprises” are prioritised and supported by the CTF. In case a CTE-certified enterprise’s priority is to improve its financial performance, it should be encouraged to comply with the CGI Safeguard policy on involuntary resettlement, and its interim safeguard standards (such as IFC Performance Standards) or other relevant international standards (e.g., World Bank Group’s “Safeguards Policy Statement No. 5”) and ensure equal treatment between male and women as compensation and other benefit beneficiaries.

Given that the higher education level of women in the Philippines, promoting the prioritising for CTF approval) the “women-led enterprises” will substantially capacitate the female entrepreneurs in the country to remove their structure barriers to the formal finances and credit as well as by strengthening their business skills. The programme also aims at addressing the limitations of many female- led enterprises stagnant in the small and micro-scale levels.

• STEM and Higher Education, and Gender in the Philippines

The Philippines has, according to UNESCO, an adult literacy rate of 98.18%. Only In a few countries found higher literacy rates than the Philippines. Among women aged 25 years or older, 98.24% of all women aged 15 years and older are literate, compared to 98.12% of men. 76.5% of adult women have reached at least a secondary level education, while the corresponding figure for men is 80%. Among those with tertiary education, there are more female graduates than males. The average Tertiary school enrollment was 35.49% in 2017. Among those who completed post-secondary courses, females (60.5%) outnumbered males (59.5%). Similarly, among those with post-baccalaureate courses, females (59.9%) outnumbered males (40.1%). Regarding STEM and higher enrollment rate, Filipinas reach around 18%.

Meanwhile, the World Economic Forum recorded an improvement in economic participation and opportunities for women in the Philippines from 2016 to 2017. Women earn less than men paid for similar work.

The Philippines ranks higher than any other Asia-Pacific country except New Zealand. However, qualified women are still underrepresented in the STEM fields. The gender gap between male and female workers with the same educational qualification and responsibilities are being paid less than men.

The Philippines is facing an overupply in information technology (IT) and needs more students to go into the science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields. The underlying of STEM education is a problem in the country. According to the World Bank, the Philippines ranks 128th in science and 132.85 in math and statistics. In the Philippines, women in STEM-related industries are often in low-skilled positions and are less likely to progress professionally.

### Vietnam

#### Legal and Institutional Setup and International Commitment of Vietnam

**International Agreements on Women’s Rights:** Vietnam is a signatory to the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948) and most of the key international instruments on gender equality. Vietnam participated in the Beijing World Conference on Women (1995) and its follow-up meetings. Vietnam was one of the founding members of the Commission on the Status of Women (CSW). Vietnam ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1982 but has not signed the Optional Protocol to CEDAW: Operational Program for the International Decade for Women: Action and Co-operation, the Declaration on Cultural Rights; and Operational Protocol to the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights.

At the regional level, Vietnam signed on the Declaration of the Advancement of Women in the ASEAN Region by 1985. The country has also ratified the ASEAN Charter on Gender Equality and effective participation in all fields and at all levels. The country also signed the Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women in the ASEAN region in 2012.

#### Institutions Arranged for the Promotion and Implementation of Gender Equality

The National Committee for the Advancement of Women (NCAFW) is the overarching gender equality initiative as the National Committee for the Advancement of Women (NCAFW), which coordinates gender mainstreaming as an inter-ministerial committee. Under NCAFW have been also created the provincial committees on women’s affairs (PCWAs) in all 64 provinces of the country.

The Vietnam Women’s Union (VWW) is a mass organization with strong links to the government. VWW also works closely together with NCAFW as the grassroots level and implements projects to improve women's lives and contribute to their economic development. In addition, at the central level, there are a number of central associations for women in various fields such as agriculture, education, health care, etc., as well as the mass party organization for women. At sub-national (provincial) levels, a Department of Labour, Invalids and Social Affairs (DOLISA), Department of Culture, Sports and Tourism (DGCS),


---
**Page 162**
---

Guia Consult Inc.

Provincial Women’s Union, Provincial Committee for the Advancement of Women exist. The Sub-provincial (district and commune) levels, there also exist similar structures.

National Action Plan on Prevention of Domestic Violence (2014-2020); the Project on the Reduction of Domestic Violence in Rural Areas of Vietnam (2015–2020); the Annual Plan to Mitigate Gender-Based Violence against Women and Girls (2016-2020) and a Programme to support gender equality among ethnic minorities in 2018-2025.

The Law on Support for Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (2017) stipulates putting greater emphasis on the recruitment of women workers, especially those employing more female workers. This is the very first time that the country’s laws and regulations explicitly mention gender equality as a priority in the context of SMEs.

Currently, the National Strategy on Gender Equality (2021–2030) sets specific targets for gender equality and meeting the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by 2030. Some of these targets include increasing female representation at all levels of government and local administration at all levels shall have female leaders; increase female employment engaged in paid work to 50% by 2025 and to approximately 69% by 2030; and reducing women’s poverty rate from the current level of 18.4% to 7.5% by 2030, compared with 14 times in 2030 compared to men.

Women and Climate Change: Vietnam adopted Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) was submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) with specific references to the gendered impacts of climate change. However, a comprehensive integration of gender in the policy frameworks underpinning NDCs is still lacking. The NDC does not include any specific targets or gender equality targets or measures identified to address women’s vulnerabilities in climate change. The updated NDC (2019) includes some references to gender equality and the need for adaptation strategies for rural women and stress that gender inequalities should not be exacerbated by the introduction of such technologies.

### General Situation Analysis of Vietnam

Vietnam is one of the fastest-growing economies in the world. The 2018 Population and Housing Census reported a population of over 96 million people, with 65% of Vietnam’s population being located in rural areas.37 Vietnam has been praised for its achievement of gender targets and the government has made significant progress towards achieving gender equality.

Such efforts have paid off with improved rankings in several global indices, including the Human Development Index and the Gender Development Index. In 2020, Vietnam’s HDI was 0.744 falling under the medium category. The country’s GDI is 0.615, falling into the low category. The country now ranks 117th out of 189 countries and territories. The 2020 female HDI value was 0.703 (0.475 for males). Vietnam has also been ranked as a high performer in the Gender Inequality Index, placing it into Group I countries.37 Vietnam has also taken tremendous steps towards becoming a lower-middle-income country and reducing poverty and achieving a low unemployment rate. OECD’s Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI),107 the level of discrimination against women is regarded as “low” (25%). Following the pattern of the index, the categories with higher discrimination are “gender-disparities in access to productive resources,” “financial resources,” and “reduced physical integrity,” scored at 32% and 31% respectively.107

The country still faces a gender imbalance regarding political decision-making. In 2020, the female representation rate was only 25%, which is significantly lower than the little improvement from 2009 (25.8%). Women are underrepresented across government bodies.106

Vietnam has a relatively high labour force participation rate (76.2%) with an average women’s participation of 43%. However, Vietnam still faces gender disparities in employment and earnings earned by women. While being highly economically active, women also bear a disproportionate amount of family responsibilities. One of the country’s challenges in the labour market is the quality of that employment rather than the share of employment.

In 2019, the agriculture sector accounted for 38.1% of female employment. Combined with the services sector, these represented the largest sectors of employment for women (56.8%). While roughly one-third of women work in agriculture and related activities, this is a significant decrease from previous years.

Since the COVID-19 pandemic outbreak, labour force participation has fallen sharply for both men and women, but especially for women. According to data released by the International Labour Organization (ILO), 64% of workers in the sectors that were hardest hit by the economic impacts of the pandemic are women. The most affected sectors include manufacturing, construction, wholesale trade, retail trade, and repair transport and communication; and arts and entertainment were worst hit.

It was reported that the ILO’s 171 partners have witnessed gender inequalities and GED in Vietnam.

Vietnam has made remarkable progress in eradicating illiteracy and providing universal education, with national literacy rates among women reaching almost 98% for both boys and girls in general education. The literacy rate of the population aged 25 or above is about 95%, while that of women aged 15–64 is only 96.3%, which is largely due to the implementation of literacy programs and reduced gender inequality in access to education, as explained in the Action Plan on Gender Equality in Education 2018-2020.107

• Climate change and gender in Vietnam

Vietnam ranked 38th in the Global Climate Risk Index for 2019 with 15% in the Climate Risk Index for 2000–2019, which is above the global average of 14%. The most significant risks have been generated by climate change-related disasters while incurring an annual national economic loss of approximately 1.5% GDP of Vietnam. These include floods and landslides, droughts, storms, and sea-level rise.

The country’s vulnerability to climate change is increasing due to its geographical location on the continental coast, where ecosystems, biodiversity and water resources are at risk and related economic sectors such as agriculture, public health and infrastructure are increasingly more vulnerable. The poor, ethnic minorities, women and children are particularly vulnerable to these risks.

**Page 165**

**Women and Agriculture:** According to a report conducted by FAO (2019), agriculture contributes 21% of Vietnam’s GDP, with women representing about 73% of the country's workforce. Women are an important labour force in agricultural production, especially in rural areas where about 63% of the women work as farmers and workers. However, women in rural areas are particularly hard hit by climate change in Vietnam and women are disproportionately impacted because they lack the resources to adapt.

Regarding rural women, the Vietnamese government is working on providing a preferential credit instrument to support women in agriculture. However, it is reported that men are the foremost beneficiaries of this policy as they have more access to land and other productive assets such as tools and machinery. Women also face additional burdens in performing household tasks like tillage, harvesting, processing and transport. It is reported that in Vietnam, climate change also intensifies women’s double burden in having to fulfill both productive and reproductive roles.

**Women and Energy:** Over the past few decades, Vietnam has emerged as an important oil and natural gas producer, contributing 10% of the country's total energy supply for the country. At the same time, however, Vietnam’s renewable energy sector is one of the most vibrant in Southeast Asia. The country has a large potential to generate electricity from hydropower (and bioenergy) as the total primary energy supply in the country.

While the country is providing almost full electrification to their households, clean energy solutions are not available to everyone, and women are considered more vulnerable in this regard. As they take on traditional roles of cooking and cleaning within the household, women are particularly affected by indoor pollution and climate change might extend the travel distance to get fuel because of the increased scarcity of natural resources.

**Gender roles make women the focal point of energy consumption in households,** while they are still marginalized in terms of decision-making at the community level about energy and they are not well integrated into the energy sector. In Vietnam, traditional gender stereotypes in science, technology, and engineering are traditionally seen as suitable only for men; thus, women are significantly underrepresented.

# Women and Water

Vietnam has a dense river network of over 230 rivers with lengths greater than 10 km. Households' access to improved water supplies increased from 65% in 2008 to 95% in 2017, which is attributed to the government's efforts and international aid.

### Women's Role

Women are responsible for managing household water resources, including rainwater harvesting, water collection, and sanitation. However, women face challenges such as limited access to clean water during menstruation, pregnancy, and caregiving.

### Water Management

- **Water supply**: Rivers provide abundant water supplies.
- **Waste management**: Women manage solid waste collection and treatment rates at 86% (2019), with 53% of households using improved sanitation facilities. However, only 7% use municipal sanitation services.
- **Recycling activities**: Women are involved in informal recycling activities.

### Environmental Issues

Water quality remains a concern due to pollution from industrial and agricultural sources. The Ministry of Health and Environment reported that 80% of diseases in Vietnam are caused by polluted water (199).

### Conclusion

Women play a crucial role in managing water resources, but they face significant challenges related to access to clean water and sanitation.


---
**Page 167**
---

# Women and Education: Regarding tertiary education, Vietnam has a total gross enrollment ratio of 28.9% (2016), with more girls than male students attending school in upper secondary and tertiary levels (31.5% vs. 28.5%).

However, women's participation in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) education is less than half of that of men’s (15.38% vs. 31.4%), underrepresenting women in the sector of STEM jobs. Women are also less likely to be involved in research and development for technological innovation. This reflects the traditional expectations imposed upon girls and boys when it comes to their future career paths.

According to UNESCO, among students enrolled in tertiary education, men are considerably more likely to specialize in engineering, manufacturing, construction, and services, while women are more likely to choose social sciences, health care, teaching, and early childhood education. Women also tend to choose vocational training programs that lead to traditionally woman-dominated occupations, limiting their career prospects in innovation and emerging roles and other economic opportunities.

Moreover, women face barriers in higher education, such as limited access to scholarships, manufacturing jobs, garment and footwear industries, the service sector, education field, and paid domestic work, among others. This is a major obstacle for women's participation in STEM fields.

## Business Entrepreneurship and Gender Dynamics in Vietnam

The entrepreneurial ecosystem of Vietnam is by far one of the most advanced among the Mekong countries and ranked 87th out of 137 countries in the 2018 Global Entrepreneurship Index (GEI). Over the past decade, Vietnam has made significant progress in promoting entrepreneurship and the role of women entrepreneurs. Since 1996, with the introduction of the Private Enterprises Law, the Vietnamese government has been actively supporting female entrepreneurs to start their own businesses being mentioned for the first time in the 2017 Law on Support for Small and Medium-sized Enterprises.

The statistics, however, are not very encouraging if we take a deeper look at women's entrepreneurial participation. Based on the 2014 (2002 Enterprise Census), Vietnam’s women own about 21% of formal enterprises with more than five employees, while men own 79%. In addition, there were only 856 large enterprises. Since 2020, half of these businesses were partially suspended or dissolved given the sharp decline in business activities due to the COVID-19 pandemic. The number of women-owned enterprises reported a revenue decrease of 75%, or more, which is double that of losses compared to men-owned enterprises.

An IFC study in 2017 on the needs and opportunities for women-owned SMEs in Vietnam, stated that Vietnam's investment climate for women is generally supportive. According to it, Vietnam has a long history of promoting gender equality and has made significant progress in reducing the gap between males and women’s legal positions in doing business. Whereas it is assumed that women and men share similar skills and experiences when running their companies, there are a multitude of challenges faced by female entrepreneurs in Vietnam that affect women's ability to realize their goals.

entrepreneurial ambitions. **108** Thus, even though men are relatively more likely to be employed in the informal sector (the informal employment rate in 2019 was 47% for women and 58.9% among men), women entrepreneurs have a higher likelihood of being self-employed.

Vietnam's labour market still has areas of gender-segregation, with women heavily represented in marketing, office and support work, human resources, and accounting, rather than in technology and innovation sectors where they are underrepresented. This limits their access to resources and opportunities for business development. The challenges of balancing business with family-care constraints can be particularly difficult for women who have to manage childcare while running their businesses. **109** Research conducted by ActionAid Vietnam reported that on average, Vietnamese women spent 108 hours per week on household chores compared to men's 45 hours.

The lack of equal division of labour between men and women constitutes a considerable brake on women’s labour participation and return to livelihoods, limiting their opportunities for training, networking, and promotion.

Lack of Financing: In general, women entrepreneurs find it more difficult to access credit. Even though female entrepreneurs bring in average annual revenues like men's 42% of women-owned SMEs had difficulty accessing finance compared with only 15% of men-owned businesses. This is partly because the time, and small-scale nature of women’s enterprises along with the fact that these kinds of business are often run by women who have no collateral to offer as security for loans. Women are also less likely than men to be able to provide a guarantor or co-signer when applying for loans. **109** Women's capacity to access finance is also influenced by cultural factors. Usually, women do not have the same level of financial literacy and business acumen as their male counterparts and are unable to fulfill these criteria since their names are mostly not included on the state-issued land use right certificates or other documents that can be used for collateral.

Women entrepreneurs in Vietnam face a number of challenges when it comes to accessing finance. They are often unable to access land usage rights, and in practice, women have less land rights and smaller farm holdings than men. Women also face restrictions on their ability to access financing through informal sources of finance, while accessing legal financial products remains a privilege for larger enterprises.

Provincial Women's Union, provincial People’s Party, and other government departments have developed several action plans on women's entrepreneurship and renewable energy in supporting the NUS. Government agencies such as the Department of Science and Technology (DST), the Ministry of Industry and Trade (MoIT) led by the Vietnam Women’s Union, also created a special category for renewable energy in 2020, with UNDP, ILO, and other international organizations providing technical assistance to support women entrepreneurs' initiative in three provinces in Vietnam to use renewable energy as a means for developing their businesses.

Innovation is an important aspect of the success of many entrepreneurial enterprises, and it is so for climate technology. Vietnam continues to be an example for other developing countries in training innovative women entrepreneurs and supporting them in scaling up their business by providing own NUS. In 2020, Vietnam had a ranking of 44 on the Global Innovation Index (GII) and ranking the lower middle-income group.

In Vietnam, the Programme will address the above-listed constraints in the following manner:

Given the equal/higher education level of women in Vietnam, promoting (by prioritizing for CTF approval) the "women-led enterprises" will substantially capacitate the female entrepreneurs in the country towards achieving their goals and objectives. The Programme will also support them to expand their business network and partnership to the global level and address the limitations of many female-led enterprises in accessing finance and markets, as well as the gender stereotypes, discrimination, and bias of women's roles in the climate technology sector and aggressive business entrepreneurship required by the climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. Generating a successful case(s) of the climate technology innovative RDMB case of one locally-based "women-led enterprise" through the Programme will trigger this perception change in the long term.

### Priority Sectors and Gender Implications in Vietnam

Areas for priority intervention through the proposed Programme in Vietnam are, among others: Climate scenarios analysis with a focus on gender equality; climate-smart agriculture; Intra-reservoir operation skills for water-efficient irrigation operation/Environmental Technology for Water.

Climate-smart agricultural activities include, among others, smart water and irrigation management; adoption of improved crop varieties; agroforestry; intercropping trees with crops; sustainable land management practices; soil conservation; integrated pest management; improved livestock production; and improved agro-climate information services.[21] Investments in climate adaptation and mitigation technologies will also be encouraged to increase the resilience of smallholder and female women. Agriculture contributes 21% of Vietnam's total GDP, with a female employment rate of about 63%. The Programme should consider women's climate vulnerability and climate risk exposure, while Vietnam is one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change impacts, especially in relation to commercial development.

Waste management could also be considered a priority sector for Vietnamese women. In the country, waste collection and treatment systems are already developed and women account for 56% of formal waste collectors, dominating the informal sector with a 65% participation rate.[22] Investment in recycling technologies will also be encouraged to increase the resilience of smallholder and female women, shifting the informal to formal sector, improving their income generation and work conditions. Promoting women's status in the waste management sector is also important for increasing the country's potential opportunity for GHG reduction and pollution mitigation while empowering women in Vietnam.

In Vietnam, gender mainstreaming into the policies and plans in agriculture, waste management, water management and renewable energy sectors need to be strengthened. Currently, there are no gender equality indicators or targets included in the national climate change adaptation strategy and policy change. The proposed Programme could consider strengthening the capacity of the relevant government entities in developing gender-sensitive strategies and integrating gender considerations at policy and plan levels, while developing sector-specific guidelines for gender mainstreaming[23].

# Final Considerations and Recommendations

The following are the summaries of the key findings and recommendations from this gender assessment.

## Agricultural Sector

In Cambodia and Indonesia, women contribute to more than 70% of the agricultural workforce, followed by Vietnam (68%) and Lao PDR (over 50%). Only in the Philippines, agriculture is male dominated. Women are responsible for most of the food production and processing activities that threaten the livelihoods and food security of millions of smallholder farmers and agribusinesses who depend on agriculture as their main source of income, especially during the lean season.

The proposed Programme aims to enhance climate-resilient RDMB support for all five countries. Prioritization of the sector will significantly affect the girls and women in the countries. Also, the potential is high to engage women entrepreneurs given the high level of women’s participation in the sector.

## Water Sector

Gender sensitivity extends to the water sector, water-related natural calamities, and water-borne disease outbreaks. Indonesian women, for example, are the principal water collectors and providers in their home: two out of every five Indonesian families delegate water responsibility to women. In Lao PDR, 42% of households with children under five years old have at least one girl child. Gender responsibilities related to water have also been found to be strongly defined in rural areas, especially for Lao and Vietnam. Women’s access to safe drinking water is a critical issue. For example, women’s access to water supplies and sanitation, where in Vietnam, households’ access to improved water supplies increased from 50% in the year of 1998 to 76% by 2013.

Women’s role in water management activities should be strengthened with proper training and skill development to cope with climate change-related water disasters, such as floods and droughts. Women can become central agents for Sustainable development and management of water supplies and water treatment facilities. Women could reduce the water contamination putting the lives of millions of men, women, boys, and girls at risk.

## Energy Sector

In all five countries, clean energy solutions are available to everyone and women are considered less privileged. Given the traditional household roles of cooking fuel and collection, women’s health is more affected by indoor pollution, and women’s time is overspent in collecting fuel because of the increased cost of firewood. Women also dominate the informal sector in the energy sector such as manufacturing, construction, and engineering sectors where women are less represented in all of the five countries analysed.

While most women are affected by the types and quality of the energy sources, girls and women are not active agents in energy transitions in all the five countries, due to gender bias in education (especially in STEM, ICT and energy subjects) as well as cultural tendencies to educate male children to higher levels. Therefore, the Programme needs to proactively invite women’s participation in renewable energy-related enterprises.


---
**Page 171**
---

# Waste Management Sector

Women’s limited participation is also observed across the countries in environmentally friendly solutions in waste management. In Indonesia, fewer women participate in the waste collection chain and in the informal sector of waste recycling. Women are more likely to collect garbage from their homes at distances away from home on trucks and carry heavy loads. In Vietnam, women represent 35-40% of the workforce in the informal sector of waste management, but this work is often characterized as unclean, unhygienic and often dangerous. In Cambodia and Lao PDR, solid waste management has recently gained importance due to the rapid urbanization and expansion of cities. Women are increasingly expected for women to make waste disposal and recycling initiatives a good opportunity for women’s employment and poverty reduction in these countries (see also Circular Economy).

## Women's Business Entrepreneurship

In all five countries, the informal sector and entrepreneurial ecosystem seemed to be limited to small and microenterprises, characterized by low profitability and informality. Common barriers are: (i) social and cultural constraints; (ii) access to business models; and (iii) lack of financial services. Women face a double burden of unpaid care work and income-generating activities.

Regarding the (ii) access to the business market, in all the countries’ labour markets, there is gender segregation, with women heavily represented in the agricultural and sales sectors, rather than technology and engineering. This limits their access to high-value-added products and opportunities for business development. The challenges of balancing business with family care constrain women’s time to participate in networking and learning activities and further grow their businesses.

In Indonesia, women's access to financial programs is limited compared to men's access. Indonesian males tend to have more access to formal financial services (45%) than females (30%). Women also use informal financial services, compared to males (29%). Men and women do not have equal ownership rights over community-based microfinance programs, making it difficult for women to save capital or obtain loans from financial institutions. In Lao PDR, women are also less likely to have a bank account or credit line. In the Philippines, although legally required, some financial institutions still discriminate against women borrowers. Women in Indonesia and the Philippines, entrepreneurs seeking micro-finance schemes, mostly encounter restrictive and complicated procedures, which make them turn to informal and expensive means for credit. Due to their lack of collateral, a bank loan is often inaccessible to women.

## STEM and Higher Education

In Indonesia, Philippines and Vietnam the level of literacy rates is comparatively high, with no significant gender gap (see Figure 1). On the other hand, Cambodia and Lao PDR had low literacy rates with marked gender differences. 

In Indonesia, Philippines and Vietnam, women’s tertiary enrolment ratio fell from 6 to 9 percentage points higher than that for men. Nevertheless, women's participation in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields is still very limited. In the Philippines, for example, only 10% of female students are represented in the sector of information communication technology (ICT) and other areas related to STEM.

This reflects the traditional expectations imposed upon girls and boys when it comes to choosing their study path or occupation. According to UNESCO, among students enrolled in tertiary education, men are constantly overrepresented in science, engineering, mathematics and computer sciences fields, while women are more likely to specialize in social sciences, education, humanities, and the arts. Additionally, women are under-represented in leadership positions within these sectors, especially in male-dominated occupations, limiting their career prospects in non-traditional and emerging roles and other areas of economic activity.


---
**Page 172**
---

Gaia Consult Inc.

economic opportunities. Moreover, women achieving higher education tend still to be clustered in lower-paid jobs, earning less than their male counterparts.[1]

Regarding education, the key barriers for women’s enrolment and STEM education have been identified as mostly financial constraints and lack of role models. Women are particularly vulnerable to these gender stigmas as rural women and girls, which are more likely to experience gender-based violence and early marriage, are more susceptible to premature withdrawals from schooling.

Decision-making & Empowerment

It is evident that numerous women in Southeast Asia are in underprivileged situations in terms of access and benefits from natural resources and environmental decision-making processes in comparison to men. Women in the five countries are not exceptions.

Recommendations: In light of the above findings regarding links related to women and climate entrepreneurship in the countries of analysis, the following recommendations should be taken into account while implementing the Collaborative R&D Programme for Promoting the Innovation of Climate Technopreneurship:

**Component 1**

Country-Driven Climate Acceleration Readiness

* Local SME selection shall prioritise candidate SMIs within each of the five target countries which meet the “women-owned SMI” (Defined as: a) at least one woman is an owner or co-owner of the total capital owned by women; b) at least one woman participates on the Board of Directors and/or different types of boards or internal committees, including but not limited to the Supervisory Board, COO, CTOs; or c) with more than 30% of the workforce are females)
* Each country SMI is recommended to hire one gender specialist, under the guidance of the Gender Specialist at the Secretariat
* Country-specific acceleration support programs (NCEAPs) should include gender awareness component and support the participating firms to assess their own gender mainstreaming readiness status.

**Component 2**

Global Acceleration for Collaborative R&D&B

* Every selected PVI will submit its own gender mainstreaming strategy -4 elements: a) Gender equality policy; b) Gender action plan; c) Gender action plan for their CTP Proposal. (If not, to support this. To be used)
* The programme should set up a fund-level gender expertise in the Global Acceleration Initiative Secretariat.
* Integration of gender into collaborative R&D&B services: Fund-level Gender Expertise and participating PVIs services to prepare gender analysis (GA) and gender action plan (GAP) in accordance with the GCT’s Gender Policy (R.2012 adopted in Nov 2019)

### Component 3
