# Appendix 5: Sector/Technology-specific Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming for CTF Enterprises (cont.)

## Renewable Energy

### Renewable Energy (Solar PV) Sector in the Five Countries

#### Cambodia

Situated in the "global sun belt" [1], Cambodia is well-positioned to utilize solar energy. The government has provided clarity in the sector by allowing electricity generation for self-consumption through off-grid systems, which are increasingly being adopted by rural households and businesses. Efforts on connecting rural farms and fisheries to affordable off-grid solutions have increased, and key mechanisms such as the Switch to Solar Incubation Programme and Impact Hub's DaikDam incubator programme have been established in Phnom Penh and Siem Reap provinces [2]. Cambodia’s submission to the UNCCD includes conditional targets for the energy, industry, transport, building, agriculture, and waste sectors. The government has also launched a Solar Energy Development Strategy (SEDS) that aims to rural areas, which requires solar home systems, DC and AC microgrids, and solar battery charging stations [3]. This strategy is expected to contribute significantly to climate-resilient agricultural production through increased access to solar irrigation systems, using solar water pumps.

#### Indonesia

The ROKB Program prioritizes the use of solar photovoltaic (PV) technology as a key climate technology. In Indonesia, solar PV technology ranks as the top priority among renewable energy technologies in terms of cost, environmental benefit, economic impact, technology maturity, country ownership, and ease of implementation [4]. The government has also issued regulations to promote the use of clean and renewable energy, particularly solar energy. Solar PV technology is considered the most mature commodity in terms of regulation, market, and technology, with various energy service providers (ESPs) offering financing options for solar PV systems.

Solar PV technology has the potential to be utilized in remote and off-grid areas across Indonesia, with direct applications to households and industries. The government has also issued regulations to promote the use of clean and renewable energy, particularly solar energy [5]. This includes the Energy Efficiency Plan and the Provision of Energy-Efficient Solar Lamps for People Who Have Not Access to Electricity.

Solar Photovoltaic (PV) is too high a potential to be implemented in Indonesia. See Table 1.B.42 of the Pre-Feasibility Study of the Program (Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal), in the form of the following


---
**Page 202**
---

### Low-cost smart grid system solar PV, local industry to build components of the solar modules, user-friendly apps to increase solar PV investment/installation, micro-inverter solar panel.

#### [Lao PDR]
The renewable energy sector in Lao PDR is experiencing significant growth, with solar technology being the dominant sub-sector. The country's prospective technology needs for climate mitigation include solar-powered irrigation systems, drones, and smart farms, which are cost-efficient compared to traditional methods. The Philippine government has also prioritized renewable energy technologies as part of its climate-smart agriculture (CSA) initiatives.

#### [Philippines]
The agricultural sector in the Philippines has potential for growth through the implementation of green technology, particularly in climate-smart agriculture (CSA) initiatives. CSA can be achieved through solar-powered irrigation systems, drones, and smart farms, which are cost-efficient compared to traditional methods. The Philippine government has also prioritized renewable energy technologies as part of its climate-smart agriculture (CSA) initiatives.

#### [Vietnam]
Vietnam has made significant strides in renewable energy, particularly in wind and solar, over the past three years. By the end of 2021, Vietnam had over 9.5 GW of rooftop solar capacity connected to the national power grid, making it one of the top 10 countries with the highest capacity of installed solar energy. Solar technology is predicted to account for USD 3 billion of the renewable energy sector's market value, which is expected to reach USD 74 billion by 2050.

#### Gender Perspective in Renewable Energy Sector (Solar PV)
Among the diverse renewable energy sectors, solar PV is a globally leading sector of gender-balanced employment. The solar sector has about double women’s employment rate compared to wind and oil and gas sectors, in 2021.


---
**Page 203**
---

# General Requirements

All the requirements under the CTF Investment Criteria Gender Component (See Table 1 of the Gender Assessment) shall apply to all the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/enterprises subject to the CTF approval. Thus, "women-led enterprises" on the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/enterprises should be encouraged to be involved in the project implementation. It is also recommended that women should be encouraged to hire more female employees (with equal working conditions and remuneration as male employees) for the project implementation.

All the CTF staff shall need to submit a simplified gender assessment and gender action plan [[Appendix 3: Simplified Gender Analysis (GA) & Gender Action Plan (GAP)](General Template)] of the Gender Assessment/Gender Action Plan (GAGAP), Annex 8 of the Funding Proposal of the Programme/Project. The "Gender Analysis" should include the specific local gender perspectives of a specific project.

It is also recommended that women should be encouraged to hire more female employees (with equal working conditions and remuneration as male employees) for the project implementation.

All the CTF staff shall need to submit a simplified gender assessment and gender action plan [[Appendix 3: Simplified Gender Analysis (GA) & Gender Action Plan (GAP)](General Template)] of the Gender Assessment/Gender Action Plan (GAGAP), Annex 8 of the Funding Proposal of the Programme/Project. The "Gender Analysis" should include the specific local gender perspectives of a specific project.

### Recommended Gender-mainstreaming approaches

All the requirements under the CTF Investment Criteria Gender Component (See Table 1 of the Gender Assessment) shall apply to all the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/enterprises subject to the CTF approval. Thus, "women-led enterprises" on the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/enterprises should be encouraged to be involved in the project implementation. It is also recommended that women should be encouraged to hire more female employees (with equal working conditions and remuneration as male employees) for the project implementation.

All the CTF staff shall need to submit a simplified gender assessment and gender action plan [[Appendix 3: Simplified Gender Analysis (GA) & Gender Action Plan (GAP)](General Template)] of the Gender Assessment/Gender Action Plan (GAGAP), Annex 8 of the Funding Proposal of the Programme/Project. The "Gender Analysis" should include the specific local gender perspectives of a specific project.

# Sector-specific Gender Mainstreaming Considerations

## Gender Action 1. Consider gender-specific occupational EHS plan

Female workers in manufacturing plants and assembling solar PV systems, and installation may face different ergonomic challenges compared to male counterparts. This is due to differences in body strength than their male counterparts. Heights are also one of the significant issues in occupational health and safety for female workers. For example, the average height of a woman in the United States is 5'4". The issue becomes more pronounced when considering the installation of Solar Roof Installations increase; this issue is deemed to have resulted in higher fatality rates for Solar Installation projects outside the United States because of more lax fire safety regulations.[10] With connection points and other components being installed at heights, female workers are more likely to be susceptible to injuries due to infrastructure build without consideration of female-body ergonomics.

### Possible solutions include:

- Conduct a gender-sensitive risk assessment. Conducting a gender-sensitive risk assessment can identify potential hazards and risks for female workers and provide recommendations for addressing them[10] (e.g., flexibility of work hours of female workers under the same or higher production targets in areas of assembly).
- Promote gender equality in design. Ergonomics should be considered in the design phase of infrastructure projects to ensure that they are inclusive of all workers, including women. This includes selecting equipment and tools that are better suited to the female body, such as smaller and lighter equipment.
- Produce health and safety products specifically designed for female workers (e.g., PPEs for average Asian female bodies, etc.).
- Ensure "zero-tolerance of SEAH and GBV" in all workplaces.

# Agriculture Sector in the Five Countries

## Cambodia

Agriculture is a key driver of Cambodia’s economy, making up an estimated 23.1% of the country's GDP in 2019 (World Bank). The sector highlights as a critical vulnerability to changes in national climate patterns, including ENSO; it is as highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, such as flooding and drought. Moreover, farmers are highly vulnerable to the rising temperatures caused by climate change, which can lead to reduced crop yields.

### Climate-resilient agriculture technology

The top adaptation technology priority in Cambodia, marking full co-benefits in terms of adaptation impacts, economic, social, environmental and gender considerations (see Table 1.4.3 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the R&D Programme).

Several programmes and initiatives are taking place including smart agriculture initiatives, supported by both public and private sectors. The government is also focusing on increasing technology innovation in agriculture sector in Cambodia is focusing on technology and apps for farmers, assuming a large number of farmers do not have access to information or training. However, the demand need be strengthened. So far, however, smart agriculture has not been extensively reached out to the Cambodian rural communities and has significant potential for scaling with proper incentives.

## Indonesia

Three out of five Indonesian live in rural areas with agriculture as their predominant occupation. Food security, self-sufficiency and increasing agricultural productivity are the main focus of government agriculture policy. While Indonesia's 2021-2024 National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN) selected climate change adaptation as a priority area for the next four years, it is still in its nascent stage in Indonesia, and the agritech is attracting least amount of funding thus far. (See Figure 1.8.4 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the R&DDB Programme).

The country faces a number of challenges related to climate change including extreme weather conditions, such as extreme heating and flooding. Due to decreasing water availability, the country’s rice production is projected to decline by more than 25% in several provinces. The priority agritech technologies are: (1) Climate-smart agriculture technology (CSAT); (2) Technology for sustainable land management; (3) Technology of efficient irrigation (intermittent, SRI, PTT, etc.) and water harvesting; (4) Technology of efficient fertilizer use (see Table 1.8.5 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the R&DDB Programme).

## Laos PDR

In Lao PDR, more than two-thirds of the workforce is employed in agriculture, mostly in small-scale farming. The country is highly exposed to climate-related hazards and is expected to get worse over time. Temperatures in Lao PDR are predicted to increase by 3°C against baseline conditions before the end of the century; while agriculture yields could decrease significantly, driven by the incidence of extreme heat during the growing season (while rainfall may increase).

To reduce sector GHG emissions, the Lao government aims to: (1) enhance low-emission and environmentally friendly practices, such as smart and precise farming, feeds and feeding


---
**Page 206**
---

Livestock management: (2) adopt water management practices at 50,000 hectares in livestock production, and (3) increase animal feed by 1.4 million tonnes by 2024, including for feed and concentrates that reduce emissions. (See Table 1.E6 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the RABD Programme.) The country also aims to: (1) promote climate resilience in farming systems and agricultural infrastructure, and (2) promote sustainable land management practices for soil conservation, water management, and carbon sequestration. The country has a great potential for soil carbon sequestration. To achieve this, the country needs to shift from widely-used burning practices to more sustainable land management practices. Capacity building and training is required for the farming population to recognize the advance of soil carbon sequestration techniques.

Through extensive consultations and prioritization exercises (incl ARBP), the following four technologies in agriculture were identified as top priority for CFF support: (1) Early warning system and insurance, (2) Application of digital management technology, (3) Livestock disease control technology, and; (4) Pest management/control technology.

| Philippines |
| --- |

The Philippines Innovation Act of 2019 (RA 11293) fosters agriculture as one of the priority areas for fostering innovation and internationalization activities of MSMEs. The country has also climate-smart agriculture (CSA) as a key component in its NDC. With Renewable energy-powered post-harvesting and processing equipment technology to address particular needs of smallholder farmers, particularly those who are not connected to the grid or use solar power, is one example as it can be used for drying, cooling, and pasteurization (see Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the RABD Programme).

| Vietnam |
| --- |

Viet Nam is an agriculture-based country, where cultivated land for farming makes up nearly 40% of the total land mass, and about 20 million people work in the agriculture sector (about 40% of the total employment), contributing 17% of the national GDP. However, in general, the agriculture sector has been receiving less attention from the government compared to other sectors.

The Vietnamese government is driving initiatives for smart rural, smart village and smart agriculture with an aim to create more efficient and modern rural services through inclusive digital transformation. Priority areas include: (1) Smart farming; (2) Climate-smart agriculture (CSA); (3) Local poultry production; (4) Integrated livestock rearing models including small-scale breeding into concentrated breeding; (5) Integrated rice farming models: rice-dryland crop rotation and integrated rice-fish systems. (See Table 1.E2 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the RABD Programme).

For mitigation actions, (1) ICM for annual upland crop cultivation has been opted in priority, followed by: (2) Shifting from double/triple rice cropping systems to rice–aquaculture farming system; (3) Improved livestock rearing practices and integrated livestock-rearing models; (4) Use of agricultural/residues. (See Table 1.E30 of the Pre-Feasibility Study, Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the RABD Programme).

## Gender Perspective in Agriculture Sector

In many developing countries, the agriculture sector has women’s faces. In Cambodia and Indonesia, the agricultural labor force is predominantly female by sex (about 80% in Cambodia & 70-80% in Indonesia respectively). More than 60% of Vietnamese women work in the agriculture sector and more than 59% of rural women are engaged in agriculture in India. In contrast, only about 12% of the agricultural population are women in the Philippines. The minority female farmers in the Philippines are concentrated in subsistence farming and food for family consumption, whereas men primarily focus on cash crops.

With varying degrees, across the countries, climate hazards threaten the livelihoods and food security of millions of smallholder farmers and agribusinesses who depend on agriculture in our selected countries, especially women. Women face a double burden of unpaid work and are more vulnerable to poverty in disadvantaged positions compared with their male counterparts. Women’s activities are often under-valued due to gender biases and lack of recognition. The challenges faced by women entrepreneurs generated by socio-cultural, traditional bias and gender roles, which figures males for land ownership, access to credit, technology and extension services. In the Philippines, for example, in the agriculture sector is known to be a sector where male-dominated old customs are traditions are perpetuated more strongly than other industries.

Given the limited education and resources available to the rural farming women in the five countries, it is of utmost importance that they have access to training, extension services and agricultural enterprises through the Programme and ensure their equal benefitting from it.


### Recommended Gender-mainstreaming approaches

#### <3> General Requirements

All the requirements under the CTP Investment Criteria: Gender Component (See Table 1 of the Gender Assessment/Action Plan) will apply to the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/entities subject to the CTP approval. Thus “women-led enterprises” on the EWS and Natural Disaster Technology application projects/entities need to be established. It is also encouraged to hire more female employees (with equal working conditions and remuneration as male employees) and improve women employees’ welfare policies.

#### <3> Sector-specific Gender Mainstreaming Considerations

**Gender Action 1:** To the extent possible, aim to set the women’s participation targets as proportionate to the women’s share of sector participation. 

In countries with predominantly higher level of participation of women in agriculture such as Cambodia (about 74%), Indonesia (70-89%), and Lao PDR (more than 50%) the Programme shall aim to increase the country’s capacity for gender mainstreaming in the agricultural sector. This can be achieved by: 1) all applicants agricultural startups and firms which are “women-owned” (in Component 1), and/or 2) more women entrepreneurs being employed in the proposed projects under the Gender Mainstreaming Component Plan (GMCP).


---
**Page 208**
---

# Gender Action 2: Proactively Tap into the women farmers' insights for innovation and identification of gender-responsive technologies

By sheer size of women occupying their hands-on experiences in the farming sector, women are a reservoir of the traditional and indigenous agricultural technologies and practices. While male farmers are more likely to be involved with large-scale commercial agriculture (e.g., rubber plantations), informal and small-scale subsistence farming has been similar with mixed farming, agroforestry, and livestock rearing. Women's participation in these activities should be the leading participants from the very planning and design stage of the project preparation.

# In Component 2: In the JV enterprise preparation stage, the Programme shall ensure the consultation activity here more or equal number of women as men at community level. Also, in the draft implementation plan, the JV enterprises for CTF application should ensure more or equal representation of women in consultation groups.


# Gender Action 3: Consciously posit women as the prototype participants and beneficiaries of the funded enterprises/offices.

Given the majority of farmers are women in most countries (except the Philippines), it is advisable that whatever business plans and designs of concerned enterprises/offices under CTF support, should assume some form of gender equality. The rationale for this is that women's participation needs to be clearly and consciously articulated due to our deeply entrenched perception posing (often while also adding value) women as secondary actors in the farming sector from the very proposal and conceptualization state. This will provide gender technology innovation.

# In Components 2 and 3, the JV enterprises in the agriculture sector shall explicitly posit women in the target country(ies) as a prototype target group for technology applications and should be reflected in their business plans, including the design of their operations and planning. Such approaches shall be explicitly reflected in the plan of the JV enterprises (in the proposed documents) during CTF application.

### Water Sector in the Five Countries
[Cambodia]

Cambodia remains a largely agrarian country with its high population dependent on subsistence farming for their livelihoods which leaves them highly vulnerable to climate shocks. ADB (2021) mentions it in its report on the great potential for the application of new water and agricultural technologies.

including drip irrigation, water harvesting, biomass and rural energy. [10] In this sense, water resource management technology remains highly relevant as one of the prioritized adaptation technologies critical for maintaining "the fertility of productive agricultural lands for food production, fisheries, and other biodiversity resources" [11].

### Indonesia

Indonesia identifies water resource management as one of the priority sectors for the adaptation technologies. The government has identified a total of 20 adaptation technologies in the sector; water resource management is selected as the highest in its prioritized adaptation technologies in the water sector, which results in improving health and well-being, and food and water security.

### Laos PDR

Laos PDR is a country rich in water resources. Flooding is a major cause of climate change-related disasters in Laos PDR, and it affects the quality of water, the loss of lives, and the livelihoods. The most vulnerable people are those living along rivers and lakes. [12] In this context, food security is a critical issue for Laos PDR. [13] 75% of Laos PDR's population relies on agriculture for their livelihood, and food security could be endangered if water demands for the agriculture sector that cannot be met. The food security-water nexus is a major challenge in Laos PDR. [14] In this context, the government has set a conditional target for the agriculture sector to develop 50,000 hectares of adjusted water management practices by 2030. [15] This target is expected to be achieved through investments that are channeled both from a climate-resilient infrastructure angle and a water resource/water security angle. [16]

The RADB Programme highlights investment priority on watershed management technology, hydropower generation, and efficient water supply technology to enhance the resilience of the water resources sector in Laos PDR.

### Philippines

In the Philippines, the gap between the available water supply and demand continues to widen. About 12.0 million people are limited to getting water from small sources. Some areas do not have water service even though they are within a few kilometers of an existing water source. [17] This is because the required services to a growing population. In addition, only 51% or 68 percent of the country's 748 water distribution systems are in good condition and fully functional. [18] Furthermore, the effects of climate change exacerbate the country’s water challenges. In this context, small-scale operational projects that are supported by the RADB Programme are highly relevant for the supply of fresh water, particularly in water-scarce regions as one of the prioritized adaptation technologies.

# Gender Perspective in the Water Sector

In most developing countries, women and girls are the primary providers, managers and users of water at household level, often without access to safe drinking water.

Traditionally, the grid-connected, large-scale water supply, distribution and wastewater treatment infrastructure development was heavily male-dominated areas due to its engineering/technical requirements. Women were not considered as a target group for physical labourers (except in some countries where women (low-income households) comprised between most of the physical labourers (with more precarious working conditions often without formal labour contracts, in informal sectors of the economy and in remote rural areas). In addition, women are also responsible for water collection at household level (such nearby the construction sites, etc.).

In the context of developing countries a significant share of a country’s population still remains without access to safe drinking water or processed water through modernized water supply and treatment system. neither they are in the protection of their health and hygiene through proper wastewater treatment systems. Lack of sufficient and clean and safe water provision is more acute in remote rural and isolated locations.

In these settings, it is often girls and women who spend time for water access at household levels: Women as well as girls are responsible for collecting water from distant sources and performing related activities, contributing to the situation of “time poverty”241, deepening gender inequality in development. Moreover, women and girls face an increase in vulnerability and Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in water points and sanitation facilities due to their proximity with these facilities and limited access to safe water sources. In this regard, a meaningful engagement of women as water service beneficiaries such as in productive uses of water and sanitation services is essential for the empowerment of women and the access to safe water and improved sanitation.

### Gender Action Plan (GAP)

#### Section II -2: Gender Analysis

Gender Action 1: Involve women in the development and maintenance of community-level water system

Water projects designed and implemented with full women’s participation at all phases of planning, design, construction, management of water and sanitation are more sustainable and effective. As women continue to bear the primary responsibility for the food, care, and health of the household, women have a critical role in ensuring that water systems meet their families’ basic needs. As such, placing women as the key players in designing, operating and maintaining the water systems especially in community levels (in non-technical or non-engineering sector) will not only change the culture within the sector but also ensure that the system is designed to meet the needs of those who use it.

With built-in, women (and girls) who are often the caretakers and guardians of their families, have a better understanding of the water and sanitation needs of their families and operate for sustainable water management at the community level. In many cases, externally financed water supply and treatment systems fail to meet the needs of the communities because they do not address the failure of institutionalization of a community-based sustainable water management governance on the ground. It is therefore important that women are involved in all aspects of water projects which not only boosts women’s self-esteem but also economic empowerment by providing technical guidance and financial support which will overall contribute to women's empowerment.

Contract women as national and provincial level staff in the field of water and sanitation management, including training them on skills related to their work. For upgrading of skills through training and opportunity to both males and females in management of water and sanitation projects.

Gender Action 2: Engage women, especially the vulnerable and low-income, for monitoring and Surveillance of drinking water sources and treatment facilities

Water-borne illnesses such as diarrhea, cholera and typhoid are common in developing countries. As women continue to bear the primary responsibility for the food, care, and health of their families, proper water treatment through monitoring and surveillance of drinking water sources by women would improve the quality of water for consumption, improved access, and economic and environmental

benefits as well. Involving women in water quality management and monitoring such as water quality testing will contribute to improving the quality of water.

Unlike external companies that conduct regular or quarterly tests, regular/frequent/permanent physical presence of the women residents at the community water facilities would ensure constant monitoring of the performance of the water facilities while namely addressing any malfunctions.


Mobility and institutionalize women’s groups (for female students) and provide training and education to empower them with knowledge and skills in water management and managerial skills for their active water management and monitoring.

Gender Action 3: Improve safe and convenient access to water and sanitation facilities for the vulnerable

In many cases in developing countries, location of the water supply facilities does not ensure equal access to all community members, especially socio-economically vulnerable and politically marginalized groups. In such situations, if the water supply facilities are located in areas where the water facilities may be subject to illegal appropriation by the elite groups, excluding the vulnerable.

- When designing and locating the water and sanitation facilities, give special consideration to the vulnerable group, particularly women and girls who are at higher risk of gender-based violence. Ensure that these facilities are safe, secure, and easily accessible for all community members.


Consider offering collective asset ownership of water utilities and facilities at community-level to avoid risks of corruption and elite capture. Locate water and sanitation facilities close to settings (e.g., through social mapping), in a secure environment and along safe access paths.

Appendix 5.A.4 Waste Management

- Waste Management Sector in the Five Countries

[Cambodia]

Waste Management Technologies are recognized as priority climate mitigation and adaptation technologies in Cambodia. (See Table A.1.D and Table A.1.E of the Demand-Driven Pre-Feasibility Study (ADB, 2016)).

The water sector is responsible for approximately 2.1% of GHG emissions in Cambodia, as of 2016. Solid waste and wastewater management is an ongoing challenge in the country with increasing levels of waste generation due to rapid urbanization and industrialization. The types of solid waste are considerable such as plastics, metals, and electronic waste. There is a need for additional waste segregation, treatment, and disposal facilities. The growing population and economic development combined with a growing middle class, has had a substantial impact on waste generation in Cambodia’s major cities and urban areas. This has led to the accumulation of solid waste in many parts of the country. Currently, households, local companies and factories frequently dispose of their waste by burning it or dumping it in public places, streets, and deserted locations, posing public health threats and soil contamination.

While there is a general lack of data on the actual amount of recycled waste in Cambodia, recycling activities of limited scale are observed in Phnom Penh. Several private companies are exploring the


---
**Page 213**
---

Guize Consult Inc.

# business potential targeting both organic and inorganic waste. Both men and women have a low level of awareness about the waste sector.[1]

## [Indonesia]

Every year, Indonesia generates 65.8 million tons of solid waste from households and industries. The rate of trash production is expected to rise as the population expands, reaching 70.8 tons per capita by 2025.

According to the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (2017), only 2.2% of the waste generated is being recycled or processed into other resources, while 66.39% of trash produced in Indonesia is landfilled and 19.85% is incinerated. The flow of recyclable materials in Indonesia is heavily dependent on the informal sector, which accounts for about 70-80% of the total waste collected.

Among the various waste management technologies related to climate change mitigation, mechanical and biological waste treatment (MBT) and in-vessel composting (IVC) technologies rank the highest. The best practices include anaerobic digestion (AD), such as upflow anaerobic sludge blanket (UASB) and high solids anaerobic digestion. (See Table I.B. 41 of the Demand-Driven Pre-Feasibility Study (Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal) of the RD & B Programme).

## [Lao PDR]

To achieve the national GHG emission reduction target, the Lao government aims to implement 500-ton-per-day sustainable municipal solid waste management project (Lao PDR NDC 2020).

There is a absence of waste treatment systems that meet the required environmental standards in many of the factories that are located in larger cities. In general, suitable technologies and sustainable waste management practices have not been widely adopted by these factories. The existing waste management facilities are often lacking predictability and gate fees are rather low than the operations are not commercially viable. In addition, there is a lack of awareness among the public about the importance of sustainable development on benefits, including environmental, climate, public health and sustainable cities benefits.

Priority technologies in waste treatment areas are: mechanical and biological waste treatment (MBT), Biogas and biotreatment technology, Gasification treatment. Methane recovery from sanitation landfilling is also a priority area for Lao PDR. The country has no experience with the anaerobic landfilling system.

## [Philippines]

Waste is one of the three climate change mitigation sectors identified in the Technology Needs Assessment (TNA) on Climate Change Mitigation (CC, 2018), together with energy and transportation. In terms of waste heat recovery (WHR), power generation from landfill methane recovery is included as technology option in the country's NDC.

# Gender Perspective in the Waste Management Sector

In the wider informal waste management sector, women are primarily found in the lower tier, working in waste picking and separating at landfill sites. Traditionally, men dominate the higher-income and decision-making roles in this sector, which includes sorting recyclables for sale to processors and reselling recyclables. This, again, reflects the gendered division of labour in society but also means that women are often excluded when waste management activities are formalized, missing out on protections and benefits, such as social security or higher wages.

## [Cambodia]

In Cambodia, informal female workers as garbage collectors and dismantlers in urban areas, such as Phnom Penh, are the key actors in waste recycling in a sector largely lacking central organization. While this is an important role for women, it also comes with significant risks due to the lack of segregation of toxic and hazardous materials, and unregulated hygienic recycling practices pose serious threats to occupational safety and health.

## [Indonesia]

In Indonesia, in the informal waste collection, segregation and recycling sector, women's roles are limited. Informal garbage collection work is often dangerous and invites gender-based violence and criminally exploitative working conditions. Women tend to be concentrated in small-scale, localized scavenging activities of lower-value recyclables, leaving heavier and more valuable materials to their male counterparts, resulting in low remuneration than males. At the household level, women are responsible for waste collection, sorting, and segregation of residual waste and recycling materials and they are responsible for the household's trash management. However, women's roles in waste management are often overlooked or undervalued, which can reduce the amount of food waste and improve the overall community waste management. However, waste management is still largely dominated by men, with government authorities' stereotypes on women and major decision-making and managerial roles are given to men.[16]

# Waste Management and Women

Introducing waste management technologies could also be considered a priority sector for women in the Philippines. Lack of waste management and segregation of industrial, toxic, and hazardous materials endangers human health and the environment. Women are often responsible for household waste disposal, exposing them to major public health risks and pollution. Women are found to be key agents in the household's waste management while underrepresented in the category of recycling collectors. The proportion of women in this sector is only 15% compared with 80% men. This gender gap exists both inside and outside the household. Education and technical capacity building activities on "how to recycle" targeting women should also be implemented aside from clean waste technologies and infrastructure.

## Vietnam

Waste management could also be considered a priority sector for Vietnamese women. Women represent 35-40% of formal waste collectors while they dominate the informal sector with a 65% participation rate. They are often responsible for household waste disposal, exposing them to major public health risks and pollution. Women play a significant role in the informal waste economy, being dealers and owners of recycling activities, but also in the formal waste economy, such as truck drivers and managers.

Women usually are the decision-makers concerning domestic food purchase, and thus have a great influence on household waste generation. However, women face many challenges due to their weak participation in community decision-making about waste disposal; men are more likely to take part in the community consultation process for waste management projects (UN Women, 2015).

Investment in recycling technologies and infrastructure in Viet Nam could contribute to the female labor migration from the informal to formal sector, improving their income generation and work conditions. The majority of women working as garbage collectors or recyclers have been engaging in the previously informal waste industry by providing them with required skills for a clean and modernized system.

### Recommended Gender-mainstreaming approaches

In Cambodia, Laos PDR and the Philippines, women constitute significant parts of the information and highly decentralized collection, occupation of garbage collection, waste pickers down to door-level workers. While they are likely to be employed in formal waste management by the use of eco-friendly solutions (90%), women are often engaged as informal collectors (17%) within the category of formal employment. Also challenged by the requirements of domestic work, the women in Cambodia and Laos PDR are more likely to be involved in the informal sector than in the formal one.

Women's participation is also low in the Philippines where they represent only 15% of the total number of waste collectors (80%). The strong opening up of dump sites has been quite common (64%) and representation of the women in this sector is only 15% compared with 80% men. This gender gap exists both inside and outside the household.

# Example of Best Practice for Gender Mainstreaming Assistance Program in the Waste Management Sector

## Project Title
Supporting The Socio-Economic Inclusiveness of Women and Youth by Waste Management

### Project Management Country
United Nations Development Organization (UNIDO)

### Project Period
Year 2014 - 2015  
Phase I: 2013 – 2016  
Phase II: 2017 – 2018  

### Main Project Activities
- Supported the socio-economic inclusiveness of women and youth through waste management activities in two pilot locations in Conakry and three regional localities.
- Implemented a community-based solid waste recycling center on plastics and organic waste.
- Conducted capacity dissemination and awareness raising on solid waste management.

#### Outputs
* Phase I:  
  - The capacity building on solid waste collection and recycling of more than 3,200 youth and women organized in 84 associations.  
  - The establishment of community-based solid waste recycling centers in Conakry.
* Phase II:  
  - The capacity of more than 5,600 women and youth for onsite collection and recycling to enable more than 500 women and youth to generate revenues through economic initiatives related to waste management.  

### General Requirements

# Sector-Specific Gender Mainstreaming Considerations

## Women and men play different roles in waste management and recycling, often living and working under dangerous and unhealthy conditions.

### In this sector, women have higher paid professions in the informal waste economy, being dealers and owners of recycling activities, but also in the formal waste economy as workers in waste treatment plants.
- **In terms of the providers of services**, employment opportunities tend to be different for women and men, with women occupying lower-grade positions than men.

### As the waste sector modernizes and applies new technologies that may require greater education and training, women are often excluded from these processes. It can lead to the dissolution of the informal sector related to the employed workforce, which can lead to job loss, accelerated poverty, and direct displacement of women from their livelihoods in the waste economy.
- **Women waste pickers/garbage collectors need to be recognized as legitimate and valuable partners for the improvement of the overall urban waste management sector** by following recommended actions:

### Gender Action 1: (Directly/Indirectly contract/volunteer the female waste pickers groups as delivery partners, provide formal and secure employment contracts with standard HSE and legal labor contracts) to them to secure them with decent jobs and safe and standard work conditions.
- **Gender Action 2:** (Related to Gender Action 1) Capitalize on their organization and modernize their practices by establishing property registered enterprise/profit entities etc. through which they can be contracted for waste management services.
### Gender Action 3: When IV enterprises involve construction and operation of a sizable waste treatment or Waste to Energy facility development, ensure the contractor includes local employment opportunities for women in the project (e.g., as drivers, guards, security, and waste experiences including informal waste collection works). If possible, consider setting a certain target (60%) of the non- and semi-skilled jobs as well as working level management positions for women. Also, provide training to women on skills required for waste treatment works (waste segregation centre work, etc.) for their smooth transition to formal waste management sector.

# EWS & Disaster Management Sector in the Five Countries

## Early Warning System (EWSs) and Disaster Management

### Disaster management entails both structural (such as dykes, embankment and ground elevation for sea level rise & flood prevention) and non-structural (such as climate change and hydrometeorological monitoring and forecasting, early warning planning, mangrove and break-water tree planting, and urban planning.).

As part of a non-structural solution for climate-change-related natural disaster management technologies, early Warning Systems (EWSs) are designed to provide information on the occurrence of hazards, including strategies and objectives. The aim of an EWS is to avoid or reduce damages inflicted by natural disasters. As such, EWSs can be defined as a set of systems that collect data from various sources, process them, and are in a permanent state of readiness and to enable early action when disaster strikes. In this sense, an EWS can be considered as a system for the prevention of disasters.

### Early warning systems, which use integrated communication networks to assist communities in getting ready for dangerous climate-related occurrences, are an adaptive response to climate change. A successful EWS fosters long-term sustainability by preserving people, employment, land, and infrastructure."

Following are the key priority climate technology areas in each of the countries, assessed by the KDB and GGGI for the R4RD Program (Annex 2 of the Funding Proposal of the Programme):

#### Cambodia

Cambodia includes a "National end-to-end early warning system with a focus on effective dissemination to population at risk" as its National Adaptation Action within its NDC**.

#### Indonesia

Indonesia's public health is significantly affected by climate change-related extreme weather and natural disasters. In this context, the following two human health sector technology areas were identified to have the highest relevance considering various factors (costs, environmental benefits, economic impacts, etc.):

1. 24-hour weather forecasting systems and early warning systems to prevent outbreaks (daily reporting system), and
2. 24-hour bio-metric monitoring of climate change disease vectors (e.g., mosquitoes) and upgrading the technologies on upgrading the hydro-meteorological information & information systems/for management of climate disease (dengue, malaria and pneumococci) to early warning to local action systems.

#### Laos PDR

Being a predominantly agrarian country, Lao PDR's EWS is related to the overall climate resilience building and adaptation strategies of the rural communities and agricultural sector. Early warning system and insurance in livestock industry is one example, as such. The communities would need to be

# Gender Perspective in EWS and Disaster Management

In general, girls and women worldwide are reported to be more vulnerable to the damages of climate change-related disasters than boys and men. This is especially true for countries such as Timor-Leste, flood and severe droughts. Higher casualties and incident rates of the girls and women are partly explained by their physical frailties (compared with boys and men), partly biological attributes but also due to girls' vulnerability in terms of access to information, decision-making power, and critical information.

Women typically do not receive disaster notifications since they are the only ones who inform them of potential disasters. In many cases, due to societal norms and barriers, women were still hesitant to take part in the planning for disaster response and capacity building. "Warning information dissemination impacts the gender gap in terms of access to information, decision-making power, and critical thinking levels, and digital literacy," in terms of recognizing, understanding, and acting. People of different genders face different challenges when it comes to forms of artificial channels of information transmission, differing communication preferences influenced by gender norms, and unique difficulties in accessing and acting on an early warning.

## Recommended Gender-mainstreaming approaches

### <1> General Requirements

All the requirements under the CIF Investment Criteria: Gender Component (See Table 1 of the Gender Assessment) must be met for all proposed IV enterprises/projects subject to the CIF approval. Thus, "women-led enterprises" on the EWS and Natural Resource Management (NRM) components of the CIF should be encouraged to hire more female employees (with equal working conditions and remuneration as male employees) in an important step towards gender mainstreaming.

All the CIF IVs shall need to submit a simplified gender assessment and gender action plan (Appendix 3, Simplified Gender Analysis (GA) & Gender Action Plan (GAP)) (General Template[] of the Gender Assessment) for each proposed IV enterprise/project (hereinafter referred to as "the Programme"). (Section II-2. Gender Analysis) requires you to assess female perspective of a specific proposed IV enterprises/projects, based on which (Section II-3. Enterprise-level Gender Action Plan) need to be established.

### <2> Sector-specific Gender Mainstreaming Considerations


---
**Page 222**
---

Gender Action Item 1: Diversify the communication modalities to ensure inclusiveness and accessibility for various groups including minority segments of the population.

Setting up an App or an Internet platform based EWS is important but may become urban/rich/income/educated/male biased. Effective EWS and disaster management practices requires going beyond traditional media such as radio, television, and print media. Linking the system to the locally actionable mechanism remains a challenge in Indonesia and other countries. The public’s understanding and awareness on the EWS and its technologies and applications remain low.

To reach out to the entire population of the country that are subject to such disasters and risks often requires special consideration of identifying and deploying viable communication methods and modalities for different groups, including those who live in remote areas, those living in urban slums, or those in remotes, as in the case of remote or isolated (as in the case of islands), or due to physical disabilities, such as blind, deaf, mute, etc. This is especially true when considering the low levels of mobile phone penetrations. In urgent disaster outbreak situations, targeted language (local dialects for ethnic minorities) and gender communication strategies are required. For example, women can play a role in a physical disaster communication network set in the community level for those not connected through Internet, or without mobile phone networks, etc.

As there is a difference in educational attainment across gender, mostly women have lower digital literacy levels. Women also tend to be more active at the household level; traditional and offline communication methods must be used in parallel manner[10]. In addition, considering the literacy levels of the population, audio-based messages are more effective than visual-based messages. Audio messages are more effective in delivering urgent information, for instance, women can take the role of providing voice recordings to community radio stations.

Formulate a communications plan, develop knowledge products and information, education and communication (IEC) materials, in easy-to-understand, gender-neutral language[10]. Ensure that the IEC materials are accessible to people with different levels of literacy. Use different communication modes for the blind, those with hearing impairments, illiterate or low-educated people. Ensure both digital/analog methods of communications.

Gender Action Item 2: Strengthen community-based climate disaster preparation and management System with equal/weighted roles of the women’s groups.

Community-driven climate risk resilience projects are crucial for disaster preparedness and responses. As in the case of Thai Than Hui province, Vietnam, there shows about the Community’s self-mobilization and its financial resources to build their own community-based disaster risk reduction (CBDRR) system through behavior adaptation (BA), which is appropriate for bottom-up approaches. Local communities have ample knowledge and experience on how to manage risks in their environment. They are also more likely to use community-based resources. Community-driven bottom-up action, once carefully and appropriately supported, will identify the optimal solution to the problem.

In case of a project, it should be designed with the local communities as beneficiaries and/or delivery agents; understand the community governance dynamic and e.g., role of community girls and women with their potential on active agents. Assess their needs and willingness to participate (as well as their concerns associated). Assess the gaps in their

- If necessary, assist mobilization and organization of girls' and women's groups. Structural engagement mechanisms (e.g., through community-based organizations) can be used to effectively prepare and execute. This would invite women as an equal partner for climate actions by empowering them. Gradually, this will shift the cultural perception that women are passive recipients of development initiatives and instead become active agents of socio-cultural changes in the country[2].

Gender Action 3: Strengthen the consultation of girls and women throughout the project cycle and empower them.

- Early warning systems and climate monitoring businesses can be made diverse forms of activities. Maintain a gender-sensitive perspective at all stages of the execution of the JV enterprises/business, from the planning to the operation stage to the extent possible.
